Extended Weil representations: the real field case
Chun-Hui Wang

Abstract
. Let F be the usual real field. Let W be a symplectic vector space over F. It is known that there are two different Weil representations of a Meteplectic covering group Sp ~ ​ ( W ) \widetilde{\operatorname{Sp}}(W) . By some twisted actions, we reorganize them into a representation of Sp ~ ± ​ ( W ) \widetilde{\operatorname{Sp}}^{\pm}(W) , a covering group over a subgroup Sp ± ⁡ ( W ) \operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}(W) of GSp ⁡ ( W ) \operatorname{GSp}(W) . Based on the works of MVW, Kudla, and Howe on reductive dual pairs in Sp ⁡ ( W ) \operatorname{Sp}(W) , we explore the analogous dual pairs in Sp ± ⁡ ( W ) \operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}(W) . Finally, following Lion-Vergne’s classical book on Weil representations and theta series, we investigate some simple theta series in Sp ± ⁡ ( W ) \operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}(W) where W W has dimension two.



1.
Introduction

Let
F
be a local field. Let
(W,\langle,\rangle)
be a symplectic vector space of dimension
2m
over
F
. Let
\operatorname{H}(W)=W\oplus F
denote the usual Heisenberg group. Let
\operatorname{Sp}(W)
(resp.
\operatorname{GSp}(W)
) denote the corresponding symplectic group(resp. similitude symplectic group). Let
\psi
be a non-trivial unitary character of
F
. Let
\widetilde{\operatorname{Sp}}(W)
denote an
8
-fold metaplectic covering group over
\operatorname{Sp}(W)
. Let
\pi_{\psi}
be a Weil representation of
\widetilde{\operatorname{Sp}}(W)\ltimes\operatorname{H}(W)
associated to
\psi
, and let
\omega_{\psi}=\pi_{\psi}|_{\widetilde{\operatorname{Sp}}(W)}
. One question is how to extend the Weil representation to the similitude group. This question has been studied systematically by Barthel in
[
Ba
]
in the
p
-adic field case. In that paper, Barthel constructed the Metaplectic 2-cocycle over

\operatorname{GSp}(W)
, defined the corresponding Metaplectic group
\operatorname{GMp}(W)
, and then obtained the extended Weil representation by induction. One can also see another two important papers: B.Robets
[
Ro1
]
and Gan-Tantono
[
GaTa
]
, which contain some similar results on the theta correspondences for the similitude groups. Consequently, C.Zhang investigated unitary similitude dual pairs in
[
Zh
]
. In the archimedean field case, one can see M.Cognet’s two papers
[
Co1
,
Co2
]
on
\operatorname{GSp}_{8}(F)
.
In the global field case, many people contribute to this subject. For examples, one can see Harris-Kudla-Sweet’s paper
[
HaKuSw
]
, S.Gelbart’s book
[
Ge
]
, B. Roberts’paper
[
Ro2
]
, etc.
We mostly approach such question mainly by following
[
Ba
]
,
[
GePi
]
,
[
Wal1
]
,
[
Wal2
]
,
[
LiVe
]
. Through some twisted actions, we continue their works by constricting the Weil representation. In this study, we continue our work into the real field case, moving from the p-adic field case(cf.
[
Wan
]
).

1.1.

Let
F
be a real field. Let
a\in F^{\times}
, and let
\psi^{a}
denote another character of
F
defined as
t\longmapsto\psi(at)
, for
t\in F
. Let
\omega_{\psi^{a}}
be the Weil representation of
\widetilde{\operatorname{Sp}}(W)
associated to
\psi^{a}
. It is known that
\omega_{\psi^{at^{2}}}\simeq\omega_{\psi^{a}}
, for any
t\in F^{\times}
. Loosely speaking, there exist two different kind of Weil representations associated to
\psi
and
\psi^{-1}
. One of our purpose is to reorganizer them as a representation of a bigger group than
\widetilde{\operatorname{Sp}}(W)
. Let
F^{\times}_{+}
be the multiplicative group of positive real numbers. Let
\operatorname{PGSp}^{\pm}(W)=\operatorname{GSp}(W)/F_{+}^{\times}
, which is isomorphic with the subgroup
\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}(W)
of
\operatorname{GSp}(W)
. Then there exists the following exact sequence:

\displaystyle 1\longrightarrow\operatorname{Sp}(W)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}(W)\longrightarrow F^{\times}/F^{\times 2}\longrightarrow 1.

Let
\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(-,-)
denote the Perrin-Rao cocycle on
\operatorname{Sp}(W)
. Based on Barthel’s paper
[
Ba
]
in the
p
-adic case, we can extend this cocycle from
\operatorname{Sp}(W)
to
\operatorname{GSp}(W)
and
\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}(W)
in Section
3
. Let
\widetilde{\operatorname{Sp}}^{\pm}(W)
denote the corresponding covering group. Then there exists an exact sequence:

\displaystyle 1\longrightarrow\mu_{8}\longrightarrow\widetilde{\operatorname{Sp}}^{\pm}(W)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}(W)\longrightarrow 1,

and
\widetilde{\operatorname{Sp}}^{\pm}(W)\simeq F_{2}\ltimes\widetilde{\operatorname{Sp}}(W)
, for
F_{2}=\{\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&\pm 1\end{pmatrix}\}
. As a consequence, we can define

\Pi_{\psi}=\operatorname{Ind}_{\widetilde{\operatorname{Sp}}(W)\ltimes\operatorname{H}(W)}^{\widetilde{\operatorname{Sp}}^{\pm}(W)\ltimes\operatorname{H}(W)}\pi_{\psi},\qquad\Omega_{\psi}=\operatorname{Res}_{\widetilde{\operatorname{Sp}}^{\pm}(W)}^{\widetilde{\operatorname{Sp}}^{\pm}(W)\ltimes\operatorname{H}(W)}\Pi_{\psi}.

The restriction of
\Omega_{\psi}
on
\widetilde{\operatorname{Sp}}(W)
consists of the exact two different Weil representations. We also consider the
2
-fold Metaplectic covering group by following P.Perrin, R.Ranga.Rao and S.Kudla’s works(cf.
[
Pe
]
,
[
Rao
]
,
[
Ku2
]
).

1.2.

In the second part(cf. Section
5.11
), we consider the dual pair in
\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}(W)
. Let us present two known results on irreducible reductive dual pairs in
\operatorname{Sp}(W)
. Retain notations from Section
5.11
.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
(\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{1}),\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{2}))
be an irreducible reductive dual pair in
\operatorname{Sp}(W)
.

(1)

If
(\mathcal{W}_{1},\mathcal{W}_{2})
is not the exception case (1*), then the exact sequence
1\longrightarrow T\longrightarrow\widehat{\operatorname{U}}(\mathcal{W}_{i})\longrightarrow\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{i})\longrightarrow 1
splits, for
i=1,2
.

(2)

If
(\mathcal{W}_{1},\mathcal{W}_{2})
is the exception case and
\epsilon_{1}=-1
,
\epsilon_{2}=1
, then

–

the exact sequence
1\longrightarrow T\longrightarrow\widehat{\operatorname{O}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})\longrightarrow\operatorname{O}(\mathcal{W}_{2})\longrightarrow 1
splits, but

–

the exact sequence
1\longrightarrow T\longrightarrow\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})\longrightarrow\operatorname{Sp}(\mathcal{W}_{1})\longrightarrow 1
does not split.

The
p
-adic version of this theorem has been proved in
[
MVW
, Chapter 1 ]
and
[
Ku1
]
. For the real field case, it was proved in
[
Ku1
, Prop.4.1]
. Moreover, explicit trivialization is also given in
[
Ku1
]
. Indeed, there is an inconspicuous question on which 2-degree covering group or
8
-degree covering group is also splitting.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
(\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{1}),\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{2}))
be an irreducible reductive dual pair in
\operatorname{Sp}(W)
as given above. Then
\widehat{\operatorname{U}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
commutates with
\widehat{\operatorname{U}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
in
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}}(W)
.

The
p
-adic version of this theorem has been proved in
[
MVW
, Chapter 1 ]
. For the real field case, one can see
[
Ho1
,
Ho2
]
. We generalize the above two results to the group
\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}(W)
.

Proposition 1.3
.

Let
(\mathcal{W}_{1},\mathcal{W}_{2})
be a pair in Theorem
1.1
.

(1)

If
(\mathcal{W}_{1},\mathcal{W}_{2})
is not the exception case (1*), then the exact sequence
1\longrightarrow T\longrightarrow\widehat{\operatorname{U}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{i})\longrightarrow\operatorname{U}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{i})\longrightarrow 1
splits, for
i=1,2
.

(2)

If
(\mathcal{W}_{1},\mathcal{W}_{2})
is the exception case and
\epsilon_{1}=-1
,
\epsilon_{2}=1
, then

(i)

the exact sequence
1\longrightarrow T\longrightarrow\widehat{\operatorname{O}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})\longrightarrow\operatorname{O}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{2})\longrightarrow 1
splits, but

(ii)

the exact sequence
1\longrightarrow T\longrightarrow\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})\longrightarrow\operatorname{Sp}(\mathcal{W}_{1})\longrightarrow 1
does not split.

Proposition 1.4
.

Let
(\mathcal{W}_{1},\mathcal{W}_{2})
be a pair in Theorem
1.1
.

(1)

If
D=E
or
\mathbb{H}
,
\widehat{\operatorname{U}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
commutes with
\widehat{\operatorname{U}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
in
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}}^{\pm}(W)
.

(2)

If
D=F
, assume
\epsilon_{1}=-1
,
\epsilon_{2}=1
.

(a)

If
2\mid\dim\mathcal{W}_{2}
, and
\mathcal{W}_{2}
is not a hyperbolic space, then:

(i)

\operatorname{O}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{2})=\operatorname{O}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
.

(ii)

if
4\mid\dim\mathcal{W}_{1}
, then
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
commutes with
\operatorname{O}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
in
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}}^{\pm}(W)
.

(iii)

if
4\nmid\dim\mathcal{W}_{1}
, then
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
only commutes with
\operatorname{SO}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
in
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}}^{\pm}(W)
, and
\operatorname{O}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
only commutes with
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
in
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}}^{\pm}(W)
.

(b)

If
2\mid\dim\mathcal{W}_{2}
, and
\mathcal{W}_{2}
is a hyperbolic space, then:

(i)

\widehat{\operatorname{O}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})\simeq\operatorname{O}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{2})\times T
.

(ii)

if
4\mid\dim\mathcal{W}_{1}
, then
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
commutes with
\widehat{\operatorname{O}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
in
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}}^{\pm}(W)
.

(iii)

if
4\nmid\dim\mathcal{W}_{1}
and
4\mid\dim\mathcal{W}_{2}
, then
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
only commutes with
\widehat{\operatorname{SO}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
in
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}}^{\pm}(W)
, and
\widehat{\operatorname{O}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
only commutes with
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
in
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}}^{\pm}(W)
.

(iv)

if
4\nmid\dim\mathcal{W}_{1}
and
4\nmid\dim\mathcal{W}_{2}
, then
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
only commutes with
\widehat{\operatorname{SO}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
in
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}}^{\pm}(W)
, and
\widehat{\operatorname{O}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
only commutes with
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
in
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}}^{\pm}(W)
.

(c)

If
2\nmid\dim\mathcal{W}_{2}
, then:

(i)

\operatorname{O}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{2})=\operatorname{O}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
.

(ii)

if
4\mid\dim\mathcal{W}_{1}
, then
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
commutes with
\operatorname{O}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
in
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}}^{\pm}(W)
.

(iii)

if
4\nmid\dim\mathcal{W}_{1}
, then
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
only commutes with
\operatorname{SO}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
, and
\operatorname{O}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
only commutes with
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
in
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}}^{\pm}(W)
.

It seems that Prop.
1.4
(2)(c) supports J.-S.Li’s works
[
Li1
]
,
[
Li2
]
and Jiang-Soudry ’s work
[
JiSo
]
on the theta correspondence between
\operatorname{Mp}_{2n}
and
\operatorname{SO}_{2r+1}
. For the finer results on Shimura-Waldspurger correspondences, one can see Gan-Ichino, Gan-Li’s recent papers
[
GaIc
]
,
[
GaLi
]
.

1.3.

In the third part(cf. Section
6
), we consider the example when
\dim W=2
and some theta series associated to the group
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})
. We mainly follow the classical book
[
LiVe
]
to deal with theta series. In an alternative way by using the global method, one can see
[
We
]
and
[
Ge
]
. Retain notations from Section
6.1
. Let
\Gamma=\breve{\Gamma}(2)^{\pm}=\langle\omega\rangle\ltimes\Gamma(2)^{\pm}
be
a discrete subgroup of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})
. Let
\gamma=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\Gamma
. Let us define
\operatorname{sgn}(z)=1
or
-1
according to
z\in\mathcal{H}
or
z\in\mathcal{H}^{-}
. Let us define

\displaystyle\theta_{1/2}(z,\epsilon)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{i\epsilon\pi n^{2}z},

\displaystyle\theta_{3/2}(z,\epsilon)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}ne^{i\epsilon\pi n^{2}z},

for
z\in\mathcal{H}^{\pm}
,
\epsilon\in\mu_{2}
. Then:

\displaystyle\theta_{1/2}(\tfrac{az+b}{cz+d},\epsilon)=\lambda^{\pm}(\gamma,\epsilon)\sqrt{\det\gamma(cz+d)}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{i(\det\gamma)\epsilon\pi n^{2}z},

(1.1)

\displaystyle\theta_{3/2}(\tfrac{az+b}{cz+d},\epsilon)=\lambda^{\pm}(\gamma,\epsilon)(\sqrt{\det\gamma(cz+d)})^{3}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}ne^{i(\det\gamma)\epsilon\pi n^{2}z},

(1.2)

for
z\in\mathcal{H}^{\pm}
,
\gamma\in\Gamma
if
\epsilon=1
or
\gamma\in\Gamma\setminus\{h(-1),h(-1)h_{-1}\}
if
\epsilon=-1
, and some constant
\lambda^{\pm}(\gamma,\epsilon)
. The main difference between theta series associated to
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})
and
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
is the following: these Metaplectic coverings over
\breve{\Gamma}(2)^{\pm}
and
\operatorname{SO}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})
are not splitting.(cf. Lemmas
6.8
,
6.9
) The above two theta series only generalize the theorem in
[
LiVe
, p.204, 2.4.13]
. In
[
LiVe
, p.205, Section 2.4.16]
, the two authors also deal with much complicated theta series and connect them with Serre and Stark’s important result in
[
SeSt
]
. We expect to work out some similar results in a separate paper in future. Finally let us propose a question to think:

Question 1.5
.

If we replace the above discrete group
\Gamma
by
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
or
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{Z})
, how to obtain modular forms or theta series from Weil representations in a natural way?

1.4.

In the fourth part(cf. Section
7
), we study the works
[
LiVe
]
,
[
Pe
]
, and
[
Rao
]
in a simple case—
\dim W=2
. Let
\widehat{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbb{Z})
denote the central extension group of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
by
T
. By
[
As
]
,
[
KiMe
]
, etc., it is known that
\widehat{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbb{Z})
can be split by some trivialization map from Dedekind sums and modular forms of weight
12
. On the other hand, in
[
LiVe
]
, the authors also gave another trivialization map for
\Gamma(2)
. In that section, we will examine their results in an elementary way, and then formulate the explicit character between these two trivialization maps for
\Gamma(2)
. Finally, by comparing two different models of Weil representations, we will demonstrate the splitting for
\widehat{\Gamma(2)}
in a different way. This method has already been used in
[
LiVe
]
only
not pointing out.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Notations and conventions

In the whole text, we will use the following notion and conventions. Let
\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbb{C}
denote the usual real numbers and complex numbers. Let
F=\mathbb{R}
. Let
(W,\langle,\rangle)
be a symplectic vector space of dimension
2m
over
F
. Let
\operatorname{H}(W)=W\oplus F
denote the usual Heisenbeg group, with the multiplication law given by

(w,t)(w^{\prime},t^{\prime})=(w+w^{\prime},t+t^{\prime}+\tfrac{\langle w,w^{\prime}\rangle}{2}),\quad\quad w,w^{\prime}\in W,t,t^{\prime}\in F.

Let
\operatorname{Sp}(W)
(resp.
\operatorname{GSp}(W)
) denote the corresponding symplectic group(resp.similitude symplectic group). Let
\lambda
be the similitude factor map from
\operatorname{GSp}(W)
to
F^{\times}
, and for
g\in\operatorname{GSp}(W)
, let
\lambda_{g}
denote its similitude factor. Let
\{e_{1},\cdots,e_{m};e_{1}^{\ast},\cdots,e_{m}^{\ast}\}
be a symplectic basis of
W
so that
\langle e_{i},e_{j}\rangle=0=\langle e_{i}^{\ast},e_{j}^{\ast}\rangle
,
\langle e_{i},e_{j}^{\ast}\rangle=\delta_{ij}
. Let
X=\operatorname{Span}\{e_{1},\cdots,e_{m}\}
,
X^{\ast}=\operatorname{Span}\{e_{1}^{\ast},\cdots,e_{m}^{\ast}\}
.

In
\operatorname{Sp}(W)
, let
P=P(X^{\ast})=\{g\in\operatorname{Sp}(W)\mid X^{\ast}g=X^{\ast}\}=\{\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
0&(a^{\ast})^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{Sp}(W)\}
,
N=\{\begin{pmatrix}1&b\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{Sp}(W)\}
,
M=\{\begin{pmatrix}a&0\\
0&(a^{\ast})^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{Sp}(W)\}
,
N^{-}=\{\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
c&1\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{Sp}(W)\}
. For a subset
S\subseteq\{1,\cdots,m\}
, let us define an element
\omega_{S}
of
\operatorname{Sp}(W)
as follows:
(e_{i})\omega_{S}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}-e_{i}^{\ast}&i\in S\\
e_{i}&i\notin S\end{array}\right.
and
(e_{i}^{\ast})\omega_{S}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}e_{i}^{\ast}&i\notin S\\
e_{i}&i\in S\end{array}\right.
. In particular, we will write simply
\omega
, for
S=\{1,\cdots,m\}
.

Let
(-,-)_{F}
denote the Hilbert symbol defined from
F^{\times}\times F^{\times}
to
\{\pm 1\}
. More precisely,

(a,b)_{F}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}-1&a<0,b<0\\
1&\textrm{ otherwise}\end{array}\right..

Let
((a))
to denote the number
0
or
a-[a]-\tfrac{1}{2}
, depending on whether
a
is an integer or not. For two coprime integers
c,d
with
c>0
, let
s(d,c)
denote the Dedekind sum, given by
\sum_{k=1}^{c}((\tfrac{k}{c}))((\tfrac{kd}{c}))
.

If
V
is a vector space over
F
of dimension
l
, and
Q
is a non-degenerate quadratic form on
V
, defined as

Q(x)=\sum_{i,j}b_{ij}x_{i}x_{j}
,
for
b_{ij}=b_{ji}
, then the determinant
\det Q
of
Q
is defined by
\det Q=\det(b_{ij})
. If
Q\sim a_{1}x_{1}^{2}+\cdots+a_{l}x_{l}^{2}
, the Hasse invariant
\epsilon(Q)
of
Q
is defined by
\epsilon(Q)=\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq l}(a_{i},a_{j})_{F}
.

If
\psi
is a non-trivial character of
F
and
a\in F
, we will write
\psi^{a}
for the character:
t\longrightarrow\psi(at)
. Let
i=\sqrt{-1}
. We will let
\psi_{0}
denote the fixed character of
F
defined as:
t\longmapsto e^{2\pi it}
, for
t\in F
. Let
\mu_{n}=\langle e^{\tfrac{2\pi i}{n}}\rangle
,
e^{\tfrac{2\pi i}{n}}\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
. Let
T=\{e^{i\theta}\mid\theta\in\mathbb{R}\}
.

For an
F
-vector space
V
, we will let
L^{p}(V)
(resp.
S(V)
) denote the
L^{p}
-vector space(resp. Schwartz space) over
V
.

2.2.
Weil representation

Note that the center of
\operatorname{H}(W)
is just
F
. Let
\psi
be a non-trivial continuous unitary character of
F
. According to the Stone-von Neumann’s theorem, only one unitary irreducible complex representation of
\operatorname{H}(W)
with central character
\psi
, up to unitary equivalence, exists. Let us call it the Heisenberg representation, and denote it by
\pi_{\psi}
. The symplectic group
\operatorname{Sp}(W)
can then act on
\operatorname{H}(W)
and leave the center
F
pointwise unchanged. By Weil’s famous work,
\pi_{\psi}
can give rise to a projective representation of
\operatorname{Sp}(W)
and then to an actual representation of a
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-covering group over
\operatorname{Sp}(W)
. As is customary, we refer to such group as a Metaplectic group and the actual representation as a Weil representation. As
F=\mathbb{R}
, this special central cover can descend to an
8
-degree or a
2
-degree cover over
\operatorname{Sp}(W)
. Let us recall some explicit
2
-cocycles associated with Metaplectic groups by following
[
LiVe
,
Pe
,
Rao
]
.

2.3.
2-cocycle I

Let
\psi=\psi_{0}^{e}
be a non-trivial character of
F
, for some
e\in F^{\times}
. Let
(Q,V)
be a non-degenerate quadratic vector space on
F
. Then:

Q(x+y)-Q(x)-Q(y)=(x,y\rho),

for an isomorphism
\rho:V\longrightarrow V^{\ast}=\operatorname{Hom}_{F}(V,F)
. Applying the character
\psi
, we obtain

\psi(Q(x+y))\psi(Q(x))^{-1}\psi(Q(y))^{-1}=\psi(x,y\rho).

Note that
\psi(x,y\rho)
is a bicharacter in
x
and
y
. Following Weil, we call
\psi(Q(x))
a character of second degree. By
[
We
]
, there exists a complex constant associated with
\psi(Q(x))
, called the Weil index, denoted by
\gamma(\psi(Q))
.

Example 2.1

(
[
Rao
, Prop. A.10]
)
.

If
V=F
and
Q(x)=x^{2}
, then
\gamma(\psi(Q))=\psi_{0}(\tfrac{\operatorname{sign}e}{8})
.

If
(Q,V)
is the above case, we will write
\gamma(\psi)
for
\gamma(\psi(Q))
, and define
\gamma(a,\psi)=\tfrac{\gamma(\psi^{a})}{\gamma(\psi)}
by following
[
Ku2
]
. For the general non-degenerate quadratic space
(Q,V)
, we have:

Lemma 2.2

(
[
Rao
, Def.A.6]
)
.

\gamma(\psi(Q))=\epsilon(Q)\gamma(\psi)^{\dim V}\gamma(\det Q,\psi)
.

For
g_{1},g_{2}\in\operatorname{Sp}(W)
, let
q(g_{1},g_{2})=q(X^{\ast},X^{\ast}g_{2}^{-1},X^{\ast}g_{1})
be the corresponding Leray invariant as given in
[
Rao
, Sections 2.3-2.6]
. Let us define:

\displaystyle\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})=\gamma(\psi(q(g_{1},g_{2})/2)).

(2.1)

Then
\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(-,-)
defines a non-trivial class of order
2
in
\operatorname{H}^{2}(\operatorname{Sp}(W),\mu_{8})
. Let
\widetilde{\operatorname{Sp}}(W)
denote the associated Metaplectic group.

2.4.
2-cocycle II

As is known that
\operatorname{Sp}(W)=\sqcup_{j=0}^{m}C_{j}
,
where
C_{j}=P\omega_{S}P
for the above
\omega_{S}
with
|S|=j
. In
[
Rao
]
, Rao defined the following functions:

\displaystyle x:\operatorname{Sp}(W)\longrightarrow F^{\times}/{(F^{\times})}^{2};p_{1}\omega_{S}p_{2}\longmapsto\det(p_{1}p_{2}|_{X^{\ast}})(F^{\times})^{2},

(2.2)

\displaystyle t:\operatorname{Sp}(W)\times\operatorname{Sp}(W)\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z};(g_{1},g_{2})\longmapsto\tfrac{1}{2}(|S_{1}|+|S_{2}|-|S_{3}|-l),

(2.3)

where
g_{1}=p_{1}\omega_{S_{1}}p_{1}^{\prime},g_{2}=p_{2}\omega_{S_{2}}p_{2}^{\prime}
and
g_{1}g_{2}=p_{3}\omega_{S_{3}}p_{3}^{\prime},l=\dim q(g_{1},g_{2})
. By
[
Rao
, p.360, Def.5.2]
, we can define the normalizing constant by

\displaystyle m_{X^{\ast}}:\operatorname{Sp}(W)\longrightarrow\mu_{8};g\longmapsto\gamma(x(g),\psi^{\tfrac{1}{2}})^{-1}\gamma(\psi^{\tfrac{1}{2}})^{-j(g)}

(2.4)

for
g=p_{1}\omega_{S}p_{2}
,
j(g)=|S|
. Let us define

\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})=m_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1}g_{2})^{-1}m_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1})m_{X^{\ast}}(g_{2})\widetilde{c}_{{X^{\ast}}}(g_{1},g_{2}),\quad\quad g_{i}\in\operatorname{Sp}(W).

(2.5)

By
[
Rao
, p.360]
,

\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}
defines a
2
-cocycle on
\operatorname{Sp}(W)
with values in
\mu_{2}
.
Let
\overline{\operatorname{Sp}}(W)
denote the associated Metaplectic group. More precisely, by
[
Rao
, Thm.5.3]
and
[
Ku2
, p.21, Remark 4.6]
,we have:

\displaystyle\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})=(x(g_{1}),x(g_{2}))_{F}(-x(g_{1})x(g_{2}),x(g_{1}g_{2}))_{F}((-1)^{t},\det(2q))_{F}(-1,-1)_{F}^{\tfrac{t(t-1)}{2}}\epsilon(2q),

(2.6)

where
t=t(g_{1},g_{2})
,
q=q(g_{1},g_{2})
, for
g_{1},g_{2}\in\operatorname{Sp}(W)
.

Example 2.3
.

If
\dim W=2
,
\operatorname{Sp}(W)\simeq\operatorname{SL}_{2}(F)
. For
g_{1},g_{2},g_{3}=g_{1}g_{2}\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(F)
with
g_{i}=\begin{pmatrix}a_{i}&b_{i}\\
c_{i}&d_{i}\end{pmatrix}
, we have:

(1)

x(g_{1})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}d_{1}F^{\times 2}&\textrm{ if }c_{1}=0\\
c_{1}F^{\times 2}&\textrm{ if }c_{1}\neq 0\end{array}\right.
;

(2)

\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})=(x(g_{1}),x(g_{2}))_{F}(-x(g_{1})x(g_{2}),x(g_{3}))_{F}
.

Proof.

See
[
Rao
, p.364]
.
∎

In particular, if we take
g_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}
, then
\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})=(x(g_{1}),x(g_{2}))_{F}=(-1,x(g_{2}))_{F}
, which is not continuous on the variable
g_{2}
. For the reason, we can take a series of elements
g_{2}^{(n)}=\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
\tfrac{1}{n}&-1\end{pmatrix}
. Then:
g_{2}^{(n)}\longrightarrow g_{1}
as
n\longrightarrow+\infty
, and
\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2}^{(n)})=1
, but
\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{1})=-1
. Note that
\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{1}^{-1})\neq 1
.

2.5.
Schrödinger model

Note that
X^{\ast}\times F
is a closed subgroup of
\operatorname{H}(W)
. Let
\psi_{X^{\ast}}
be a one-dimensional unitary representation of
X^{\ast}\times F
extended from
\psi
by the trivial action of
X^{\ast}
. Let

\pi_{\psi}=\operatorname{Ind}_{X^{\ast}\times F}^{\operatorname{H}(W)}\psi_{X^{\ast}},V_{\psi}=\operatorname{Ind}_{X^{\ast}\times F}^{\operatorname{H}(W)}\mathbb{C}.

According to
[
BeHa
, pp.110-111]
,
\pi_{\psi}
defines a Heisenberg representation
\operatorname{H}(W)
with central character
\psi
. By Weil’s result,
\pi_{\psi}
can extend to be a unitary representation of
\widetilde{\operatorname{Sp}}(W)\ltimes\operatorname{H}(W)
. Here, we require the action of the center subgroup
\mu_{8}
of
\widetilde{\operatorname{Sp}}(W)
to be the identity map. Since
\operatorname{Sp}(W)
is a perfect group, this extension is unique.

Let
dx
denote the self-dual Haar measure on
F
with respect to
\psi
. Let
dx
,
dx^{\ast}
be the Haar measures on
X
,
X^{\ast}
, given by the product measures from
dx
of
F
. The representation
\pi_{\psi}
can be realized on
L^{2}(X)
by the following formulas:

\pi_{\psi}[(x,0)+(x^{\ast},0)+(0,k)]f(y)=\psi(k+\langle x+y,x^{\ast}\rangle)f(x+y),

(2.7)

\pi_{\psi}[\begin{pmatrix}1&b\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},t]f(y)=t\psi(\tfrac{1}{2}\langle y,yb\rangle)f(y),

(2.8)

\pi_{\psi}[\begin{pmatrix}a&0\\
0&a^{\ast-1}\end{pmatrix},t]f(y)=t|\det(a)|^{1/2}f(ya),

(2.9)

\pi_{\psi}([\omega,t])f(y)=t\int_{X^{\ast}}\psi(\langle y,y^{\ast}\rangle)f(y^{\ast}\omega^{-1})dy^{\ast},

(2.10)

where
f(y)\in S(X)
,
x,y\in X,x^{\ast},y^{\ast}\in X^{\ast}
,
k\in F
,
t\in\mu_{8}
, and
\begin{pmatrix}a&0\\
0&a^{\ast-1}\end{pmatrix}
,
\begin{pmatrix}1&b\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}
,
\omega\in\operatorname{Sp}(W)
,
e_{i}\omega=-e_{i}^{\ast}
,
e_{i}^{\ast}\omega=e_{i}
.
Moreover, under such actions, for
g_{1},g_{2}\in\operatorname{Sp}(W)
,

\Pi_{\psi}(g_{1})\Pi_{\psi}(g_{2})=\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})\Pi_{\psi}(g_{1}g_{2}),

for the Perrin-Rao’s 2-cocyle
\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(-,-)
associated with
\psi
and
X^{\ast}
. This precise result can be seen from
[
Rao
]
and
[
Pe
]
.

For the other elements
\omega_{S}
, let
X_{S}=\operatorname{Span}\{e_{i}\mid i\in S\}
,
X_{S^{\prime}}=\operatorname{Span}\{e_{i}\mid i\notin S\}
and
X_{S}^{\ast}=\operatorname{Span}\{e^{\ast}_{i}\mid i\in S\}
,
X_{S^{\prime}}^{\ast}=\operatorname{Span}\{e^{\ast}_{i}\mid i\notin S\}
. By
[
Pe
, p.388, Prop.2.1.4]
or
[
Rao
, p.351, Lmm.3.2,(3.9)]
, for
x=x_{s}+x_{s^{\prime}}\in X_{S}\oplus X_{S^{\prime}}
,

\begin{split}[\Pi_{\psi}(\omega_{S})f](x_{s}+x_{s^{\prime}})&=\int_{X_{S}^{\ast}}\psi(\tfrac{1}{2}\langle x_{s^{\prime}}\omega_{S},x_{s}\omega_{S}\rangle+\langle z_{s}^{\ast}\omega_{S},x_{s}\omega_{S}\rangle)f(x_{s^{\prime}}\omega_{S}+z_{s}^{\ast}\omega_{S})dz_{s}^{\ast}\\
&=\int_{X_{S}^{\ast}}\psi(\langle x_{s},z_{s}^{\ast}\rangle)f(x_{s^{\prime}}\omega_{S}+z_{s}^{\ast}\omega^{-1}_{S})dz_{s}^{\ast}.\end{split}

(2.11)

2.6.
Lattice model

To state the lattice model, let us define another type of Heisenberg group
\operatorname{H}_{\psi}(W)
, which consists of the set
W\oplus T
, with the multiplication law given by

(w,t)(w^{\prime},t^{\prime})=(w+w^{\prime},tt^{\prime}\psi(\langle w,w^{\prime}\rangle/2)),\quad\quad w,w^{\prime}\in W,t,t^{\prime}\in T.

Then there exists a group homomorphism:

h_{\psi}:\operatorname{H}(W)\longrightarrow\operatorname{H}_{\psi}(W);(w,t)\longmapsto(w,\psi(t)).

Let
L
be a lattice in
W
(cf.
[
LiVe
, p.138]
). Let us define

L^{\bot}=\{w\in W\mid\psi(\langle w,l\rangle)=1,\textrm{ for all }l\in L\}.

If
L=L^{\bot}
,
L
is called a self-dual lattice with respect to
\psi
. Let
\operatorname{H}(L)
be the subgroup of
\operatorname{H}(W)
with the underlying set
L\times F
. Let
\operatorname{H}_{\psi}(L)
be the image of
\operatorname{H}(L)
in
\operatorname{H}_{\psi}(W)
via the map
h_{\psi}
. Then
\operatorname{H}_{\psi}(L)
is a normal maximal abelian subgroup of
\operatorname{H}_{\psi}(W)
. Let us define

\rho=\operatorname{Ind}_{\operatorname{H}_{\psi}(L)}^{\operatorname{H}_{\psi}(W)}\psi_{T},

where
\psi_{T}
is a unitary character of
\operatorname{H}_{\psi}(L)
which extends the identity action on
T
. By
[
BeHa
, Lemma 3.B.3]
and
[
KaTa
, Theorem 4.3.10]
,
\rho
is an irreducible representation of
\operatorname{H}_{\psi}(W)
.

Let us go back to
\operatorname{H}(W)
. Let
\psi_{L}=\psi_{T}\circ h_{\psi}
, which is a one-dimensional unitary character of
\operatorname{H}(L)
extended from
\psi
of
F
. Let us define:

\pi^{\prime}_{\psi}=\operatorname{Ind}_{\operatorname{H}(L)}^{\operatorname{H}(W)}\psi_{L},

It can be seen that
\pi^{\prime}_{\psi}\simeq\rho\circ h_{\psi}
. Hence
\pi_{\psi}^{\prime}
defines a Heisenberg representation of
\operatorname{H}(W)
with central character
\psi
.

Example 2.4
.

If
\psi=\psi_{0}
,
L=\mathbb{Z}e_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathbb{Z}e_{m}\oplus\mathbb{Z}e_{1}^{\ast}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathbb{Z}e_{m}^{\ast}
, we let
\mathcal{H}_{\psi}(L)
be the set of measurable functions
f:W\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
such that

(i)

f(l+w)=\psi(-\tfrac{\langle x_{1},x_{l}^{\ast}\rangle}{2}-\tfrac{\langle l,w\rangle}{2})f(w)
, for
l=x_{l}+x_{l}^{\ast}\in L=(L\cap X)\oplus(L\cap X^{\ast})
,
w\in W
;

(ii)

\int_{L\setminus W}||f(w)||^{2}dw<+\infty
.

Here, we choose a
W
-right invariant measure on
L\setminus W
. Then
\pi_{\psi}^{\prime}
can be realized on
\mathcal{H}_{\psi}(L)
by the following formulas:

\pi_{\psi}^{\prime}([w^{\prime},t])f(w)=\psi(t+\tfrac{\langle w,w^{\prime}\rangle}{2})f(w+w^{\prime})

for
w,w^{\prime}\in W
,
t\in F
.

3.
Extension to
\operatorname{GSp}(W)

Our main purpose is to extend Barthel’s results in
[
Ba
]
from the cases of the
p
-adic field to the real field.

3.1.
2
-cocycle I

Let us first consider the
8
-degree covering case. Note that
[\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}]
is the unique non-trivial class of order
2
in
\operatorname{H}^{2}(\operatorname{Sp}(W),\mu_{8})
, and
\operatorname{Sp}(W)
is a perfect group. Let
\alpha
now be a continuous automorphism of
\operatorname{Sp}(W)
. By Moore’s cohomology theory, there exists a unique automorphism
\alpha^{\ast}
of
\widetilde{\operatorname{Sp}}(W)
such that the following diagram

\begin{CD}1@>{}>{}>\mu_{8}@>{}>{}>\widetilde{\operatorname{Sp}}(W)@>{}>{}>\operatorname{Sp}(W)@>{}>{}>1\\
\Big\|@V{}V{\alpha^{\ast}}V@V{}V{\alpha}V\\
1@>{}>{}>\mu_{8}@>{}>{}>\widetilde{\operatorname{Sp}}(W)@>{}>{}>\operatorname{Sp}(W)@>{}>{}>1\end{CD}

is commutative. Moreover, there exists a unique function
\nu(\alpha,-):\operatorname{Sp}(W)\longrightarrow\mu_{8}
, such that the following equality holds:

\displaystyle\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1}^{\alpha},g_{2}^{\alpha})=\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})\nu(\alpha,g_{1})^{-1}\nu(\alpha,g_{2})^{-1}\nu(\alpha,g_{1}g_{2}).

(3.1)

The map
\alpha^{\ast}
is given as:

\alpha^{\ast}:\widetilde{\operatorname{Sp}}(W)\longrightarrow\widetilde{\operatorname{Sp}}(W);[g,\epsilon]\longmapsto[g,\nu(\alpha,g)\epsilon].

Lemma 3.1
.

For two automorphisms
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}
,
\nu(\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2},g)=\nu(\alpha_{1},g)\nu(\alpha_{2},g^{\alpha_{1}})
.

Proof.

Because
(g,\epsilon)^{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}=(g^{\alpha_{1}},\nu(\alpha_{1},g)\epsilon)^{\alpha_{2}}=(g^{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}},\nu(\alpha_{1},g)\nu(\alpha_{2},g^{\alpha_{1}})\epsilon)
.
∎

Example 3.2
.

If
\alpha=h\in\operatorname{Sp}(W)
, and
h
acts on
\operatorname{Sp}(W)
by conjugation, i.e.,
g^{h}=h^{-1}gh
, for
g\in\operatorname{Sp}(W)
, then
\nu(\alpha,g)=\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(h^{-1},gh)\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g,h)
.

Proof.

By the properties of the cocycles, we obtain:

\tfrac{\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1}^{h},g_{2}^{h})}{\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})}=[\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(h^{-1},g_{1}g_{2}h)\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1}g_{2},h)][\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(h^{-1},g_{1}h)\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},h)]^{-1}[\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(h^{-1},g_{2}h)\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{2},h)]^{-1}.

(3.2)

So
\nu(\alpha,g)=\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(h^{-1},gh)\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g,h)
by the uniqueness.
∎

Example 3.3

(Barthel)
.

If
\alpha=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&y\end{pmatrix}
, for some
y\in F^{\times}
, then

\displaystyle\nu(\alpha,g)=(\det a_{1}a_{2},y)_{F}\gamma(y,\psi^{\tfrac{1}{2}})^{-|S|},

(3.3)

for
g=p_{1}\omega_{S}p_{2}
,
p_{i}=\begin{pmatrix}a_{i}&b_{i}\\
0&d_{i}\end{pmatrix}
,
i=1,2
.

Proof.

We shall follow the proof of
[
Ba
, p.212, Prop.1.2.A]
in the
p
-adic case.
By (
3.1
), there exists a character
\chi_{y}
of
F^{\times}
and a non-zero complex number
\beta_{y}
such that

•

\nu(\alpha,g)=\nu(\alpha,p_{1})\nu(\alpha,\omega_{S})\nu(\alpha,p_{2})
,

•

\nu(y,\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
0&(a^{\ast})^{-1}\end{pmatrix})=\chi_{y}(\det a)
, and

•

\nu(y,\omega_{S})=\beta_{y}^{|S|}
.

By the embedding
\operatorname{SL}_{2}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Sp}
, it reduces to discussing the simple case that
\dim W=2
. Following
[
Ba
, p.212]
, let
u_{-}(x)=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
x&1\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}
. Then:

\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
x&1\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}1&x^{-1}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}x&1\\
0&x^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\quad\textrm{ implies }\quad\nu(y,u_{-}(x))=\beta_{y}\chi_{y}(x).

For
s,t,s+t\in F^{\times}
,

\begin{split}\beta_{y}\chi_{y}(s+t)=\nu(y,u_{-}(s+t))&=\nu(y,u_{-}(s))\nu(y,u_{-}(t))\tfrac{\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(u_{-}(s)^{y},u_{-}(t)^{y})}{\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(u_{-}(s),u_{-}(t))}\\
&=\nu(y,u_{-}(s))\nu(y,u_{-}(t))\tfrac{\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(u_{-}(sy^{-1}),u_{-}(ty^{-1}))}{\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(u_{-}(s),u_{-}(t))}\\
&=\beta_{y}\chi_{y}(s)\beta_{y}\chi_{y}(t)\tfrac{\gamma(\psi^{\tfrac{1}{2}st(s+t)y^{-3}})}{\gamma(\psi^{\tfrac{1}{2}st(s+t)})}\\
&=\beta_{y}^{2}\chi_{y}(st)(st(s+t),y)_{F}\gamma(y,\psi^{\tfrac{1}{2}}).\end{split}

(3.4)

Hence:

\displaystyle\beta_{y}^{-1}\chi_{y}(\tfrac{1}{s}+\tfrac{1}{t})=(\tfrac{1}{s}+\tfrac{1}{t},y)_{F}\gamma(y,\psi^{\tfrac{1}{2}}).

(3.5)

Therefore,
\chi_{y}(s)=(s,y)_{F}
,
\beta_{y}^{-1}=\gamma(y,\psi^{\tfrac{1}{2}})
.
∎

3.2.
Subgroups and quotient groups of
\operatorname{GSp}(W)

Let
F^{\times}_{+}
be the multiplicative subgroup of positive real numbers. Let
\operatorname{GSp}^{+}(W)=F^{\times}_{+}\operatorname{Sp}(W)
,
\operatorname{PGSp}(W)=\operatorname{GSp}(W)/F^{\times}
,
\operatorname{PGSp}^{\pm}(W)=\operatorname{GSp}(W)/F_{+}^{\times}
.
Then:

•

F_{+}^{\times}=F^{\times 2}
and
F^{\times}/F^{\times 2}\simeq\mu_{2}
.

•

There exists an exact sequence:
1\longrightarrow\operatorname{Sp}(W)\longrightarrow\operatorname{GSp}(W)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lambda}}{{\longrightarrow}}F^{\times}\longrightarrow 1.

•

There exists an exact sequence:
1\longrightarrow\operatorname{Sp}(W)\longrightarrow\operatorname{PGSp}^{\pm}(W)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\dot{\lambda}}}{{\longrightarrow}}F^{\times}/F^{\times 2}\longrightarrow 1.

Let us choose a section map

\displaystyle s:F^{\times}\longrightarrow\operatorname{GSp}(W);y\longmapsto\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&y\end{pmatrix}.

(3.6)

Let
F_{2}
denote the image of
\mu_{2}
. Then:

\operatorname{GSp}(W)\simeq F^{\times}\ltimes\operatorname{Sp}(W),\quad\quad\operatorname{PGSp}^{\pm}(W)\simeq F_{2}\ltimes\operatorname{Sp}(W).

Let us define

\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}(W)=\{h\in\operatorname{GSp}(W)\mid\lambda_{h}=\pm 1\}.

Then
\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}(W)\simeq F_{2}\ltimes\operatorname{Sp}(W)
, which is a subgroup of
F^{\times}\ltimes\operatorname{Sp}(W)
. By lifting the action of
F^{\times}
from
\operatorname{Sp}(W)
to
\widetilde{\operatorname{Sp}}(W)
, we obtain a group
F^{\times}\ltimes\widetilde{\operatorname{Sp}}(W)
; let us denote it by
\widetilde{\operatorname{GSp}}(W)
. Then there exists an exact sequence:

1\longrightarrow\mu_{8}\longrightarrow\widetilde{\operatorname{GSp}}(W)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{GSp}(W)\longrightarrow 1.

Let
\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}
denote the
2
-cocycle associated to this exact sequence from
\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}
. For
(y_{1},g_{1},\epsilon_{1}),(y_{2},g_{2},\epsilon_{2})\in F^{\times}\ltimes\widetilde{\operatorname{Sp}}(W)
,

\begin{split}(y_{1},g_{1},\epsilon_{1})\cdot(y_{2},g_{2},\epsilon_{2})&=(y_{1}y_{2},[g_{1},\epsilon_{1}]^{y_{2}}[g_{2},\epsilon_{2}])\\
&=(y_{1}y_{2},[g_{1}^{y_{2}},\nu(y_{2},g_{1})\epsilon_{1}][g_{2},\epsilon_{2}])\\
&=(y_{1}y_{2},[g_{1}^{y_{2}}g_{2},\nu(y_{2},g_{1})\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1}^{y_{2}},g_{2})\epsilon_{1}\epsilon_{2}]).\end{split}

(3.7)

Hence:

\displaystyle\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}([y_{1},g_{1}],[y_{2},g_{2}])=\nu(y_{2},g_{1})\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1}^{y_{2}},g_{2}),\quad\quad[y_{i},g_{i}]\in F^{\times}\ltimes\operatorname{Sp}(W).

(3.8)

By the restriction on
F_{2}
, we obtain a subgroup
F_{2}\ltimes\widetilde{\operatorname{Sp}}(W)
; let us denote it
\widetilde{\operatorname{Sp}}^{\pm}(W)
. Then there exists an exact sequence:

\displaystyle 1\longrightarrow\mu_{8}\longrightarrow\widetilde{\operatorname{Sp}}^{\pm}(W)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}(W)\longrightarrow 1.

(3.9)

Lemma 3.4
.

\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}(h_{1},h_{2})=1
, for
h_{i}\in F_{2}\subseteq\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}(W)
.

Proof.

It follows from the above formula (
3.8
).
∎

Lemma 3.5
.

\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}(yI_{2m},h)=1=\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}(h,yI_{2m})
, for
y\in F^{\times}_{+}
,
h\in\operatorname{GSp}(W)
.

Proof.

Let us write
yI_{2m}
and
h
in the forms of
s(y^{2})g_{y}
and
s(h)g_{h}
by using the section map
s
as given in (
3.6
), for
g_{y}=\begin{pmatrix}y&0\\
0&y^{-1}\end{pmatrix}
,
s(h)=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&\lambda_{h}\end{pmatrix}
,
g_{h}=p_{1}\omega_{S}p_{2}\in\operatorname{Sp}(W)
,
p_{i}=\begin{pmatrix}a_{i}&b_{i}\\
0&d_{i}\end{pmatrix}
,
i=1,2
. Then:

\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}(yI_{2m},h)=\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}([y^{2},g_{y}],[\lambda_{h},g_{h}])=\nu(\lambda_{h},g_{y})\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{y}^{\lambda_{h}},g_{h})=(y^{m},\lambda_{h})_{F}=1,

\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}(h,yI_{2m})=\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}([\lambda_{h},g_{h}],[y^{2},g_{y}])=\nu(y^{2},g_{h})\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{h}^{y^{2}},g_{y})=\nu(y^{2},g_{h})=(\det a_{1}a_{2},y^{2})_{F}\gamma(y^{2},\psi^{\tfrac{1}{2}})^{-|S|}=1.

∎

As a consequence, we can see that
F_{+}^{\times}
lies in the center of
\widetilde{\operatorname{GSp}}(W)
and
\widetilde{\operatorname{GSp}}(W)/F^{\times}_{+}\simeq\widetilde{\operatorname{Sp}}^{\pm}(W)
.

Lemma 3.6
.

If
2\nmid m
,
\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}(\begin{pmatrix}y_{1}&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}y_{2}&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix})=(y_{1},y_{2})_{F}
, for
y_{i}\in F^{\times}
.

Proof.

Write
\begin{pmatrix}y_{i}&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}=s(y_{i})g_{i}
,
for
g_{i}=\begin{pmatrix}y_{i}&0\\
0&y_{i}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}.
Then

\displaystyle\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}(\begin{pmatrix}y_{1}&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}y_{2}&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}([y_{1},g_{1}],[y_{2},g_{2}])

(3.10)

\displaystyle=\nu(y_{2},g_{1})\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1}^{y_{2}},g_{2})

(3.11)

\displaystyle=(y_{1}^{m},y_{2})_{F}=(y_{1},y_{2})_{F}.

(3.12)

∎

Notice that
\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}(s(y_{1}),s(y_{2}))=1
, for
y_{i}\in F^{\times}
.

Remark 3.7
.

If define
f:F^{\times}\longrightarrow\mu_{8};y\longmapsto e^{\tfrac{[1-\operatorname{sgn}(y)]\pi i}{4}}
, then
(y_{1},y_{2})_{F}=f(y_{1})^{-1}f(y_{2})^{-1}f(y_{1}y_{2})
.

3.3.
2
-cocycle II

Instead of the
8
-degree cover, let us consider the
2
-degree covering case. For an automorphism
\alpha
of
\operatorname{Sp}(W)
, let us define
\nu_{2}:\alpha\times\operatorname{Sp}(W)\longrightarrow\mu_{2}
such that

(g,\epsilon)^{\alpha}=(g^{\alpha},\nu_{2}(\alpha,g)\epsilon),\qquad\qquad(g,\epsilon)\in\overline{\operatorname{Sp}}(W).

Similarly,
\nu_{2}
determines an automorphism of
\overline{\operatorname{Sp}}(W)
iff the following equality holds:

\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g,g^{\prime})=\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g^{\alpha},g^{{}^{\prime}\alpha})\nu_{2}(\alpha,g)\nu_{2}(\alpha,g^{\prime})\nu_{2}(\alpha,gg^{\prime})^{-1}.

(3.13)

Lemma 3.8
.

\nu_{2}(\alpha,g)=\nu(\alpha,g)\tfrac{m_{X^{\ast}}(g)}{m_{X^{\ast}}(g^{\alpha})}
.

Proof.

By (
2.5
),(
3.1
),(
3.13
),

\begin{split}&\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g,g^{\prime})m_{X^{\ast}}(gg^{\prime})m_{X^{\ast}}(g)^{-1}m_{X^{\ast}}(g^{\prime})^{-1}\\
&=\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g,g^{\prime})\\
&=\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g^{\alpha},g^{{}^{\prime}\alpha})\nu(\alpha,g)\nu(\alpha,g^{\prime})\nu(\alpha,gg^{\prime})^{-1}\\
&=\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g^{\alpha},g^{{}^{\prime}\alpha})m_{X^{\ast}}(g^{\alpha}g^{{}^{\prime}\alpha})m_{X^{\ast}}(g^{\alpha})^{-1}m_{X^{\ast}}(g^{{}^{\prime}\alpha})^{-1}\nu(\alpha,g)\nu(\alpha,g^{\prime})\nu(\alpha,gg^{\prime})^{-1}.\end{split}

Hence:

\tfrac{\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g,g^{\prime})}{\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g^{\alpha},g^{{}^{\prime}\alpha})}=[\tfrac{m_{X^{\ast}}(gg^{\prime})}{m_{X^{\ast}}(g^{\alpha}g^{{}^{\prime}\alpha})}]^{-1}\tfrac{m_{X^{\ast}}(g)}{m_{X^{\ast}}(g^{\alpha})}\tfrac{m_{X^{\ast}}(g^{\prime})}{m_{X^{\ast}}(g^{{}^{\prime}\alpha})}\nu(\alpha,g)\nu(\alpha,g^{\prime})\nu(\alpha,gg^{\prime})^{-1}.

So
\nu_{2}(\alpha,g)=\nu(\alpha,g)\tfrac{m_{X^{\ast}}(g)}{m_{X^{\ast}}(g^{\alpha})}
.
∎

By lifting the action of
F^{\times}
from
\operatorname{Sp}(W)
to
\overline{\operatorname{Sp}}(W)
, we can obtain a group
F^{\times}\ltimes\overline{\operatorname{Sp}}(W)
and an exact sequence:

1\longrightarrow\mu_{2}\longrightarrow F^{\times}\ltimes\overline{\operatorname{Sp}}(W)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\longrightarrow}}F^{\times}\ltimes\operatorname{Sp}(W)\longrightarrow 1.

Let us write
\overline{\operatorname{GSp}}(W)
for
F^{\times}\ltimes\overline{\operatorname{Sp}}(W)
and denote the corresponding
2
-cocycle by
\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}
. Then:

\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}([y_{1},g_{1}],[y_{2},g_{2}])=\nu_{2}(y_{2},g_{1})\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1}^{y_{2}},g_{2}),\quad\quad[y_{i},g_{i}]\in F^{\times}\ltimes\operatorname{Sp}(W).

Consequently, substituting the equality in Lemma
3.8
, we obtain:

\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}([y_{1},g_{1}],[y_{2},g_{2}])=m_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1}^{y_{2}}g_{2})^{-1}m_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1})m_{X^{\ast}}(g_{2})\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}([y_{1},g_{1}],[y_{2},g_{2}]).

(3.14)

By abuse of notations, let us also write
m_{X^{\ast}}[y_{1},g_{1}]=m_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1})
. By the restriction on
F_{2}
, we obtain a subgroup
F_{2}\ltimes\overline{\operatorname{Sp}}(W)
; let us denote it
\overline{\operatorname{Sp}}^{\pm}(W)
. Then there exists an exact sequence:

\displaystyle 1\longrightarrow\mu_{2}\longrightarrow\overline{\operatorname{Sp}}^{\pm}(W)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}(W)\longrightarrow 1.

(3.15)

Lemma 3.9
.

\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}(yI_{2m},h)=1=\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}(h,yI_{2m})
, for
y\in F^{\times}_{+}
,
h\in\operatorname{GSp}(W)
.

Proof.

Let
yI_{2m}=s(y)g_{y}
and
h=s(h)g_{h}
for
g_{y}=\begin{pmatrix}y&0\\
0&y^{-1}\end{pmatrix}
,
s(h)=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&\lambda_{h}\end{pmatrix}
,
g_{h}=p_{1}\omega_{S}p_{2}\in\operatorname{Sp}(W)
,
p_{i}=\begin{pmatrix}a_{i}&b_{i}\\
0&d_{i}\end{pmatrix}
,
i=1,2
. Then:

\displaystyle\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}(yI_{2m},h)

\displaystyle=\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}([y,g_{y}],[\lambda_{h},g_{h}])

(3.16)

\displaystyle=m_{X^{\ast}}(g_{y}^{\lambda_{h}}g_{h})^{-1}m_{X^{\ast}}(g_{y})m_{X^{\ast}}(g_{h})\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}([y,g_{y}],[\lambda_{h},g_{h}])

(3.17)

\displaystyle=m_{X^{\ast}}(g_{y}^{\lambda_{h}}g_{h})^{-1}m_{X^{\ast}}(g_{y})m_{X^{\ast}}(g_{h})

(3.18)

\displaystyle=m_{X^{\ast}}(g_{y}g_{h})^{-1}m_{X^{\ast}}(g_{y})m_{X^{\ast}}(g_{h})

(3.19)

\displaystyle=(x(g_{y}),x(g_{h}))_{F}=(y^{m},x(g_{h}))_{F}=1;

(3.20)

\displaystyle\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}(h,yI_{2m})

\displaystyle=\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}([\lambda_{h},g_{h}],[y,g_{y}])

(3.21)

\displaystyle=m_{X^{\ast}}(g_{h}^{y}g_{y})^{-1}m_{X^{\ast}}(g_{h})m_{X^{\ast}}(g_{y})\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}([\lambda_{h},g_{h}],[y,g_{y}])

(3.22)

\displaystyle=m_{X^{\ast}}(g_{h}^{y}g_{y})^{-1}m_{X^{\ast}}(g_{h})m_{X^{\ast}}(g_{y})

(3.23)

\displaystyle=(x(g_{h}^{y}),x(g_{y}))_{F}=(x(g_{h}^{y}),y^{m})_{F}=1.

(3.24)

∎

As a consequence,
F_{+}^{\times}
lies in the center of
\overline{\operatorname{GSp}}(W)
and
\overline{\operatorname{GSp}}(W)/F^{\times}_{+}\simeq\overline{\operatorname{Sp}}^{\pm}(W)
.

3.3.1.
Example

Let us consider the case that
\dim W=2
,
\operatorname{GSp}(W)\simeq\operatorname{GL}_{2}(F)
. Let
h_{i}=\begin{pmatrix}a_{i}&b_{i}\\
c_{i}&d_{i}\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{GL}_{2}(F)
,
i=1,2,3
, with
h_{3}=h_{1}h_{2}
. Let us write
h_{i}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&y_{i}\end{pmatrix}g_{i}
, for
y_{i}=\det h_{i}
,
g_{i}=\begin{pmatrix}a_{i}&b_{i}\\
y_{i}^{-1}c_{i}&y_{i}^{-1}d_{i}\end{pmatrix}
. Then:

\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}(h_{1},h_{2})=\nu_{2}(y_{2},g_{1})\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1}^{y_{2}},g_{2})=\nu_{2}(\det h_{2},g_{1})\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1}^{\det h_{2}},g_{2}).

(1)

If
c_{1}=0
,
m_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1})=m_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1}^{y_{2}})
, so
\nu_{2}(y_{2},g_{1})=\nu(y_{2},g_{1})=(y_{2},a_{1})_{F}
.

(2)

If
c_{1}\neq 0
, then:

m_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1})=\gamma(c_{1}y_{1}^{-1},\psi^{\tfrac{1}{2}})^{-1}\gamma(\psi^{\tfrac{1}{2}})^{-1}=\gamma({\psi}^{\tfrac{1}{2}c_{1}y_{1}^{-1}})^{-1}
,

m_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1}^{y_{2}})=\gamma({\psi}^{\tfrac{1}{2}c_{1}y_{1}^{-1}y_{2}^{-1}})^{-1}
,

\tfrac{m_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1})}{m_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1}^{y_{2}})}=\gamma(y^{-1}_{2},\psi^{\tfrac{1}{2}})(y_{1}c_{1},y_{2})_{F}
,

\nu_{2}(y_{2},g_{1})=\nu(y_{2},g_{1})\tfrac{m_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1})}{m_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1}^{y_{2}})}=(y_{1}c_{1},y_{2})_{F}\gamma(y_{2},\psi^{\tfrac{1}{2}})^{-1}\gamma(y^{-1}_{2},\psi^{\tfrac{1}{2}})(y_{1}c_{1},y_{2})_{F}=1
.

4.
Extended Weil representations

4.1.

Recall that
\pi_{\psi}
is a Weil representation of
\widetilde{\operatorname{Sp}}(W)\ltimes\operatorname{H}(W)
associated to
\psi
, and
\omega_{\psi}=\pi_{\psi}|_{\widetilde{\operatorname{Sp}}(W)}
. Let
\widetilde{\operatorname{GSp}}(W)
act on
\operatorname{H}(W)
via
\operatorname{GSp}(W)
.

Lemma 4.1
.

\omega_{\psi}\simeq\omega_{\psi^{h^{2}}}
, for any
h\in F^{\times}
, and
\omega_{\psi}\ncong\omega_{\psi^{-1}}
.

Proof.

We will follow the proof in
[
MVW
, p.36]
in the
p
-adic case. Let
\widetilde{h}=[h,1]\in\widetilde{\operatorname{GSp}}(W)
, and
\widetilde{g}=[g,\epsilon]\in\widetilde{\operatorname{Sp}}(W)
,
w_{t}=(w,t)\in\operatorname{H}(W)
. Then:

\pi_{\psi}(w_{t}^{\widetilde{h}})=\pi_{\psi}(wh,t\lambda_{h})\simeq\pi_{\psi^{\lambda_{h}}}(w_{t});

it implies that

\pi_{\psi}([\widetilde{g},w_{t}]^{\widetilde{h}})=\pi_{\psi}([\widetilde{g}^{\widetilde{h}},(wh,t\lambda_{h})])\simeq\pi_{\psi^{\lambda_{h}}}([\widetilde{g},w_{t}]).

In particular, by choosing
h\in F_{+}^{\times}
, we obtain:

\widetilde{g}^{\widetilde{h}}=\widetilde{g},

\omega_{\psi}(\widetilde{g})=\pi_{\psi}(\widetilde{g})\simeq\pi_{\psi^{h^{2}}}(\widetilde{g})=\omega_{\psi^{h^{2}}}(\widetilde{g}).

The second statement can derive from the character calculations of
\omega_{\psi}
and
\omega_{\psi^{-1}}
in
[
Th
, Thm.1C]
.
∎

Let us define:

\Pi_{\psi}=\operatorname{Ind}_{\widetilde{\operatorname{Sp}}(W)\ltimes\operatorname{H}(W)}^{\widetilde{\operatorname{Sp}}^{\pm}(W)\ltimes\operatorname{H}(W)}\pi_{\psi},\qquad\Omega_{\psi}=\operatorname{Res}_{\widetilde{\operatorname{Sp}}^{\pm}(W)}^{\widetilde{\operatorname{Sp}}^{\pm}(W)\ltimes\operatorname{H}(W)}\Pi_{\psi}.

By Clifford-Mackey theory,
\Omega_{\psi}\simeq\operatorname{Ind}_{\widetilde{\operatorname{Sp}}(W)}^{\widetilde{\operatorname{Sp}}^{\pm}(W)}\omega_{\psi}
; its restriction on
\widetilde{\operatorname{Sp}}(W)
consists of the two Weil representations
\omega_{\psi}
and
\omega_{\psi^{-1}}
. Therefore,
\Omega_{\psi}
is independent of the choice of
\psi
. Let us denote it by
\Omega
, and call it the Weil representation of
\widetilde{\operatorname{Sp}}^{\pm}(W)
.

Note that as a representation of
\operatorname{H}(W)
,
\Pi_{\psi}\simeq\pi_{\psi}\oplus\pi_{\psi^{-1}}
, which is not irreducible. Let

\operatorname{H}^{\pm}(W)=F_{2}\ltimes\operatorname{H}(W)
. Then
\Pi_{\psi}
is an irreducible representation of
\operatorname{H}^{\pm}(W)
.

4.2.
Schrödinger model

Retain notations from Section
2.5
. Then:

\Pi_{\psi}\simeq\operatorname{Ind}_{X^{\ast}\times F}^{\operatorname{H}^{\pm}(W)}\psi_{X^{\ast}}.

So
\Pi_{\psi}
can be realized on
L^{2}(\mu_{2}\times X)
by the following formulas:

\Pi_{\psi}[(x,0)+(x^{\ast},0)+(0,k)]f([\epsilon,y])=\psi(\epsilon k+\epsilon\langle x+y,x^{\ast}\rangle)f([\epsilon,x+y]),

(4.1)

\Pi_{\psi}(s(-1))f([\epsilon,y])=f([-\epsilon,y]),

(4.2)

for
x,y\in X
,
x^{\ast}\in X^{\ast}
,
k\in F
,
\epsilon\in\mu_{2}
.

Let us prove the results:

(x,0)+(x^{\ast},0)+(0,k)=(x,x^{\ast};k+\tfrac{\langle x,x^{\ast}\rangle}{2})\in\operatorname{H}(W);

\displaystyle\Pi_{\psi}[(x,0)+(x^{\ast},0)+(0,k)]f(\epsilon,y)

\displaystyle=f([\epsilon,y]\cdot[1,(x,x^{\ast};k+\tfrac{\langle x,x^{\ast}\rangle}{2})])

\displaystyle=f([\epsilon,(x+y,x^{\ast};k+\tfrac{\langle x+y,x^{\ast}\rangle}{2})]

\displaystyle=f([1,(\epsilon x^{\ast},\epsilon k+\epsilon\langle x+y,x^{\ast}\rangle)][\epsilon,x+y])

\displaystyle=\psi(\epsilon k+\epsilon\langle x+y,x^{\ast}\rangle)f([\epsilon,x+y]).

Following
[
Ba
, p.214]
, let us extend the above formulas (
4.1
),(
4.2
) to
\widetilde{\operatorname{Sp}}^{\pm}(W)
. Let us identity
L^{2}(\mu_{2}\times X)
with
\Pi_{\psi}(F_{2})L^{2}(X)
. For any
f\in L^{2}(\mu_{2}\times X)
, let us write
f([\epsilon,x])=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}f_{1}(x)&\textrm{ if }\epsilon=1,\\
\Pi_{\psi}(s(-1))f_{2}(x)&\textrm{ if }\epsilon=-1,\end{array}\right.
where
s:\mu_{2}\longrightarrow F_{2}
, given from (
3.6
). For
(1,g)\in\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}(W)
, we have:

\displaystyle([1,g],1)([\epsilon,1],1)

\displaystyle=([\epsilon,g^{s(\epsilon)}],\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}([1,g],[\epsilon,1]))

(4.3)

\displaystyle=([\epsilon,g^{s(\epsilon)}],\nu(\epsilon,g)\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g^{s(\epsilon)},1))

\displaystyle=([\epsilon,g^{s(\epsilon)}],\nu(\epsilon,g))

\displaystyle=([\epsilon,1],1)([1,g^{s(\epsilon)}],\nu(\epsilon,g)).

1) If
g=\begin{pmatrix}1&b\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}
, then
\nu(1,g)=1=\nu(-1,g)
.

\displaystyle\Pi_{\psi}(g)f([1,y])

\displaystyle=\pi_{\psi}(g)f_{1}(y)=\psi(\tfrac{1}{2}\langle y,yb\rangle)f_{1}(y)

\displaystyle=\psi(\tfrac{1}{2}\langle y,yb\rangle)f([1,y]),

\displaystyle\Pi_{\psi}(g)f([-1,y])

\displaystyle=\Pi_{\psi}([-1,1])\pi_{\psi}(g^{s(-1)})f_{2}(y)

\displaystyle=\psi(\tfrac{1}{2}\langle y,-yb\rangle)\Pi_{\psi}([-1,1],1)f_{2}(y)

\displaystyle=\psi(\tfrac{1}{2}\langle y,-yb\rangle)f([-1,y]).

Hence:

\Pi_{\psi}[\begin{pmatrix}1&b\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}]f([\epsilon,y])=\psi(\tfrac{1}{2}\langle y,\epsilon yb\rangle)f([\epsilon,y]).

(4.4)

2) If
g=\begin{pmatrix}a&0\\
0&a^{\ast-1}\end{pmatrix}
,then
\nu(1,g)=1
,
\nu(-1,g)=(\det a,-1)_{F}
.

\displaystyle\Pi_{\psi}(g)f([1,y])=\pi_{\psi}(g)f_{1}(y)=|\det(a)|^{1/2}f_{1}(ya)=|\det(a)|^{1/2}f([1,ya]).

\displaystyle\Pi_{\psi}(g)f([-1,y])

\displaystyle=\Pi_{\psi}([-1,1])\pi_{\psi}[g^{s(-1)},\nu(-1,g)]f_{2}(y)

\displaystyle=|\det(a)|^{1/2}(\det a,-1)_{F}\Pi_{\psi}([-1,1],1)f_{2}(ya)

\displaystyle=|\det(a)|^{1/2}(\det a,-1)_{F}f([-1,ya]).

Hence:

\Pi_{\psi}[\begin{pmatrix}a&0\\
0&a^{\ast-1}\end{pmatrix}]f([\epsilon,y])=|\det(a)|^{1/2}(\det a,\epsilon)_{F}f([\epsilon,ya]).

(4.5)

3) If
g=\omega
, then
\nu(1,g)=1
,
\nu(-1,g)=\gamma(-1,\psi^{\tfrac{1}{2}})^{-m}
. Let
f_{1},f_{2}\in S(X)
.

\displaystyle\Pi_{\psi}(g)f([1,y])=\pi_{\psi}(g)f_{1}(y)=\int_{X^{\ast}}\psi(\langle y,y^{\ast}\rangle)f_{1}(y^{\ast}\omega^{-1})dy^{\ast}.

\displaystyle\Pi_{\psi}(g)f([-1,y])

\displaystyle=\Pi_{\psi}([-1,1])\pi_{\psi}[-g,\nu(-1,g)]f_{2}(y)

\displaystyle=\Pi_{\psi}([-1,1])\nu(-1,g)\int_{X^{\ast}}\psi(\langle-y,y^{\ast}\rangle)f_{2}(y^{\ast}\omega^{-1})dy^{\ast}

\displaystyle=\nu(-1,g)\int_{X^{\ast}}\psi(\langle-y,y^{\ast}\rangle)f([-1,y^{\ast}\omega^{-1}])dy^{\ast}.

Hence:

\Pi_{\psi}(\omega)f([\epsilon,y])=\nu(\epsilon,g)\int_{X^{\ast}}\psi(\langle\epsilon y,y^{\ast}\rangle)f([\epsilon,y^{\ast}\omega^{-1}])dy^{\ast}.

(4.6)

By
[
We
]
, the above actions determine this representation.

4.3.
The cocycle

Let
h_{i}=[\epsilon_{i},g_{i}]\in\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}(W)
,
f\in L^{2}(\mu_{2}\times X)
with
f|_{X}=f_{1}
,
f|_{[-1\times X]}=\Pi_{\psi}([-1,1])f_{2}
, for
f_{1},f_{2}\in L^{2}(X)
. Then:

\displaystyle\Pi_{\psi}(h_{1})\Pi_{\psi}(h_{2})f_{1}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{\psi}([\epsilon_{1},1])\Pi_{\psi}([1,g_{1}])\Pi_{\psi}([\epsilon_{2},1])\Pi_{\psi}([1,g_{2}])f_{1}

(4.7)

\displaystyle=\Pi_{\psi}([\epsilon_{1},1])\Pi_{\psi}([1,g_{1}])\Pi_{\psi}([\epsilon_{2},1])\pi_{\psi}(g_{2})f_{1}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{\psi}([\epsilon_{1},1])\Pi_{\psi}([\epsilon_{2},g_{1}^{s(\epsilon_{2})}])\nu(\epsilon_{2},g_{1})\pi_{\psi}(g_{2})f_{1}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{\psi}([\epsilon_{1},1])\Pi_{\psi}([\epsilon_{2},1])\Pi_{\psi}([1,g_{1}^{s(\epsilon_{2})}])\nu(\epsilon_{2},g_{1})\pi_{\psi}(g_{2})f_{1}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{\psi}([\epsilon_{1},1])\Pi_{\psi}([\epsilon_{2},1])\pi_{\psi}(g_{1}^{s(\epsilon_{2})})\nu(\epsilon_{2},g_{1})\pi_{\psi}(g_{2})f_{1}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{\psi}([\epsilon_{1}\epsilon_{2},1])\nu(\epsilon_{2},g_{1})\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1}^{s(\epsilon_{2})},g_{2})\pi_{\psi}(g_{1}^{s(\epsilon_{2})}g_{2})f_{1}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}([\epsilon_{1},g_{1}],[\epsilon_{2},g_{2}])\Pi_{\psi}([\epsilon_{1}\epsilon_{2},g_{1}^{s(\epsilon_{2})}g_{2}])f_{1}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}(h_{1},h_{2})\Pi_{\psi}(h_{1}h_{2})f_{1}.

\displaystyle\Pi_{\psi}(h_{1})\Pi_{\psi}(h_{2})\Pi_{\psi}([-1,1])f_{2}

(4.8)

\displaystyle=\Pi_{\psi}([\epsilon_{1},1])\Pi_{\psi}([1,g_{1}])\Pi_{\psi}([\epsilon_{2},1])\Pi_{\psi}([1,g_{2}])\Pi_{\psi}([-1,1])f_{2}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{\psi}([\epsilon_{1},1])\Pi_{\psi}([1,g_{1}])\Pi_{\psi}([\epsilon_{2},1])\Pi_{\psi}([-1,g_{2}^{s(-1)}])\nu(-1,g_{2})f_{2}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{\psi}([\epsilon_{1},1])\Pi_{\psi}([1,g_{1}])\Pi_{\psi}([\epsilon_{2},1])\Pi_{\psi}([-1,1])\pi_{\psi}(g_{2}^{s(-1)})\nu(-1,g_{2})f_{2}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{\psi}([\epsilon_{1},1])\Pi_{\psi}([\epsilon_{2},g_{1}^{s(\epsilon_{2})}])\nu(\epsilon_{2},g_{1})\Pi_{\psi}([-1,1])\nu(-1,g_{2})\pi_{\psi}(g_{2}^{s(-1)})f_{2}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{\psi}([\epsilon_{1},1])\Pi_{\psi}([\epsilon_{2},1])\nu(\epsilon_{2},g_{1})\Pi_{\psi}([1,g_{1}^{s(\epsilon_{2})}])\Pi_{\psi}([-1,1])\nu(-1,g_{2})\pi_{\psi}(g_{2}^{s(-1)})f_{2}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{\psi}([\epsilon_{1}\epsilon_{2},1])\nu(\epsilon_{2},g_{1})\Pi_{\psi}([-1,g_{1}^{s(-\epsilon_{2})}])\nu(-1,g_{1}^{s(\epsilon_{2})})\nu(-1,g_{2})\pi_{\psi}(g_{2}^{s(-1)})f_{2}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{\psi}([\epsilon_{1}\epsilon_{2},1])\nu(\epsilon_{2},g_{1})\Pi_{\psi}([-1,1])\pi_{\psi}(g_{1}^{s(-\epsilon_{2})})\nu(-1,g_{1}^{s(\epsilon_{2})})\nu(-1,g_{2})\pi_{\psi}(g_{2}^{s(-1)})f_{2}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{\psi}([\epsilon_{1}\epsilon_{2},1])\nu(\epsilon_{2},g_{1})\nu(-1,g_{1}^{s(\epsilon_{2})})\nu(-1,g_{2})\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1}^{s(-\epsilon_{2})},g_{2}^{s(-1)})\Pi_{\psi}([-1,1])\pi_{\psi}(g_{1}^{s(-\epsilon_{2})}g_{2}^{s(-1)})f_{2}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{\psi}([\epsilon_{1}\epsilon_{2},1])\nu(\epsilon_{2},g_{1})\nu(-1,g_{1}^{s(\epsilon_{2})})\nu(-1,g_{2})\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1}^{s(-\epsilon_{2})},g_{2}^{s(-1)})\nu(-1,g_{1}^{s(\epsilon_{2})}g_{2})^{-1}\pi_{\psi}(g_{1}^{s(\epsilon_{2})}g_{2})\Pi_{\psi}([-1,1])f_{2}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{\psi}([\epsilon_{1}\epsilon_{2},1])\nu(\epsilon_{2},g_{1})\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1}^{s(\epsilon_{2})},g_{2})\pi_{\psi}(g_{1}^{s(\epsilon_{2})}g_{2})\Pi_{\psi}([-1,1])f_{2}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}([\epsilon_{1},g_{1}],[\epsilon_{2},g_{2}])\Pi_{\psi}([\epsilon_{1}\epsilon_{2},g_{1}^{s(\epsilon_{2})}g_{2}])\Pi_{\psi}([-1,1])f_{2}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}(h_{1},h_{2})\Pi_{\psi}(h_{1}h_{2})\Pi_{\psi}([-1,1])f_{2}.

By (
4.7
), (
4.8
), for
f\in L^{2}(\mu_{2}\times X)
, we have:

\Pi_{\psi}(h_{1})\Pi_{\psi}(h_{2})f=\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}(h_{1},h_{2})\Pi_{\psi}(h_{1}h_{2})f.

(4.9)

4.4.
Lattice model

Retain notations from Section
2.6
. Then
\Pi_{\psi}=\operatorname{Ind}_{\operatorname{H}(L)}^{\operatorname{H}^{\pm}(W)}\psi_{L}
.
For example, if
\psi=\psi_{0}
, we take
L=\mathbb{Z}e_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathbb{Z}e_{m}\oplus\mathbb{Z}e_{1}^{\ast}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathbb{Z}e_{m}^{\ast}
. Let
\mathcal{H}_{\psi}^{\pm}(L)
be the set of measurable functions
f:\mu_{2}\times W\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
such that

(i)

f(\epsilon,l+w)=\psi(-\tfrac{\langle x_{l},x^{\ast}_{l}\rangle}{2}-\epsilon\tfrac{\langle l,w\rangle}{2})f(\epsilon,w)
, for
l=x_{l}+x_{l}^{\ast}\in L=(X\cap L)\oplus(X^{\ast}\cap L)
,
w\in W
,

(ii)

\int_{L\setminus W}||f(\epsilon,w)||^{2}dw<+\infty
,

where
w\in W
,
\epsilon\in\mu_{2}
, and
dw
is a
W
-right invariant measure on
L\setminus W
. Then
\Pi_{\psi}
can be realized on
\mathcal{H}_{\psi}^{\pm}(L)
by the following formulas:

\Pi_{\psi}([w^{\prime},t])f(\epsilon,w)=\psi(t+\epsilon\tfrac{\langle w,w^{\prime}\rangle}{2})f(\epsilon,w+w^{\prime}),

(4.10)

\Pi_{\psi}[-1,0]f(\epsilon,x+x^{\ast})=f(-\epsilon,x-x^{\ast}),

(4.11)

for
w=x+x^{\ast},w^{\prime}=x^{\prime}+x^{{}^{\prime}\ast}\in W
,
\epsilon\in\mu_{2}
,
t\in F
.

5.
Splitting of dual pairs in
\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}(W)

5.1.
Notations

Following
[
MVW
]
and
[
Ku1
]
, let
F=\mathbb{R}
,
E=\mathbb{C}
. Let
\mathbb{H}
denote the unique(up to isometry) quaternion algebra over
F
, with a basis
\{1,i,j,k\}
such that
ij=-ji=k
,
i^{2}=j^{2}=k^{2}=-1
. Assume
E=F(i)
. Let
D
be a division algebra over
F
with an involution
\tau
such that
F
consists of all
\tau
-fixed points of
D
. According to
[
MVW
]
and
[
Ku1
]
, it has one of the following forms, up to isometry:

•

D=F,\tau=\operatorname{Id}
;

•

D=E
,
\tau
=the complex conjugation;

•

D=\mathbb{H}
,
\tau=
the quaternion conjugation.

Assume that
(W,\langle,\rangle)
has the following tensor product decomposition:

W=\mathcal{W}_{1}\otimes_{D}\mathcal{W}_{2},\qquad\langle,\rangle=\kappa_{D}\operatorname{Trd}_{D/F}\big(\langle,\rangle_{1}\otimes\tau(\langle,\rangle_{2})\big)

for a right
\epsilon_{1}
-hermitian space
(\mathcal{W}_{1},\langle,\rangle_{1})
over
D
, a left
\epsilon_{2}
-hermitian space
(\mathcal{W}_{2},\langle,\rangle_{2})
over
D
with
\epsilon_{1}\epsilon_{2}=-1
, and
\kappa_{D}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}1&\textrm{ if }D=F,\\
\tfrac{1}{2}&\textrm{ if }D=E,\mathbb{H}.\end{array}\right.
It has the following different cases that correspond to the above forms:

(1)

[Symplectic-orthogonal type]
D=F
,
\mathcal{W}_{1}
(resp.
\mathcal{W}_{2}
) is a symplectic(resp. a symmetric) vector space over
F
and vice versa.

(2)

[Unitary type]
D=E
,
\mathcal{W}_{1}
(resp.
\mathcal{W}_{2}
) is a hermitian (resp. skew hermitian ) vector space over
E
and vice versa.

(3)

[Quaternion unitary type]
D=\mathbb{H}
,
\mathcal{W}_{1}
(resp.
\mathcal{W}_{2}
) is a hermitian (resp. skew hermitian ) vector space over
D
and vice versa.

Let
\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
(resp.
\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
) denote the group of isometries of
(\mathcal{W}_{1},\langle,\rangle)
(resp.
(\mathcal{W}_{2},\langle,\rangle)
), and
\operatorname{GU}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
(resp.
\operatorname{GU}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
) the group of similitude isometries of
(\mathcal{W}_{1},\langle,\rangle)
(resp.
(\mathcal{W}_{2},\langle,\rangle)
). By
[
MVW
, p.15]
, except one case in the above (3) where the skew hermitain vector space has dimension
1
, the pair
\big(\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{1}),\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{2})\big)
is an irreducible
dual reductive pair
of type I in the sense of Howe.
Let

1\longrightarrow T\longrightarrow\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}}(W)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Sp}(W)\longrightarrow 1

be a Metaplectic central topological extension of
\operatorname{Sp}(W)
by
T
associated to
\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}
. We now let
\widehat{\operatorname{U}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
,
\widehat{\operatorname{U}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
be the preimages of
\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
,
\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
in
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}}(W)
respectively. To state a result about the irreducible dual reductive pair from
[
MVW
]
, let us list separately a case:

(1*)

[Exception case] In the above symplectic-orthogonal type, the orthogonal vector space has odd dimension.

Theorem 5.1
.

Let
(\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{1}),\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{2}))
be an irreducible reductive dual pair in
\operatorname{Sp}(W)
as given above.

(1)

If
(\mathcal{W}_{1},\mathcal{W}_{2})
is not the exception case (1*), then the exact sequence
1\longrightarrow T\longrightarrow\widehat{\operatorname{U}}(\mathcal{W}_{i})\longrightarrow\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{i})\longrightarrow 1
splits, for
i=1,2
.

(2)

If
(\mathcal{W}_{1},\mathcal{W}_{2})
is the exception case and
\epsilon_{1}=-1
,
\epsilon_{2}=1
, then

–

the exact sequence
1\longrightarrow T\longrightarrow\widehat{\operatorname{O}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})\longrightarrow\operatorname{O}(\mathcal{W}_{2})\longrightarrow 1
splits, but

–

the exact sequence
1\longrightarrow T\longrightarrow\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})\longrightarrow\operatorname{Sp}(\mathcal{W}_{1})\longrightarrow 1
does not split.

The
p
-adic version of this theorem has been proved in
[
MVW
, Chapter 1 ]
and
[
Ku1
]
. For the real field case, it was proved in
[
Ku1
, Prop.4.1]
. Moreover, explicit trivialization is also given in
[
Ku1
]
.

Theorem 5.2
.

Let
(\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{1}),\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{2}))
be an irreducible reductive dual pair in
\operatorname{Sp}(W)
as given above. Then
\widehat{\operatorname{U}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
commutates with
\widehat{\operatorname{U}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
in
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}}(W)
.

The
p
-adic version of this theorem has been proved in
[
MVW
, Chapter 1 ]
. For the real field case, one can see
[
Ho1
,
Ho2
]
. Recall

\iota:\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{1})\times\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{2})\longrightarrow\operatorname{Sp}(W).

The kernel of this map is trivial or
\langle\pm I\rangle
. By Theorem
5.2
, there exists a group homomorphism:

\displaystyle\widehat{\iota}:

\displaystyle\widehat{\operatorname{U}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})\times\widehat{\operatorname{U}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})\longrightarrow\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}}(W)

(5.1)

\displaystyle(\widehat{u}_{1},\widehat{u}_{2})\longmapsto\widehat{u}_{1}\widehat{u}_{2}.

(5.2)

The image of this map belongs to
\widehat{\iota(\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{1})\times\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{2}))}
. If
(\mathcal{W}_{1},\mathcal{W}_{2})
is a pair in the above theorem
5.1
(1), then there exists a composite group homomorphism:

\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{1})\times U(\mathcal{W}_{2})\longrightarrow\widehat{\operatorname{U}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})\times\widehat{\operatorname{U}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})\longrightarrow\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}}(W).

Since the restriction of
\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}
on
\ker(\iota)\times\operatorname{Sp}(W)
or
\operatorname{Sp}(W)\times\ker(\iota)
is trivial, the composite map is trivial on
\ker(\iota)
. Hence
\widehat{\iota(\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{1})\times\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{2}))}
is a splitting group. For simplicity, we can say that the exact sequence

1\longrightarrow T\longrightarrow\widehat{\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{1})\times\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{2})}\longrightarrow\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{1})\times\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{2})\longrightarrow 1

is splitting.
In the following, we will extend these results to the dual pairs in
\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}(W)
.

5.1.1.
The group
\operatorname{U}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{i})

Let
\operatorname{U}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{i})=\{g\in\operatorname{GU}(\mathcal{W}_{i})\mid\lambda_{g}=\pm 1\}
.

Lemma 5.3
.

If
\mathcal{W}_{i}
is a hyperbolic space over
D
of even dimension, then there exists an exact sequence:

1\longrightarrow\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{i})\longrightarrow\operatorname{U}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{i})\longrightarrow\mu_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lambda}}{{\longrightarrow}}1
.

Proof.

Assume
i=2
. Assume that
\mathcal{W}_{2}\simeq n_{2}H
, for a hyperbolic plane
H
over
D
. Let
H=Y\oplus Y^{\ast}
be a complete polarisation. Then we can define a section map

s:\mu_{2}\longrightarrow\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{2});a\longmapsto\underbrace{h_{a}\times\cdots\times h_{a}}_{n_{2}}

where
h_{a}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccccc}1&0\\
0&a\end{array}\right)\in\operatorname{GU}(H)
.
∎

Let
\Lambda_{\operatorname{U}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{i})}
be the image of
\operatorname{U}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{i})
in
\mu_{2}
under the map
\lambda
. Assume that
\mathcal{W}_{i}=\mathcal{W}_{i}^{0}\oplus\mathcal{W}_{i}^{1}
and
\mathcal{W}_{i}^{0}\bot\mathcal{W}_{i}^{1}
, for an anisotropic vector space
\mathcal{W}_{i}^{0}
and a hyperbolic subspace
\mathcal{W}_{i}^{1}\simeq n_{i}H
.

Lemma 5.4
.

\Lambda_{\operatorname{U}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{i})}=\Lambda_{\operatorname{U}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}^{0}_{i})}
.

Proof.

Assume
\mathcal{W}_{i}
is a right vector space over
D
. For
g\in\operatorname{U}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{i})
, the action of
g
on
\mathcal{W}_{i}
will yield another Witt decomposition:
\mathcal{W}_{i}=g\mathcal{W}_{i}^{0}\oplus g\mathcal{W}_{i}^{1}
. By Witt’s Theorem,
g\cdot\mathcal{W}_{i}^{0}=g_{0}\cdot\mathcal{W}_{i}^{0}
for some suitable
g_{0}\in\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{i})
. Moreover,
g_{0}^{-1}g\cdot(\mathcal{W}_{i}^{0})=\mathcal{W}_{i}^{0}
. So
g_{0}^{-1}g\in\operatorname{GU}(\mathcal{W}_{i}^{0})
, and
\lambda(g_{0}^{-1}g)=\lambda(g)
. This shows that
\Lambda_{\operatorname{U}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{i})}\subseteq\Lambda_{\operatorname{U}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}^{0}_{i})}
. On the other hand, by the above lemma,
\Lambda_{\operatorname{U}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{i}^{1})}=\{\pm 1\}
.
Hence, for
h_{0}\in\operatorname{U}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}^{0}_{i})
with
\lambda=\lambda(h_{0})\in F^{\times}
, we can find an element
g_{H}\in\operatorname{U}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{i}^{1})
satisfying
\lambda(g_{H})=\lambda
. Then
g:=h_{0}\times g_{H}
, viewed as an element of
\operatorname{U}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{i})
, satisfies
\lambda(g)=\lambda(h_{0})
. Hence
\Lambda_{\operatorname{U}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{i}^{0})}\subseteq\Lambda_{\operatorname{U}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{i})}
.
∎

By this lemma, we can determine the image of
\lambda
in
\mu_{2}
by means of the characteristic of the anisotropic subspace of
\mathcal{W}_{i}
. The following result is from
[
MVW
, p.7]
.

Lemma 5.5
.

Up to isometry:

-

an anisotropic quadratic vector space over
F
has the following form:
nF(1)
, and
-nF(1)
, for
1\leq n\leq 4
, where
F(1)
is a quadratic vector space over
F
of dimension
1
with the form
x\longrightarrow x^{2}
;

-

an anisotropic hermitian vector space over
E
has the following form:
nE
, and
-nE
, for
1\leq n\leq 2
, where the hermitian form on
E
is given by
(x,y)\longmapsto\tau(x)y
, for
x,y\in E
;

-

an anisotropic right hermitian vector space over
\mathbb{H}
has the following form:
\mathbb{H}
, with the form
(x,y)\longmapsto\tau(x)y
.

Suppose that
(V,\langle,\rangle)
is an anisotropic right hermitian space over
E
. Then we can take an element
i
of
E^{\times}
such that
\overline{i}/{i}=-1
. As it is known that multiplication of
\langle,\rangle
by
i

will give a skew right hermitian form
i\langle,\rangle
on
V
. In analogy with Lemma
5.5
, we have:

Lemma 5.6
.

Up to isometry:

-

an anisotropic right skew hermitian vector space over
E
has the following form:
nE
, and
-nE
, for
1\leq n\leq 2
, where the skew hermitian form on
E
is given by
(x,y)\longmapsto\tau(x)iy
, for
x,y\in E
;

-

an anisotropic right skew hermitian vector space over
\mathbb{H}
has the following form:
\mathbb{H}
, with the form
(x,y)\longmapsto\tau(x)iy
.

Lemma 5.7
.

\Lambda_{\operatorname{U}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{i})}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\mu_{2}&\textrm{ if }\mathcal{W}_{i}\textrm{ is a hyperbolic space},\\
1&\textrm{ otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.4
, it reduces to an anisotropic subspace
\mathcal{W}_{i}^{0}
of
\mathcal{W}_{i}
. If
\mathcal{W}_{i}^{0}\neq 0
, by Lemmas
5.5
,
5.6
, case by case, we can obtain that
\Lambda_{\operatorname{U}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{i}^{0})}=1
.
∎

Let
\mathcal{W}_{i}\simeq nH
be a hyperbolic group. Let
\{f_{1},\cdots,f_{n};f_{1}^{\ast},\cdots,f_{n}^{\ast}\}
be a split hyperbolic basis of
\mathcal{W}_{i}
so that
\langle f_{k},f_{l}\rangle=0=\langle f_{k}^{\ast},f_{l}^{\ast}\rangle
and
\langle f_{k},f_{l}^{\ast}\rangle=\delta_{kl}
. Then we can embed
H\hookrightarrow\mathcal{W}_{i}
by the subbase
\{e_{n},e_{n}^{\ast}\}
. Let
\mathfrak{A}=\{\begin{pmatrix}a&0\\
0&\overline{a}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\mid a\in D^{\times}\}
.

Lemma 5.8
.

There exists a group homomorphism:

\tfrac{\operatorname{U}(H)}{[U(H),U(H)]}\longrightarrow\tfrac{\operatorname{U}(nH)}{[U(nH),U(nH)]}.

Proof.

Let
E_{nH}(D)
denote the elementary linear subgroup of
\operatorname{U}(nH)
. By
[
HaMeTi
, p.28, p.230 ]
, we have:

•

E_{nH}(D)=[E_{nH}(D),E_{nH}(D)]
;

•

\operatorname{U}(nH)=\operatorname{U}(H)E_{nH}(D)
.

Hence
[\operatorname{U}(nH),\operatorname{U}(nH)]=[\operatorname{U}(H),\operatorname{U}(H)][\operatorname{U}(nH),E_{nH}(D)]\supseteq[\operatorname{U}(H),\operatorname{U}(H)]E_{nH}(D)
. So the result holds.
∎

Lemma 5.9
.

If
D=E
or
\mathbb{H}
, or
D=F
and
\epsilon=-1
, there exists a surjective homomorphism:

\tfrac{D^{\times}}{[D^{\times},D^{\times}]}\longrightarrow\tfrac{\operatorname{U}(H)}{[U(H),U(H)]}.

Proof.

Straightforward.
∎

Lemma 5.10
.

If
D=F
and
\epsilon=1
,
\operatorname{U}(nH)=\operatorname{O}(nH)
. Then we have:

•

There exists a surjective group homomorphism:

F^{\times}\simeq\tfrac{\operatorname{SO}(H)}{[\operatorname{SO}(H),\operatorname{SO}(H)]}\longrightarrow\tfrac{\operatorname{SO}(nH)}{[\operatorname{SO}(nH),\operatorname{SO}(nH)]}.

•

[\operatorname{O}(H),\operatorname{O}(H)]=\{\begin{pmatrix}a^{2}&0\\
0&a^{-2}\end{pmatrix}\mid a\in F^{\times}\}
.

•

\tfrac{\operatorname{O}(H)}{[\operatorname{O}(H),\operatorname{O}(H)]}\simeq(F^{\times}/F^{\times 2})\times\langle\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\rangle
.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.8
,
\operatorname{SO}(nH)=[\operatorname{SO}(nH)\cap\operatorname{O}(H)]E_{nH}(F)
. Hence there exists a group homomorphism:

\tfrac{\operatorname{SO}(H)}{[\operatorname{SO}(H),\operatorname{SO}(H)]}\longrightarrow\tfrac{\operatorname{SO}(nH)}{[\operatorname{SO}(nH),\operatorname{SO}(nH)]}.

Note:

\operatorname{O}(H)=\{\begin{pmatrix}a&0\\
0&a^{-1}\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}0&a\\
a^{-1}&0\end{pmatrix}\mid a\in F^{\times}\},\quad\quad\operatorname{SO}(H)=\{\begin{pmatrix}a&0\\
0&a^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\mid a\in F^{\times}\}.

By direct calculation, we can see that the results are all correct.
∎

5.2.
The splitting of
\widehat{\operatorname{U}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{i})

Proposition 5.11
.

Let
(\mathcal{W}_{1},\mathcal{W}_{2})
be a pair in Theorem
5.1
.

(1)

If
(\mathcal{W}_{1},\mathcal{W}_{2})
is not the exception case (1*), then the exact sequence
1\longrightarrow T\longrightarrow\widehat{\operatorname{U}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{i})\longrightarrow\operatorname{U}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{i})\longrightarrow 1
splits, for
i=1,2
.

(2)

If
(\mathcal{W}_{1},\mathcal{W}_{2})
is the exception case and
\epsilon_{1}=-1
,
\epsilon_{2}=1
, then

(i)

the exact sequence
1\longrightarrow T\longrightarrow\widehat{\operatorname{O}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})\longrightarrow\operatorname{O}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{2})\longrightarrow 1
splits, but

(ii)

the exact sequence
1\longrightarrow T\longrightarrow\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})\longrightarrow\operatorname{Sp}(\mathcal{W}_{1})\longrightarrow 1
does not split.

5.2.1.
The proof of Part (1)

According to Lemma
5.6
, if
\mathcal{W}_{i}
is not a hyperbolic space,
\operatorname{U}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{i})=\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{i})
, so the result holds. Assume now that
\mathcal{W}_{i}\simeq n_{i}H
, for a hyperbolic plane
H
over
D
. Let
\mathcal{W}_{i}=Y\oplus Y^{\ast}
be a complete polarisation.

By almost symmetry, we assume
i=1
. Assume
\dim\mathcal{W}_{2}=n_{2}
. Then
W=\mathcal{W}_{1}\otimes_{D}W_{2}\simeq(Y\otimes\mathcal{W}_{2})\oplus(Y^{\ast}\otimes\mathcal{W}_{2})
is a complete polarisation. Assume that
X=Y\otimes\mathcal{W}_{2}
,
X^{\ast}=Y^{\ast}\otimes\mathcal{W}_{2}
. There exists an exact sequence:

1\to\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{1})\to\operatorname{U}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{1})\to\mu_{2}\to 1.

Now let
\operatorname{H}^{2}(\operatorname{U}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{1}),T)_{1}
denote the kernel of the restriction from
\operatorname{H}^{2}(\operatorname{U}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{1}),T)
to
\operatorname{H}^{2}(\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{1}),T)
.
By Hochschild-Serre spectral sequence, there exists the following long exact sequence of six terms:

0\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hom}(\mu_{2},T)\to\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{U}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{1}),T)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{1}),T)^{\operatorname{U}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{1})}\longrightarrow\operatorname{H}^{2}(\mu_{2},T)

\longrightarrow\operatorname{H}^{2}(\operatorname{U}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{1}),T)_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{H}^{1}\big(\mu_{2},\operatorname{H}^{1}(\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{1}),T)\big)

Since the restriction of
[\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}]
to
\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
is trivial, there exists a function
f:\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{1})\longrightarrow T
such that

\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})=f(g_{1}g_{2})f(g_{1})^{-1}f(g_{2})^{-1},\qquad\quad g_{i}\in\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{1}).

Let
f
extend to be a Borel function of
\operatorname{U}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
by taking the trivial value outside
\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
. We replace
\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}
with
\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}^{\prime}=\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}\circ\delta_{1}f
. Then:

\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}^{\prime}(h_{1},h_{2})=\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}(h_{1},h_{2})f(h_{1})f(h_{2})f(h_{1}h_{2})^{-1},\quad\quad h_{1},h_{2}\in\operatorname{U}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{1}).

Recall the section map
s_{1}:\mu_{2}\longrightarrow\operatorname{U}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{1});\epsilon\longmapsto\begin{pmatrix}I&0\\
0&\epsilon I\end{pmatrix}
, and
s_{1}(\epsilon)\otimes I_{2}=s(\epsilon)
. By abuse of notations, we will omit
I_{2}
and don’t distinguish
s_{1}
from
s
. Then for
g\in\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
, we have:

\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}^{\prime}(s_{1}(1),g)=\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}(s_{1}(1),g)=\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}([1,1],[1,g])=1;

\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}^{\prime}(s_{1}(-1),g)=\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}(s_{1}(-1),g)f(g)=\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}([-1,1],[1,g])f(g)=\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(1,g)f(g)=f(g).

For any
h\in\operatorname{U}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
, let us write
h=s_{1}(\epsilon_{h})g_{h}
, for
\epsilon_{h}\in\mu_{2}
,
g_{h}\in\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
. Define a Borel function
\varkappa
of
\operatorname{U}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
as

\varkappa(h)=\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}^{\prime}(s_{1}(\epsilon_{h}),g_{h})
. Let us define

\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}^{\prime\prime}=\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}^{\prime}\circ\delta_{1}(\varkappa).

So the map
p
is just given by

p([\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}]):\epsilon\longmapsto(g\longrightarrow\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}^{\prime\prime}(g,s(\epsilon))),\quad g\in\operatorname{U}(W_{\nu}),\epsilon\in\mu_{2}.

Note that
p([\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}])(\epsilon)
is a character of
\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
, which factors through
\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{1})\longrightarrow\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{1})/[\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{1}),\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{1})]
. According to Lemma
5.8
, if
(D,\epsilon_{1})\neq(F,1)
,
p([\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}])(\epsilon)
depends on the values on
D^{\times}
. Let
g=\operatorname{diag}(1,\cdots,1,a;1,\cdots,1,\overline{a}^{-1})
, for
a\in D^{\times}
. Then:

\displaystyle\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}^{\prime\prime}(g,s(-1))

\displaystyle=\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}^{\prime}(g,s(-1))\varkappa(g)\varkappa(s(-1))\varkappa(gs(-1))^{-1}

(5.3)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}^{\prime}(g,s(-1))\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}^{\prime}(s(1),g)\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}^{\prime}(s(-1),1)\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}^{\prime}(s(-1),s(-1)^{-1}gs(-1))^{-1}

(5.4)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}(g,s(-1))f(g)f(g^{s(-1)})^{-1}

(5.5)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g,s(-1))\nu(-1,g)f(g)f(g^{s(-1)})^{-1}

(5.6)

\displaystyle=(-1,\det a)_{F}f(g)f(g^{s(-1)})^{-1}.

(5.7)

5.3.

If
D=E
, or
D=\mathbb{H}
, we know that
\det a=\operatorname{Nrd}(a)\in F_{+}^{\times}
, so
(-1,\det a)_{F}=1
. Since
\operatorname{H}^{2}(\mu_{2},T)=0
and
p([\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}])=0
, the restriction of
[\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}]
on
\operatorname{U}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
is trivial.

5.4.

If
D=F
,
\mathcal{W}_{1}
is symplectic and
\mathcal{W}_{2}
is orthogonal. Since
\dim\mathcal{W}_{2}
is even,
\det a=a^{2k}\in F_{+}^{\times}
, so
(-1,\det a)_{F}=1
.

5.5.

If
D=F
,
\epsilon_{1}=1
,
\mathcal{W}_{1}
is orthogonal and
\mathcal{W}_{2}
is symplectic. Let
\mathcal{A}_{i}=(f_{i1},\cdots,f_{in_{i}};f_{i1}^{\ast},\cdots,f_{in_{i}}^{\ast})
be a split hyperbolic basis of
\mathcal{W}_{i}
. Let us write
X_{1}=(f_{11},\cdots,f_{1n_{1}})
,
X_{1}^{\ast}=(f_{11}^{\ast},\cdots,f_{1n_{1}}^{\ast})
. Then

\{X_{1}\otimes f_{21},X_{1}^{\ast}\otimes f_{21},\cdots,X_{1}\otimes f_{2n_{2}},X^{\ast}_{1}\otimes f_{2n_{2}};X^{\ast}_{1}\otimes f^{\ast}_{21},X_{1}\otimes f^{\ast}_{21},\cdots,X^{\ast}_{1}\otimes f^{\ast}_{2n_{2}},X_{1}\otimes f^{\ast}_{2n_{2}}\}

forms a symplectic basis of
W
. Recall:

s:\operatorname{O}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{1})\times\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{2})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{1}\otimes s_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{1}\otimes_{F}\mathcal{W}_{2})=\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}(W).

Hence

s_{1}(g)\otimes I_{\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{2})}=\begin{pmatrix}g\otimes I_{2}&0\\
0&(g^{\ast})^{-1}\otimes I_{2}\end{pmatrix},\quad\quad g\in\operatorname{O}(\mathcal{W}_{1});

s_{1}(\epsilon)\otimes I_{\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{2})}=\operatorname{diag}\Bigg(\begin{pmatrix}I_{1}&0\\
0&\epsilon I_{1}\end{pmatrix},\cdots,\begin{pmatrix}I_{1}&0\\
0&\epsilon I_{1}\end{pmatrix};\begin{pmatrix}\epsilon I_{2}&0\\
0&I_{2}\end{pmatrix},\cdots,\begin{pmatrix}\epsilon I_{2}&0\\
0&I_{2}\end{pmatrix}\Bigg),\quad\epsilon\in\mu_{2}.

Let us write
s_{1}(\epsilon)\otimes I_{\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{2})}=s(\epsilon)g({\epsilon})
, for some
g({\epsilon})=\begin{pmatrix}a&0\\
0&a^{-1}\end{pmatrix}
, and
h_{i}=g(\epsilon_{i})[s_{1}(g_{i})\otimes I_{\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{2})}]
. Then:

\displaystyle\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}(s_{1}(\epsilon_{1})s_{1}(g_{1})\otimes I_{\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{2})},s_{1}(\epsilon_{2})s_{1}(g_{2})\otimes I_{\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{2})})

(5.8)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}([\epsilon_{1},h_{1}],[[\epsilon_{2},h_{2}]])=\nu(\epsilon_{2},h_{1})\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(h_{1}^{s(\epsilon_{1})},h_{2})

(5.9)

\displaystyle=\nu(\epsilon_{2},h_{1}).

(5.10)

5.5.1.

If
4\mid\dim\mathcal{W}_{2}
, then
\nu(\epsilon_{2},h_{1})=(\epsilon_{2},(\epsilon_{1}\det g)^{n_{1}})_{F}=1
.

5.5.2.

If
4\nmid\dim\mathcal{W}_{2}
,
\nu(\epsilon_{2},h_{1})=(\epsilon_{2},(\epsilon_{1}\det g)^{n_{1}})_{F}=(\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{1}\det g)_{F}
. Then the restriction of
\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}
on
\operatorname{O}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
factors through
\operatorname{O}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{1})/\operatorname{SO}(\mathcal{W}_{1})\simeq\mu_{2}\times\operatorname{O}(\mathcal{W}_{1})/\operatorname{SO}(\mathcal{W}_{1})\simeq\mu_{2}\times\mu_{2}
. Moreover,

\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}((\epsilon_{1},[\det g_{1}]),(\epsilon_{2},[\det g_{2}]))=(\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{1}[\det g_{1}])_{F}=(\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{1})_{F}(\epsilon_{2},[\det g_{1}])_{F}.

By Remark
3.7
,
(\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{1})_{F}
defines a splitting cocycle on
\mu_{2}
. As

\operatorname{H}^{2}(\mu_{2}\times\mu_{2},T)\simeq\operatorname{H}^{2}(\mu_{2},T)\oplus\operatorname{H}^{2}(\mu_{2},T)\oplus\operatorname{H}^{1}(\mu_{2},H^{1}(\mu_{2},T)),

\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}
is not a splitting cocycle on
\mu_{2}\times\mu_{2}
. The subtle part is to see whether this
2
-cocycle can be splitting on
\operatorname{O}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
.

By Hochschild-Serre spectral sequence, there exists the following long exact sequence:

0\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hom}(\mu_{2}\times\mu_{2},T)\to\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{O}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{1}),T)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{SO}(\mathcal{W}_{1}),T)^{\operatorname{O}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{1})}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle tr}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{H}^{2}(\mu_{2}\times\mu_{2},T)

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle inf_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{H}^{2}(\operatorname{O}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{1}),T)_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{H}^{1}\big(\mu_{2}\times\mu_{2},\operatorname{H}^{1}(\operatorname{SO}(\mathcal{W}_{1}),T))

5.5.2.1

Let us first show that
p_{1}[\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}]
is trivial. Note that
