\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}(s_{1}(g_{1})\otimes I_{\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{2})},s_{1}(g_{2})\otimes I_{\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{2})})=1,\quad\quad g_{i}\in\operatorname{O}(\mathcal{W}_{1}).

For any
h\in\operatorname{O}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
, let us write
h=[\epsilon_{h},g_{h}]
, for some
\epsilon_{h}\in\mu_{2}
,
g_{h}\in\operatorname{O}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
. As

\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}(s_{1}(\epsilon_{h})\otimes I_{\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{2})},s_{1}(g_{h})\otimes I_{\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{2})})=1,

the map
p_{1}
is just given by

p_{1}([\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}]):\epsilon\longmapsto(g\longrightarrow\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}(g\otimes I_{\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{2})},s_{1}(\epsilon)\otimes I_{\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{2})})=1),\quad g\in\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{1}),\epsilon\in\mu_{2}.

5.5.2.2

Let us show that the
2
-cocycle
\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}
does come from
\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{SO}(\mathcal{W}_{1}),T)^{\operatorname{O}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{1})}
by the transgression.
Under the basis
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and its subbase
\{f_{1n_{1}},f_{1n_{1}}^{\ast}\}
, let us write

\mathcal{W}_{1}=n_{1}H,\quad\operatorname{O}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{1})=\operatorname{O}^{\pm}(n_{1}H),\quad\operatorname{SO}(\mathcal{W}_{1})=\operatorname{SO}(n_{1}H).

By Lemma
5.10
,

\operatorname{SO}(n_{1}H)=\operatorname{SO}(H)E_{n_{1}H}(F),\quad\operatorname{O}^{\pm}(n_{1}H)=\operatorname{O}^{\pm}(H)E_{n_{1}H}(F),\quad E_{n_{1}H}(F)=[E_{n_{1}H}(F),E_{n_{1}H}(F)].

Any character of
\operatorname{SO}(n_{1}H)
is trivial on
E_{n_{1}H}(F)
. By the explicit construction of the transgression in
[
NeScWi
, p.65]
, we can assume
n_{1}=1
. In this case, let
\chi\in\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{SO}(H),T)^{\operatorname{O}^{\pm}(H)}
, then
\chi
is trivial or
\chi(\begin{pmatrix}a&0\\
0&a^{-1}\end{pmatrix})=\operatorname{sgn}(a)
. It is clear that if
\chi
is the trivial character,
[tr(\chi)]
can not be
[\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}]
. So let us consider the other character. Note:

\operatorname{O}^{\pm}(H)=\{\begin{pmatrix}\pm a&0\\
0&a^{-1}\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}0&\pm a\\
a^{-1}&0\end{pmatrix}\mid a\in F^{\times}\},

and

\operatorname{O}^{\pm}(H)/\{\begin{pmatrix}a^{2}&0\\
0&a^{-2}\end{pmatrix}\}\simeq\Bigg\{\pm\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},\pm\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix},\pm\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix},\pm\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}\Bigg\}.

Following
[
NeScWi
, p.65]
, let us extend
\chi
to
\operatorname{O}^{\pm}(H)
by defining

\chi(g)=1,\quad\chi(-g)=-1,

for
g\in\mathcal{B}=\Bigg\{\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}\Bigg\}
. Then:

tr(\chi)(g_{1},g_{2})=[\partial\chi](g_{1},g_{2})=\chi(g_{1})\chi(g_{2})\chi(g_{1}g_{2})^{-1}.

Note that
\partial\chi(-g_{1},g_{2})=\partial\chi(g_{1},g_{2})=\partial\chi(g_{1},-g_{2})
. The correspondence between
\mu_{2}\times\mu_{2}
and
\mathcal{B}
is given by

\displaystyle\mu_{2}\times\mu_{2}\longrightarrow\mathcal{B};

(5.11)

\displaystyle[1,1]\to\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}

(5.12)

\displaystyle[-1,1]\to\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}

(5.13)

\displaystyle[1,-1]\to\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}

(5.14)

\displaystyle[-1,-1]\to\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}.

(5.15)

Hence

\partial\chi([\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{1}^{\prime}],[\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{2}^{\prime}])=(\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{1}^{\prime})_{F},

which means that
[\partial\chi]=[\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}]
. Finally we can see that
[\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}]
is also splitting on
\operatorname{O}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
.

5.5.3.
The proof of Part (2)

In this case,
O^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{2})=O(\mathcal{W}_{2})
, and
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
is not splitting. By the long exact sequence of six terms, we know that the two covering over
\operatorname{Sp}(W)
only extends to
\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}(W)
in a unique way. Hence
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})\simeq\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
, which is also not splitting.

5.6.
The bicommutant

We now let
\widehat{\operatorname{U}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
,
\widehat{\operatorname{U}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
be the preimages of
\operatorname{U}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
,
\operatorname{U}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
in
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}}^{\pm}(W)
respectively.

Proposition 5.12
.

Let
(\mathcal{W}_{1},\mathcal{W}_{2})
be a pair in Theorem
5.1
.

(1)

If
D=E
or
\mathbb{H}
,
\widehat{\operatorname{U}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
commutes with
\widehat{\operatorname{U}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
in
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}}^{\pm}(W)
.

(2)

If
D=F
, assume
\epsilon_{1}=-1
,
\epsilon_{2}=1
.

(a)

If
2\mid\dim\mathcal{W}_{2}
, and
\mathcal{W}_{2}
is not a hyperbolic space, then:

(i)

\operatorname{O}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{2})=\operatorname{O}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
.

(ii)

if
4\mid\dim\mathcal{W}_{1}
, then
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
commutes with
\operatorname{O}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
in
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}}^{\pm}(W)
.

(iii)

if
4\nmid\dim\mathcal{W}_{1}
, then
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
only commutes with
\operatorname{SO}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
in
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}}^{\pm}(W)
, and
\operatorname{O}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
only commutes with
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
in
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}}^{\pm}(W)
.

(b)

If
2\mid\dim\mathcal{W}_{2}
, and
\mathcal{W}_{2}
is a hyperbolic space, then:

(i)

\widehat{\operatorname{O}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})\simeq\operatorname{O}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{2})\times T
.

(ii)

if
4\mid\dim\mathcal{W}_{1}
, then
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
commutes with
\widehat{\operatorname{O}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
in
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}}^{\pm}(W)
.

(iii)

if
4\nmid\dim\mathcal{W}_{1}
and
4\mid\dim\mathcal{W}_{2}
, then
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
only commutes with
\widehat{\operatorname{SO}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
in
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}}^{\pm}(W)
, and
\widehat{\operatorname{O}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
only commutes with
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
in
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}}^{\pm}(W)
.

(iv)

if
4\nmid\dim\mathcal{W}_{1}
and
4\nmid\dim\mathcal{W}_{2}
, then
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
only commutes with
\widehat{\operatorname{SO}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
in
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}}^{\pm}(W)
, and
\widehat{\operatorname{O}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
only commutes with
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
in
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}}^{\pm}(W)
.

(c)

If
2\nmid\dim\mathcal{W}_{2}
, then:

(i)

\operatorname{O}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{2})=\operatorname{O}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
.

(ii)

if
4\mid\dim\mathcal{W}_{1}
, then
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
commutes with
\operatorname{O}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
in
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}}^{\pm}(W)
.

(iii)

if
4\nmid\dim\mathcal{W}_{1}
, then
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
only commutes with
\operatorname{SO}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
, and
\operatorname{O}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
only commutes with
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
in
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}}^{\pm}(W)
.

5.7.
The proof of Proposition
5.12

5.7.1.

If neither
\mathcal{W}_{1}
nor
\mathcal{W}_{2}
is a hyperbolic space, then
\operatorname{U}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{i})=\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{i})
. So the result follows from Theorem
5.2
.

5.7.2.

If
\mathcal{W}_{1}\simeq n_{1}H
,
\mathcal{W}_{2}
is not a hyperbolic space, and
\dim_{F}\mathcal{W}_{2}
is even, then
\operatorname{U}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{2})=\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
,
\operatorname{U}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{1})=\operatorname{U}^{\pm}(n_{1}H)
. By Proposition
5.11
,

\widehat{\operatorname{U}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})\simeq\operatorname{U}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{1})\times T,\quad\widehat{\operatorname{U}}(\mathcal{W}_{i})\simeq\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{i})\times T.

By Theorem
5.2
,
\widehat{\operatorname{U}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
is commuted with
\widehat{\operatorname{U}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
. Let
\mathcal{W}_{1}=Y\oplus Y^{\ast}
be a complete polarisation. Assume that

X=Y\otimes_{D}\mathcal{W}_{2}
,
X^{\ast}=Y^{\ast}\otimes_{D}\mathcal{W}_{2}
. There exists a section map:

s:\mu_{2}\longrightarrow\widehat{\operatorname{U}}(\mathcal{W}_{1});\epsilon\longmapsto\begin{pmatrix}I&0\\
0&\epsilon I\end{pmatrix}.

Let
S_{1}=s(\mu_{2})
, and
\mathcal{S}_{1}=s(\mu_{2})\otimes I_{\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{2})}\subseteq\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}(W)
. Then
\widehat{\operatorname{U}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})=\widehat{S}_{2}\widehat{\operatorname{U}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
. So it suffices to show that
\widehat{S}_{1}
is commuted with
\widehat{\operatorname{U}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
. For
g\in\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
,
s(-1)\otimes I_{\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{2})}\in\mathcal{S}_{2}
,

\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}(I_{\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{1})}\otimes g,s(-1)\otimes I_{\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{2})})=\nu((\det g)^{n_{1}},-1)\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(I_{\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{1})}\otimes g,I_{\operatorname{Sp}(W)})=((\det g)^{n_{1}},-1)_{F};

\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}(s(-1)\otimes I_{\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{2})},I_{\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{1})}\otimes g)=\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(I_{\operatorname{Sp}(W)},I_{\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{1})}\otimes g)=1.

a) If
D=E
or
\mathbb{H}
,
\det g
is a positive number, so
((\det g)^{n_{1}},-1)_{F}=1
and
\widehat{S}_{1}
commutes with
\widehat{\operatorname{U}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
.

b) If
D=F
, and
\mathcal{W}_{1}
is symplectic with
2\mid{n_{1}}
, then
((\det g)^{n_{1}},-1)_{F}=1
and
\widehat{S}_{1}
commutes with
\widehat{\operatorname{U}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
.

c) If
D=F
, and
\mathcal{W}_{1}
is symplectic with
2\nmid{n_{1}}
, then
((\det g)^{n_{1}},-1)_{F}=1
implies that
\det g>0
. So
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
only commutes with
\widehat{\operatorname{SO}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
. Consequently,
\widehat{\operatorname{O}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
only commutes with
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
.

5.7.3.

If
\mathcal{W}_{1}\simeq nH
,
\mathcal{W}_{2}
is not a hyperbolic space, and
\dim_{F}\mathcal{W}_{2}
is odd. In this case,
\mathcal{W}_{1}
is symplectic and
\mathcal{W}_{2}
is orthogonal. Then
\operatorname{U}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{1})\simeq\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
,
\operatorname{U}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{2})\simeq\operatorname{O}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
, and
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})\simeq\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
,
\widehat{\operatorname{O}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})\simeq\operatorname{O}(\mathcal{W}_{2})\times T
. Similarly as above, we have:

•

If
2\mid{n_{1}}
,
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
commutes with
\widehat{\operatorname{O}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
or
\operatorname{O}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
.

•

If
2\nmid{n_{1}}
,
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
only commutes with
\widehat{\operatorname{SO}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
or
\operatorname{SO}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
, and
\widehat{\operatorname{O}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
only commutes with
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
.

5.7.4.

If
\mathcal{W}_{2}\simeq n_{2}H
,
\mathcal{W}_{1}
is not a hyperbolic space, and
\dim_{F}\mathcal{W}_{1}
is even, the proof is similar as above.

5.7.5.

If
\mathcal{W}_{1}
and
\mathcal{W}_{2}
both are hyperbolic spaces, then
\operatorname{U}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{i})\neq\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{i})
and
\widehat{\operatorname{U}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{i})\simeq\operatorname{U}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{i})\times T
. Let
\mathcal{A}_{i}=(f_{i1},\cdots,f_{in_{i}};f_{i1}^{\ast},\cdots,f_{in_{i}}^{\ast})
be a split hyperbolic basis of
\mathcal{W}_{i}
. Let us write:

s_{i}:\mu_{2}\longrightarrow\operatorname{U}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{i});\epsilon\longmapsto\begin{pmatrix}I&0\\
0&\epsilon I\end{pmatrix},

where

s_{1}(-1)(f_{11},\cdots,f_{1n_{1}};f_{11}^{\ast},\cdots,f_{1n_{1}}^{\ast})=(f_{11},\cdots,f_{1n_{1}};-f_{11}^{\ast},\cdots,-f_{1n_{1}}^{\ast}),

(f_{21},\cdots,f_{2n_{2}};f_{21}^{\ast},\cdots,f_{2n_{2}}^{\ast})s_{2}(-1)=(f_{21},\cdots,f_{2n_{2}};-f_{21}^{\ast},\cdots,-f_{2n_{2}}^{\ast}).

Let
S_{i}=s_{i}(\mu_{2})
, and
\widehat{S_{i}}\subseteq\widehat{\operatorname{U}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{i})
. Let us write
X_{1}=(f_{11},\cdots,f_{1n_{1}})
,
X_{1}^{\ast}=(f_{11}^{\ast},\cdots,f_{1n_{1}}^{\ast})
,
Y_{1}=(f_{21},\cdots,f_{2n_{2}})
,
Y_{1}^{\ast}=(f_{21}^{\ast},\cdots,f_{2n_{2}}^{\ast})
. Then:

(f_{11}\otimes Y_{1},f_{11}\otimes Y_{1}^{\ast},\cdots,f_{1n_{1}}\otimes Y_{1},f_{1n_{1}}\otimes Y_{1}^{\ast};f^{\ast}_{11}\otimes Y_{1}^{\ast},\epsilon_{2}f^{\ast}_{11}\otimes Y_{1},\cdots,f_{1n_{1}}^{\ast}\otimes Y_{1}^{\ast},\epsilon_{2}f_{1n_{1}}^{\ast}\otimes Y_{1})

forms a split hyperbolic basis of
\mathcal{W}_{1}\otimes_{D}\mathcal{W}_{2}
. Recall:

s:\operatorname{U}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{1})\times\operatorname{U}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{2})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{1}\otimes s_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{U}^{\pm}(\mathcal{W}_{1}\otimes_{D}\mathcal{W}_{2})\longrightarrow\operatorname{Sp}(W).

Hence

s_{1}(\epsilon)\otimes I_{\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{2})}=\begin{pmatrix}I_{1}\otimes I_{2}&0\\
0&\epsilon I_{1}\otimes I_{2}\end{pmatrix}=s(\epsilon),

I_{\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{1})}\otimes s_{2}(\epsilon)=\operatorname{diag}\Bigg(\begin{pmatrix}I_{2}&0\\
0&\epsilon I_{2}\end{pmatrix},\cdots,\begin{pmatrix}I_{2}&0\\
0&\epsilon I_{2}\end{pmatrix};\begin{pmatrix}\epsilon I_{2}&0\\
0&I_{2}\end{pmatrix},\cdots,\begin{pmatrix}\epsilon I_{2}&0\\
0&I_{2}\end{pmatrix}\Bigg).

Let us write
I_{\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{2})}\otimes s_{2}(\epsilon)=s(\epsilon)g_{0}
, for some
g_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}a&0\\
0&a^{-1}\end{pmatrix}
. Then:

\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}(s_{1}(-1)\otimes I_{\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{2})},I_{\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{1})}\otimes s_{2}(-1))=\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}([-1,1],[-1,g_{0}])=\nu(-1,1)\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(1,g_{0})=1,

\displaystyle\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}(I_{\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{1})}\otimes s_{2}(-1),s_{1}(-1)\otimes I_{\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{W}_{2})})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}([-1,g_{0}],[-1,1])=\nu(-1,g_{0})\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{0}^{s(-1)},1)

\displaystyle=\nu(-1,g_{0})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}1&\textrm{ if }D=E,\mathbb{H},\\
(-1,(-1)^{n_{1}n_{2}})_{F}&\textrm{ if }D=F.\end{array}\right.

Hence:

(1)

If
D=E
or
\mathbb{H}
,
\widehat{S_{1}}
commutes with
\widehat{S_{2}}
. Consequently,
\widehat{\operatorname{U}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
is commuted with
\widehat{\operatorname{U}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
in
\widetilde{\operatorname{Sp}}^{\pm}(W)
.

(2)

If
D=F
, assume that
\mathcal{W}_{1}
is symplectic and
\mathcal{W}_{2}
is orthogonal. Let
g_{1}\in\operatorname{Sp}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
,
g_{2}\in\operatorname{O}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
. Then:

\displaystyle\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}(s_{1}(-1)\otimes I_{2},I_{1}\otimes g_{2})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}([-1,1],[1,I_{1}\otimes g_{2}])

\displaystyle=\nu(1,1)\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(1,I_{1}\otimes g_{2})=1;

\displaystyle\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}(I_{1}\otimes g_{2},s_{1}(-1)\otimes I_{2})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}([1,I_{1}\otimes g_{2}],[-1,1])

\displaystyle=\nu(-1,I_{1}\otimes g_{2})\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}([I_{1}\otimes g_{2}]^{s(-1)},1)

\displaystyle=\nu(-1,I_{1}\otimes g_{2})=((\det g_{2})^{n_{1}},-1)_{\mathbb{R}}.

Hence:

i)

If
2\mid n_{1}
,
\widehat{S_{1}}
commutes with
\widehat{\operatorname{O}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
.

ii)

If
2\nmid n_{1}
,
\widehat{S_{1}}
only commutes with
\widehat{\operatorname{SO}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
.

Similarly, by choosing the similar split hyperbolic basis for
\mathcal{W}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{W}_{2}
, we can obtain that
\widehat{S_{2}}
commutes with
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
. We conclude:

a)

If
2\mid n_{1}
and
2\mid n_{2}
, by the above proof of Part (1), we know that
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
commutes with
\widehat{\operatorname{O}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
and
\widehat{\operatorname{O}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
commutes with
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
. By the above discussion,
\widehat{S_{1}}
commutes with
\widehat{S_{2}}
and
\widehat{\operatorname{O}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
. Hence
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
commutes with
\widehat{\operatorname{O}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
.

b)

If
2\mid n_{1}
and
2\nmid n_{2}
,
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
commutes with
\widehat{\operatorname{O}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
,
\widehat{S_{1}}
commutes with
\widehat{S_{2}}
,

\widehat{\operatorname{O}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
commutes with
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
. Hence
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
commutes with
\widehat{\operatorname{O}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
.

c)

If
2\nmid n_{1}
and
2\mid n_{2}
,
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
only commutes with
\widehat{\operatorname{SO}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
,
\widehat{S_{1}}
commutes with
\widehat{S_{2}}
,

\widehat{\operatorname{O}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
commutes with
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
. Hence
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
only commutes with
\widehat{\operatorname{SO}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
and
\widehat{\operatorname{O}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
only commutes with
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
.

d)

If
2\nmid n_{1}
,
2\nmid n_{2}
,
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
only commutes with
\widehat{\operatorname{SO}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
,
\widehat{S_{1}}
is not commuted with
\widehat{S_{2}}
,

\widehat{\operatorname{O}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
commutes with
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
. Hence
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
only commutes with
\widehat{\operatorname{SO}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
and
\widehat{\operatorname{O}^{\pm}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})
only commutes with
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
.

5.8.
Unitary Weil representation

In this last subsection, we will let
V
be a complex vector space of dimension
m
with a basis
\{f_{1},\cdots,f_{m}\}
. Let
(,)_{V}
be a left hermitian form on
V
given as follows:

(v,v^{\prime})_{V}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}z_{i}\overline{z^{\prime}}_{i},\quad\quad z=\sum_{i=1}^{m}z_{i}f_{i},z^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}z_{i}^{\prime}f_{i}.

Let
\operatorname{H}(V)=V\oplus F
be the group of elements
(v,t)
, with the
multiplication law given by

(v,t)(v^{\prime},t^{\prime})=(v+v^{\prime},\tfrac{i\operatorname{Im}(v,v^{\prime})_{V}}{2}),\quad\quad v,v^{\prime}\in V,t,t^{\prime}\in F.

Then there exists a canonical
\operatorname{Gal}(\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{R})
-action on
V
. We can extend this action to
\operatorname{H}(V)
as follows:

\operatorname{H}(V)\times\operatorname{Gal}(\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{R})\longrightarrow V;(\sigma,[v,t])\longrightarrow[v^{\sigma},\chi(\sigma)t],

for the non-trivial quadratic character
\chi
of
\operatorname{Gal}(\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{R})
. Let
\operatorname{Gal}(\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{R})=\langle\sigma\rangle
,
F_{2}=\langle s(-1)\rangle
.

Lemma 5.13
.

There exists a group isomorphism:

\displaystyle\phi:

\displaystyle\operatorname{Gal}(\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{R})\ltimes\operatorname{H}(V)\longrightarrow\operatorname{H}^{\pm}(W)\simeq F_{2}\ltimes\operatorname{H}(W);

(5.16)

\displaystyle\quad\sigma\longmapsto s(-1)

(5.17)

\displaystyle\quad(\sum^{m}_{k=1}(a_{k}+ib_{k})f_{k},t)\longmapsto(\sum^{m}_{k=1}a_{k}e_{k}+b_{k}e_{k}^{\ast},t).

(5.18)

Proof.

It is clear that
\phi
is a bijective map.

1)

\displaystyle\phi([\sum^{m}_{k=1}(a_{k}+ib_{k})f_{k},t]^{\sigma})

\displaystyle=\phi([\sum^{m}_{k=1}(a_{k}-ib_{k})f_{k},-t])=[\sum^{m}_{k=1}a_{k}e_{k}-b_{k}e_{k}^{\ast},-t]

\displaystyle=[\sum^{m}_{k=1}a_{k}e_{k}+b_{k}e_{k}^{\ast},t]^{s(-1)}=\phi([\sum^{m}_{k=1}(a_{k}+ib_{k})f_{k},t])^{\phi(\sigma)}.

2) For two element
h_{l}=(w_{l},t_{l})\in\operatorname{H}(W)
, with
w_{l}=\sum^{m}_{k=1}a^{l}_{k}e_{k}+b^{l}_{k}e_{k}^{\ast}
, for
l=1,2
, we have:

\displaystyle h_{1}h_{2}=(w_{1}+w_{2},t_{1}+t_{2}+\tfrac{\langle w_{1},w_{2}\rangle}{2})=(w_{1}+w_{2},t_{1}+t_{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{m}(a_{k}^{1}b_{k}^{2}-a_{k}^{2}b_{k}^{1}));

\displaystyle\phi^{-1}(h_{l})=(\sum^{m}_{k=1}(a^{l}_{k}+ib^{l}_{k})f_{k},t_{l});

\displaystyle\phi^{-1}(h_{1}h_{2})=(\sum^{m}_{k=1}(a^{1}_{k}+ib^{1}_{k})f_{k}+\sum^{m}_{k=1}(a^{2}_{k}+ib^{2}_{k})f_{k},t_{1}+t_{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{m}(a_{k}^{1}b_{k}^{2}-a_{k}^{2}b_{k}^{1}));

\displaystyle\phi^{-1}(h_{1})\phi^{-1}(h_{2})

\displaystyle=(\sum^{m}_{k=1}(a^{1}_{k}+ib^{1}_{k})f_{k},t_{1})(\sum^{m}_{k=1}(a^{2}_{k}+ib^{2}_{k})f_{k},t_{2})

\displaystyle=(\sum^{m}_{k=1}(a^{1}_{k}+ib^{1}_{k})f_{k}+\sum^{m}_{k=1}(a^{2}_{k}+ib^{2}_{k})f_{k},t_{1}+t_{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}\sum^{m}_{k=1}(a^{1}_{k}b^{2}_{k}-b^{1}_{k}a^{2}_{k})).

Hence
\phi^{-1}(h_{1}h_{2})=\phi^{-1}(h_{1})\phi^{-1}(h_{2})
.
∎

Lemma 5.14
.

The above
\phi
can extend to be a group homomorphism:

\phi:\operatorname{Gal}(\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{R})\operatorname{U}(V)\ltimes\operatorname{H}(V)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}(W)\ltimes\operatorname{H}(W).

Proof.

Let
h=(v,t)\in\operatorname{H}(V)
, with
v=\sum^{m}_{k=1}(a_{k}+ib_{k})f_{k}
. Let
g\in\operatorname{U}(V)
. Then:

\displaystyle h^{(\sigma,g)}=h^{(\sigma,1)(1,g)}=(\sum^{m}_{k=1}(a_{k}-ib_{k})f_{k},-t)^{g}=([\sum^{m}_{k=1}(a_{k}-ib_{k})f_{k}]g,-t);

\displaystyle\phi(h^{(\sigma,g)})=\phi([\sum^{m}_{k=1}(a_{k}-ib_{k})f_{k}]g,-t)=([\sum^{m}_{k=1}(a_{k}e_{k}-ib_{k}e^{\ast}_{k})]g,-t);

\displaystyle\phi(h)^{\phi(\sigma,g)}

\displaystyle=(\sum^{m}_{k=1}(a_{k}e_{k}+ib_{k}e^{\ast}_{k}),t)^{(s(-1),g)}=(\sum^{m}_{k=1}(a_{k}e_{k}-ib_{k}e^{\ast}_{k}),-t)^{g}

\displaystyle=([\sum^{m}_{k=1}(a_{k}e_{k}-ib_{k}e^{\ast}_{k})]g,-t)=\phi(h^{(\sigma,g)}).

∎

Let
\widehat{\operatorname{U}(V)}
be the preimage of
\operatorname{U}(V)
in
\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}}(W)
. Then this
\phi
can extend to a group homomorphism:

\phi:\operatorname{Gal}(\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{R})\widehat{\operatorname{U}(V)}\ltimes\operatorname{H}(V)\longrightarrow\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}}^{\pm}(W)\ltimes\operatorname{H}(W).

Let us next show that
\widehat{\operatorname{U}(V)}
is splitting from Theorem
5.1
in details.

5.8.1.

Let
\mathcal{W}=E
, be a right vector space over
E
, endowed with the skew hermitian form:

(z,z^{\prime})_{\mathcal{W}}=-i\overline{z}z^{\prime}.

Let
\mathcal{V}=V
, endowed with the hermitian form:
(-,-)_{\mathcal{V}}=(-,-)_{V}
.
Then

(\mathcal{W}\otimes\mathcal{V},\tfrac{1}{2}\operatorname{Tr}_{E/F}[(,)_{\mathcal{W}}\otimes\overline{(,)}_{\mathcal{V}}])

is a symplectic space over
F
of dimension
2m
. Let
\operatorname{H}(\mathcal{W}\otimes\mathcal{V})
be the corresponding Heisenberg group. Then there exists the following isomorphism:

\ell:\operatorname{H}(\mathcal{W}\otimes\mathcal{V})\longrightarrow\operatorname{H}(V);(\sum w_{l}\otimes v_{l},t)\longmapsto(\sum w_{l}v_{l},t).

It is clear that
\ell
is a bijective map. Let us show that it is also a group homomorphism:

i\operatorname{Im}z=\tfrac{1}{2}\operatorname{Tr}_{E/F}(iz),\quad\quad z\in E;

\displaystyle(\sum w_{l}\otimes v_{l},t_{1})(\sum w_{k}\otimes v_{k},t_{2})

\displaystyle=(\sum_{l}w_{l}\otimes v_{l}+\sum_{k}w_{k}\otimes v_{k},t_{1}+t_{2}+\tfrac{1}{4}\sum_{l}\sum_{k}\operatorname{Tr}_{E/F}[-i\overline{w_{l}}w_{k}\overline{(v_{l},v_{k})}_{V}]);

\displaystyle\ell((\sum w_{l}\otimes v_{l},t_{1})(\sum w_{k}\otimes v_{k},t_{2}))=(\sum_{l}w_{l}v_{l}+\sum_{k}w_{k}v_{k},t_{1}+t_{2}+\tfrac{1}{4}\sum_{l}\sum_{k}\operatorname{Tr}_{E/F}[-i\overline{w_{l}}w_{k}\overline{(v_{l},v_{k})}_{V}]);

\displaystyle\ell([\sum_{l}w_{l}\otimes v_{l},t_{1}])\ell([\sum_{k}w_{k}\otimes v_{k},t_{2}])

\displaystyle=(\sum_{l}w_{l}v_{l},t_{1})(\sum_{k}w_{k}v_{k},t_{2})

\displaystyle=(\sum_{l}w_{l}v_{l}+\sum_{k}w_{k}v_{k},t_{1}+t_{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}i\operatorname{Im}(\sum_{l}w_{l}v_{l},\sum_{k}w_{k}v_{k})_{V})

\displaystyle=(\sum_{l}w_{l}v_{l}+\sum_{k}w_{k}v_{k},t_{1}+t_{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{l}\sum_{k}i\operatorname{Im}(w_{l}\overline{w_{k}}(v_{l},v_{k})_{V})

\displaystyle=(\sum_{l}w_{l}v_{l}+\sum_{k}w_{k}v_{k},t_{1}+t_{2}+\tfrac{1}{4}\sum_{l}\sum_{k}\operatorname{Tr}_{E/F}(iw_{l}\overline{w_{k}}(v_{l},v_{k})_{V}).

Moreover,
\ell
can extend to an isomorphism:

\ell:\operatorname{Gal}(\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{R})\operatorname{U}(\mathcal{V})\ltimes\operatorname{H}(\mathcal{W}\otimes\mathcal{V})\longrightarrow\operatorname{Gal}(\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{R})\operatorname{U}(V)\ltimes\operatorname{H}(V).

By Theorem
5.1
,
\widehat{\operatorname{U}(V)}
is splitting. In the next section, we will see the explicit trivialization when
\dim_{E}V=1
.

6.
Parenthesis: Modular form on the two fold covering space

In this section, let us consider the example when
\dim W=2
. We mainly follow
[
LiVe
]
to deal with theta series. On the other hand, approaching theta series in a more gobal way, see
[
We
]
and
[
Ge
]
.

6.1.
Notations

Assume
\psi=\psi_{0}
. In this case,
F=\mathbb{R}
,
E=\mathbb{C}=F(i)
,
\operatorname{Gal}(E/F)=\langle\sigma\rangle
,
F_{2}=\langle s(-1)\rangle
for
s(-1)=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{SL}^{\pm}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
,
\operatorname{Sp}(W)\simeq\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
,
\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}(W)\simeq\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})
,
X=Fe_{1}\simeq\mathbb{R}
,
X^{\ast}=Fe_{1}^{\ast}\simeq\mathbb{R}
,
V=E
,
\operatorname{U}(V)=\{\cos\theta+i\sin\theta\mid-\pi<\theta\leq\pi\}
. By Lemma
5.13
, we can treat
\operatorname{U}(V)
as the subgroup
\operatorname{SO}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, through the map:
\cos\theta+i\sin\theta\longrightarrow\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta&\sin\theta\\
-\sin\theta&\cos\theta\end{pmatrix}
. Let:

•

\operatorname{sgn}:\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow\{\pm 1,0\};t\longmapsto\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}1&\textrm{ if }t>0\\
0&\textrm{ if }t=0\\
-1&\textrm{ if }t<0\end{array}\right.
;

•

P(\mathbb{R})=\{g=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
0&a^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\}
,
N(\mathbb{R})=\{g=\begin{pmatrix}1&b\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\}
,
N_{-}(\mathbb{R})=\{g=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
c&1\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\}
;

•

N
: a positive integer number bigger than
2
;

•

\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})=\{g=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\mid a,b,c,d\in\mathbb{Z}\}
;

•

\Gamma(N)=\{\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})\mid\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\equiv\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}(\bmod N)\}
;

•

P^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})=\{g=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
0&a^{-1}\det g\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{SL}^{\pm}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\}
;

•

P_{>0}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})=\{g=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
0&a^{-1}\det g\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{SL}^{\pm}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\mid a>0\}
;

•

\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{Z})=\{g=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})\mid a,b,c,d\in\mathbb{Z}\}
;

•

\operatorname{SO}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})=\{g=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta&\sin\theta\\
\mp\sin\theta&\pm\cos\theta\end{pmatrix}\mid-\pi<\theta\leq\pi\}
;

•

\mathcal{H}^{\pm}=\{b+ai\in\mathbb{C}\mid a\neq 0\}
;

•

\operatorname{H}(V)=E\oplus\mathbb{R}
, with the multiplication:
(z,t)(z^{\prime},t^{\prime})=(z+z^{\prime},t+t^{\prime}+\tfrac{1}{2}i\operatorname{Im}(z\overline{z^{\prime}}))
;

•

\operatorname{H}(W)=\mathbb{R}\oplus\mathbb{R}\oplus\mathbb{R}
, with the multiplication:
(x,y;t)(x^{\prime},y^{\prime};t^{\prime})=(x+x^{\prime},y+y^{\prime};t+t^{\prime}+\tfrac{1}{2}(xy^{\prime}-x^{\prime}y))
;

•

\operatorname{Gal}(\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{R})\ltimes\operatorname{H}(V)=\operatorname{Gal}(\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{R})\ltimes(\mathbb{C}\oplus\mathbb{R})
, with the action:
(z,t)^{\sigma}=(z^{\sigma},-t)
;

•

\operatorname{H}^{\pm}(W)=F_{2}\ltimes(\mathbb{R}\oplus\mathbb{R}\oplus\mathbb{R})
, with the action:
(x,y;t)^{s(-1)}=(x,-y;-t)
;

•

\operatorname{Arg}(z)\in(-\pi,\pi]
, for the principal argument function
\operatorname{Arg}
on
\mathbb{C}
;

•

\omega=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}
,
u(b)=\begin{pmatrix}1&b\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}
,
u_{-}(c)=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
c&1\end{pmatrix}
,
h(a)=\begin{pmatrix}a&0\\
0&a^{-1}\end{pmatrix}
,
h_{\epsilon}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&\epsilon\end{pmatrix}
.

Let
g_{1},g_{2},g_{3}=g_{1}g_{2}\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
with
g_{i}=\begin{pmatrix}a_{i}&b_{i}\\
c_{i}&d_{i}\end{pmatrix}
.

(1)

x(g_{1})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}d_{1}\mathbb{R}^{\times 2}&\textrm{ if }c_{1}=0,\\
c_{1}\mathbb{R}^{\times 2}&\textrm{ if }c_{1}\neq 0.\end{array}\right.

(2)

m_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}e^{\tfrac{i\pi}{4}[1-\operatorname{sgn}(d_{1})]}&\textrm{ if }c_{1}=0,\\
e^{\tfrac{i\pi}{4}[-\operatorname{sgn}(c_{1})]}&\textrm{ if }c_{1}\neq 0.\end{array}\right.

(3)

\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})=(x(g_{1}),x(g_{2}))_{\mathbb{R}}(-x(g_{1})x(g_{2}),x(g_{3}))_{\mathbb{R}}
.

(4)

\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})=\gamma(\psi(q(g_{1},g_{2})/2))=\gamma(\psi(\tfrac{1}{2}c_{1}c_{2}c_{3}))=e^{\tfrac{i\pi}{4}\operatorname{sgn}(c_{1}c_{2}c_{3})}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}1&\textrm{ if }c_{1}c_{2}c_{3}=0,\\
e^{\tfrac{i\pi}{4}}&\textrm{ if }c_{1}c_{2}c_{3}>0,\\
e^{-\tfrac{i\pi}{4}}&\textrm{ if }c_{1}c_{2}c_{3}<0.\end{array}\right.

Let
h_{1},h_{2},h_{3}=h_{1}h_{2}\in\operatorname{SL}^{\pm}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
with
h_{i}=\begin{pmatrix}a_{i}&b_{i}\\
c_{i}&d_{i}\end{pmatrix}
,
y_{i}=\det h_{i}
,
g_{i}=\begin{pmatrix}a_{i}&b_{i}\\
y_{i}^{-1}c_{i}&y_{i}^{-1}d_{i}\end{pmatrix}
. Then:

•

\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}(h_{1},h_{2})=\nu(y_{2},g_{1})\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1}^{y_{2}},g_{2})
.

•

\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}(h_{1},h_{2})=\nu_{2}(y_{2},g_{1})\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1}^{y_{2}},g_{2})
.

•

\nu(y_{2},g_{1})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}(y_{2},a_{1})_{\mathbb{R}}&c_{1}=0,\\
(y_{1}c_{1},y_{2})_{\mathbb{R}}\gamma(y_{2},\psi^{\tfrac{1}{2}})^{-1}&c_{1}\neq 0.\end{array}\right.

•

\nu_{2}(y_{2},g_{1})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}(y_{2},a_{1})_{\mathbb{R}}&c_{1}=0,\\
1&c_{1}\neq 0.\end{array}\right.

Lemma 6.1
.

For
h=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{SL}^{\pm}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
,
p=\begin{pmatrix}a_{1}&b_{1}\\
0&a_{1}^{-1}\det p\end{pmatrix}\in P^{\pm}_{>0}(\mathbb{R})
, we have:

•

\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}(p,h)=1
;

•

\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}(h,p)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}(\det p,a)_{\mathbb{R}}&\textrm{ if }c=0\\
(c\det h,\det p)_{\mathbb{R}}\gamma(\det p,\psi^{\tfrac{1}{2}})^{-1}&\textrm{ if }c\neq 0\end{array}\right.
.

Proof.

Let
g_{h}=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c\det h&d\det h\end{pmatrix}
, and
g_{p}=\begin{pmatrix}a_{1}&b_{1}\\
0&a_{1}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}
. Then:

\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}(p,h)=\nu(\det h,g_{p})\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}((g_{p})^{\det h},g_{h})=(\det h,a_{1})_{\mathbb{R}}=1;

\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}(h,p)=\nu(\det p,g_{h})\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}((g_{h})^{\det p},g_{p})=\nu(\det p,g_{h})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}(\det p,a)_{\mathbb{R}}&\textrm{ if }c=0,\\
(c\det h,\det p)_{\mathbb{R}}\gamma(\det p,\psi^{\tfrac{1}{2}})^{-1}&\textrm{ if }c\neq 0.\end{array}\right.

∎

Lemma 6.2
.

For
h=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{SL}^{\pm}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
,
p=\begin{pmatrix}a_{1}&b_{1}\\
0&a_{1}^{-1}\det p\end{pmatrix}\in P^{\pm}_{>0}(\mathbb{R})
, we have:

•

\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}(p,h)=1
;

•

\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}(h,p)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}(\det p,a)_{\mathbb{R}}&\textrm{ if }c=0\\
1&\textrm{ if }c\neq 0\end{array}\right.
.

Proof.

Let
g_{h}=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c\det h&d\det h\end{pmatrix}
, and
g_{p}=\begin{pmatrix}a_{1}&b_{1}\\
0&a_{1}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}
. Then:

\displaystyle\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}(p,h)

\displaystyle=\nu_{2}(\det h,g_{p})\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}((g_{p})^{\det h},g_{h})

\displaystyle=(\det h,a_{1})_{\mathbb{R}}(x(g_{p}^{\det h}),x(g_{h}))_{\mathbb{R}}(-x(g_{p}^{\det h})x(g_{h}),x((g_{p})^{\det h}g_{h}))_{\mathbb{R}}

\displaystyle=1;

\displaystyle\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}(h,p)

\displaystyle=\nu_{2}(\det p,g_{h})\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}((g_{h})^{\det p},g_{p})=\nu_{2}(\det p,g_{h})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}(\det p,a)_{\mathbb{R}}&\textrm{ if }c=0,\\
1&\textrm{ if }c\neq 0.\end{array}\right.

∎

6.2.
Splitting of
\widehat{\operatorname{U}}(V)

Following
[
LiVe
, pp.75-77]
, let us define the function
u:\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}
by

u(\theta)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}2k&\textrm{ if }\theta=k\pi,\\
2k+1&\textrm{ if }k\pi<\theta<(k+1)\pi.\end{array}\right.

Lemma 6.3
.

\operatorname{sgn}[\sin(\theta_{1})\sin(\theta_{2})\sin(\theta_{1}+\theta_{2})]=u(\theta_{1})+u(\theta_{2})-u(\theta_{1}+\theta_{2})
.

Proof.

See
[
LiVe
, pp.75-77]
.
∎

Let us modify the above
u
by a character of
\mathbb{R}
in the following way:

u^{\prime}(\theta)=u(\theta)+\tfrac{2}{\pi}\theta.

Lemma 6.4
.

(1)

u^{\prime}(\theta+k\pi)=u^{\prime}(\theta)+4k
, for
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.

(2)

\operatorname{sgn}[\sin(\theta_{1})\sin(\theta_{2})\sin(\theta_{1}+\theta_{2})]=u^{\prime}(\theta_{1})+u^{\prime}(\theta_{2})-u^{\prime}(\theta_{1}+\theta_{2})
.

Proof.

1) Note that
u(\theta+k\pi)=u(\theta)+2k
. So
u^{\prime}(\theta+k\pi)=u(\theta+k\pi)+\tfrac{2}{\pi}(\theta+k\pi)=u(\theta)+2k+\tfrac{2}{\pi}\theta+2k=u^{\prime}(\theta)+4k
.

2) Since
u^{\prime}
is different from
u
by a character of
\mathbb{R}
, the result follows from the above lemma.
∎

Lemma 6.5
.

The restriction of
[\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}]
on
\operatorname{U}(V)
is trivial, with an explicit trivialization:

\widetilde{s}:\operatorname{U}(V)\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\ast};\cos\theta+i\sin\theta\longmapsto e^{\tfrac{i\pi}{4}u^{\prime}(\theta)},

such that
\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})=\widetilde{s}(g_{1})^{-1}\widetilde{s}(g_{2})^{-1}\widetilde{s}(g_{1}g_{2})
, for
g_{i}\in\operatorname{U}(V)
.

Proof.

It is well defined by the above lemma (1).

\displaystyle\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(e^{i\theta_{1}},e^{i\theta_{2}})

\displaystyle=e^{\tfrac{i\pi}{4}\operatorname{sgn}[-\sin(\theta_{1})\sin(\theta_{2})\sin(\theta_{1}+\theta_{2})]}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{ Lemma \ref{theq}(2)}}}{{=}}e^{-\tfrac{i\pi}{4}u^{\prime}(\theta_{1})}e^{-\tfrac{i\pi}{4}u^{\prime}(\theta_{2})}e^{\tfrac{i\pi}{4}u^{\prime}(\theta_{1}+\theta_{2})}.

∎

Lemma 6.6
.

The restriction of
[\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}]
on
\operatorname{U}(V)
is trivial, with an explicit trivialization:

\overline{s}(e^{i\theta})=e^{\tfrac{i\theta}{2}}
, for
-\pi<\theta\leq\pi
,
such that
\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})=\overline{s}(g_{1})^{-1}\overline{s}(g_{2})^{-1}\overline{s}(g_{1}g_{2})
, for
g_{i}\in\operatorname{U}(V)
.

Proof.

By (
2.5
) and the above lemma, we have:

\displaystyle\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})

\displaystyle=m_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1}g_{2})^{-1}m_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1})m_{X^{\ast}}(g_{2})\widetilde{c}_{{X^{\ast}}}(g_{1},g_{2})

\displaystyle=[\widetilde{s}(g_{1}g_{2})m_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1}g_{2})^{-1}][\widetilde{s}(g_{1})m_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1})^{-1}]^{-1}[\widetilde{s}(g_{2})m_{X^{\ast}}(g_{2})^{-1}]^{-1}.

Let
\overline{s}(e^{i\theta})=\widetilde{s}(e^{i\theta})m_{X^{\ast}}(e^{i\theta})^{-1}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}1&\textrm{ if }\theta=0,\\
e^{\tfrac{i\theta}{2}}&\textrm{ if }0<\theta<\pi,\\
e^{\tfrac{i\theta}{2}}&\textrm{ if }-\pi<\theta<0,\\
e^{\tfrac{i\theta}{2}}&\textrm{ if }\theta=\pi.\end{array}\right.
Then
\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})=\overline{s}(g_{1}g_{2})\overline{s}(g_{1})^{-1}\overline{s}(g_{2})^{-1}
.
∎

Remark 6.7
.

(1)

The trivialization map for
\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}
can not be valued in
\mu_{8}
.

(2)

The trivialization map for
\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}
can not be valued in
\mu_{2}
.

Proof.

We only prove the first statement. If there exists another trivialization
\widetilde{s}^{\prime}:\operatorname{U}(V)\longrightarrow\mu_{8}
, then
\widetilde{s}
and
\widetilde{s}^{\prime}
will differ by a measurable group homomorphism
\chi:\operatorname{U}(V)\longrightarrow T
. Since
\operatorname{U}(V)
is separable,
\chi
is also continuous. It is known that
\chi(e^{i\theta})=e^{in\theta}
, for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then
e^{i\theta n-i\theta\tfrac{1}{2}}\in\mu_{8}
, for all
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
; this is impossible.
∎

6.3.
Spin group

Let us go into the low spin group
\operatorname{Spin}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
. By
[
Se
, pp.18-19]
, there exists an exact sequence

\displaystyle 1\longrightarrow\{\pm 1\}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Spin}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{A}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{SO}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\to 1,

(6.1)

where
\operatorname{Spin}_{2}(\mathbb{R})=\{\cos\theta+k\sin\theta\mid k^{2}=-1\}
, and
\mathcal{A}(\cos\theta+k\sin\theta)=\begin{pmatrix}\cos 2\theta&\sin 2\theta\\
-\sin 2\theta&\cos 2\theta\end{pmatrix}
. Note that
\operatorname{H}^{2}(\operatorname{SO}_{2}(\mathbb{R}),T)=0
and
\operatorname{H}^{2}(\operatorname{SO}_{2}(\mathbb{R}),\mu_{2})\simeq\mu_{2}
. By Remark
6.7
,
[\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}]
is the unique nontrivial class in
\operatorname{H}^{2}(\operatorname{SO}_{2}(\mathbb{R}),\mu_{2})
. Hence
\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}
determines the above exact sequence (
6.1
). Let us identity
T
with
\operatorname{SO}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
by
e^{i\theta}\longrightarrow\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta&\sin\theta\\
-\sin\theta&\cos\theta\end{pmatrix}
. Let

1\longrightarrow\{\pm 1\}\longrightarrow\widetilde{T}\longrightarrow T\longrightarrow 1

be the exact sequence associated to
\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}
. Then
\operatorname{Spin}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\simeq\widetilde{T}
. Note that there exists a group homomorphism:
\sqrt{\,}:\widetilde{T}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}T
.

6.4.
Non-splitting of
\widehat{\operatorname{SO}^{\pm}_{2}}(\mathbb{R})

Lemma 6.8
.

The restriction of
[\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}]
on
\operatorname{SO}^{\pm}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
is not trivial.

Proof.

Note that there exists an exact sequence:

1\longrightarrow\operatorname{SO}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\longrightarrow\operatorname{SO}^{\pm}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\longrightarrow\mu_{2}\longrightarrow 1.

By Hochschild-Serre spectral sequence, there exists the following long exact sequence of six terms:

\displaystyle 0\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hom}(\mu_{2},T)\to\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{SO}^{\pm}_{2}(\mathbb{R}),T)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{SO}_{2}(\mathbb{R}),T)^{\operatorname{SO}^{\pm}_{2}(\mathbb{R})}\longrightarrow\operatorname{H}^{2}(\mu_{2},T)

(6.2)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\operatorname{H}^{2}(\operatorname{SO}^{\pm}_{2}(\mathbb{R}),T)_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{H}^{1}\big(\mu_{2},\operatorname{H}^{1}(\operatorname{SO}_{2}(\mathbb{R}),T)\big).

(6.3)

By Lemma
6.6
,

\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})=\overline{s}(g_{1}g_{2})\overline{s}(g_{1})^{-1}\overline{s}(g_{2})^{-1},\qquad\quad g_{i}\in\operatorname{SO}_{2}(\mathbb{R}).

Let
\overline{s}
extend to be a Borel function of
\operatorname{SO}^{\pm}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
by taking the trivial value outside
\operatorname{SO}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
. We replace
\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}
with
\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}^{\prime}=\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}\circ\delta_{1}\overline{s}
. Then:

\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}^{\prime}(h_{1},h_{2})=\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}(h_{1},h_{2})\overline{s}(h_{1})\overline{s}(h_{2})\overline{s}(h_{1}h_{2})^{-1},\quad\quad h_{1},h_{2}\in\operatorname{SO}^{\pm}_{2}(\mathbb{R}).

Recall the section map
s:\mu_{2}\longrightarrow\operatorname{SO}^{\pm}_{2}(\mathbb{R});\epsilon\longmapsto\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&\epsilon\end{pmatrix}
. Then for
g\in\operatorname{SO}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, we have:

\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}^{\prime}(s(1),g)=\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}(s(1),g)=\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}([1,1],[1,g])=1;

\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}^{\prime}(s(-1),g)=\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}(s(-1),g)\overline{s}(g)=\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}([-1,1],[1,g])\overline{s}(g)=\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(1,g)\overline{s}(g)=\overline{s}(g).

For any
h\in\operatorname{SO}^{\pm}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, let us write
h=s(\epsilon_{h})g_{h}
, for
\epsilon_{h}\in\mu_{2}
,
g_{h}\in\operatorname{SO}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
. Define a Borel function
\varkappa
of
\operatorname{SO}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})
as

\varkappa(h)=\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}^{\prime}(s_{1}(\epsilon_{h}),g_{h})
. Let us define

\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}^{\prime\prime}=\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}^{\prime}\circ\delta_{1}(\varkappa).

So the map
p
is just given by

p([\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}]):\epsilon\longmapsto(g\longrightarrow\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}^{\prime\prime}(g,s(\epsilon))),\quad g\in\operatorname{SO}_{2}(\mathbb{R}),\epsilon\in\mu_{2}.

Note that
p([\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}])(\epsilon)
is a character of
\operatorname{SO}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
. Let
g=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta&\sin\theta\\
-\sin\theta&\cos\theta\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{SO}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
,
g^{s(-1)}=\begin{pmatrix}\cos(-\theta)&\sin(-\theta)\\
-\sin(-\theta)&\cos(-\theta)\end{pmatrix}
. Then:

\displaystyle\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}^{\prime\prime}(g,s(-1))

\displaystyle=\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}^{\prime}(g,s(-1))\varkappa(g)\varkappa(s(-1))\varkappa(gs(-1))^{-1}

(6.4)

\displaystyle=\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}^{\prime}(g,s(-1))\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}^{\prime}(s(1),g)\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}^{\prime}(s(-1),1)\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}^{\prime}(s(-1),s(-1)^{-1}gs(-1))^{-1}

(6.5)

\displaystyle=\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}(g,s(-1))\overline{s}(g)\overline{s}(g^{s(-1)})^{-1}

(6.6)

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}\overline{s}(g)\overline{s}(g^{s(-1)})^{-1}=e^{i\theta}&\textrm{ if }-\pi<\theta<\pi\\
-1&\textrm{ if }\theta=\pi\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle=e^{i\theta}.

(6.9)

Note that there does not exist a character
f
of
\operatorname{SO}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
such that
e^{i\theta}=f(e^{i\theta})f^{-1}((e^{i\theta})^{s(-1)})=f(e^{2i\theta})
. So In the above exact sequence of six terms,
p([\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}])\neq 0
, but
\operatorname{H}^{2}(\mu_{2},T)=0
, which implies that
[\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}]\neq 0
.
∎

6.5.
Non-splitting of
\overline{\Gamma^{\pm}(2)}
I

Lemma 6.9
.

The restriction of
[\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}]
on
\Gamma^{\pm}(2)
is not trivial.

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of Lemma
6.8
by replacing
\operatorname{SO}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
with
\Gamma^{\pm}(2)
. We use the notations from there. For
g=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\Gamma(2)
,
g^{s(-1)}=\begin{pmatrix}a&-b\\
-c&d\end{pmatrix}
,

\displaystyle\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}^{\prime\prime}(g,s(-1))

\displaystyle=\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}(g,s(-1))\overline{\beta}(g)\overline{\beta}(g^{s(-1)})^{-1}.

(6.10)

If
c=0
,
\overline{\beta}(g)\overline{\beta}(g^{s(-1)})^{-1}=1
,
\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}(g,s(-1))=(a,-1)_{F}
, so
\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}^{\prime\prime}(g,s(-1))=(a,-1)_{F}
.

If
c\neq 0
,
\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}(g,s(-1))=1
,

\displaystyle\overline{\beta}(g)\overline{\beta}(g^{s(-1)})^{-1}

\displaystyle=\beta(d,c)e^{\tfrac{\pi i\operatorname{sgn}(c)}{4}}\beta(d,c)e^{\tfrac{\pi i\operatorname{sgn}(c)}{4}}

\displaystyle=\beta(d,c)^{2}e^{\tfrac{\pi i\operatorname{sgn}(c)}{2}}

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}1&d\equiv 1(\bmod 4),\\
-1&d\equiv 3(\bmod 4).\end{array}\right.

Similarly,
[\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}]\neq 0
on
\Gamma^{\pm}(2)
. Let
\overline{\Gamma^{\pm}(2)}
denote the two-degree covering group over
\Gamma^{\pm}(2)
associated to
\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}
.
∎

6.6.
Two models

Let
\Pi_{\psi}
be the Heisenberg representation of
\operatorname{H}^{\pm}(W)
as defined in Section
4.2
and Section
4.1
. It can be realized on the vector spaces
\mathcal{H}^{\pm}(Y)=\operatorname{Ind}_{Y\times F}^{\operatorname{H}^{\pm}(W)}\psi_{Y}
and
\mathcal{H}^{\pm}(L)=\operatorname{Ind}_{\operatorname{H}(L)}^{\operatorname{H}^{\pm}(W)}\psi_{L}
, for any
Y\in\Lambda
,
L\in\mathcal{L}_{\psi}
, where
\psi_{Y}
(resp.
\psi_{L}
) is an extending character of
Y\times F
(resp.
\operatorname{H}(L)
) from
\psi
of
F
.

In particular, take
Y^{\ast}=X^{\ast}
,
Y=X
and
L=\mathbb{Z}e_{1}\oplus\mathbb{Z}e_{1}^{\ast}
. Note that the actions of
\operatorname{H}^{\pm}(W)
on
\mathcal{H}^{\pm}(X^{\ast})
and
\mathcal{H}^{\pm}(L)
are given by the formulas (
4.1
) (
4.2
) and (
4.10
) (
4.11
) respectively. By
[
We
, pp.164-165]
, or
[
LiVe
, pp.142-145]
, there exists a pair of explicit isomorphisms between
\mathcal{H}^{\pm}(X^{\ast})\simeq L^{2}(\mu_{2}\times X)
and
\mathcal{H}^{\pm}(L)
, given as follows:

\theta_{L,X^{\ast}}(f^{\prime})(\epsilon,w)=\sum_{l\in L/L\cap X^{\ast}}f^{\prime}(\epsilon,w+l)\psi(\epsilon\tfrac{\langle l,w\rangle}{2}+\tfrac{\langle x_{l},x^{\ast}_{l}\rangle}{2}),

(6.11)

\begin{split}\theta_{X^{\ast},L}(f)(\epsilon,y)&=\int_{X^{\ast}/X^{\ast}\cap L}f([\dot{y}^{\ast},0]+[(\epsilon,y),0])d\dot{y}^{\ast}\\
&=\int_{X^{\ast}/X^{\ast}\cap L}\psi(\epsilon\tfrac{\langle\dot{y}^{\ast},y\rangle}{2})f(\epsilon,y+\epsilon\dot{y}^{\ast})d\dot{y}^{\ast},\end{split}

(6.12)

for
w=x+x^{\ast}\in W
,
y\in X
,
f^{\prime}\in S(\mu_{2}\times X)\subseteq L^{2}(\mu_{2}\times X)
,
f\in L^{1}(W)\cap\mathcal{H}^{\pm}(L)
. It can be checked that
\theta_{L,X^{\ast}}
and
\theta_{X^{\ast},L}
define a pair of inverse intertwining operators between
\operatorname{Ind}_{\operatorname{H}(L)}^{\operatorname{H}^{\pm}(W)}\psi_{L}
and
\operatorname{Ind}_{X^{\ast}\times\mathbb{R}}^{\operatorname{H}^{\pm}(W)}\psi_{X^{\ast}}
.
For
g\in\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}(W)
, we can define

\Pi_{\psi}(g)f=\theta_{L,X^{\ast}}[\Pi_{\psi}(g)(f^{\prime})]=\theta_{L,X^{\ast}}[\Pi_{\psi}(g)\theta_{X^{\ast},L}(f)].

(6.13)

Under such action, for
g_{1},g_{2}\in\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}(W)
,

\begin{split}\Pi_{\psi}(g_{1})[\Pi_{\psi}(g_{2})f]&=\theta_{L,X^{\ast}}[\Pi_{\psi}(g_{1})\theta_{X^{\ast},L}]([\Pi_{\psi}(g_{2})f])\\
&=\theta_{L,X^{\ast}}([\Pi_{\psi}(g_{1})\theta_{X^{\ast},L}\theta_{L,X^{\ast}}[\Pi_{\psi}(g_{2})\theta_{X^{\ast},L}(f)]))\\
&=\theta_{L,X^{\ast}}[\Pi_{\psi}(g_{1})\Pi_{\psi}(g_{2})\theta_{X^{\ast},L}(f)]\\
&=\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})\Pi_{\psi}(g_{1}g_{2})f.\end{split}

(6.14)

6.7.
Non-splitting of
\overline{\Gamma^{\pm}(2)}
II

Let us consider
g\in\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}(L)
. Under the basis
\{e_{1},e_{1}^{\ast}\}
of
\mathcal{L}
,
\operatorname{Sp}^{\pm}(L)\simeq\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{Z})
. Let
w=x+x^{\ast}\in W
.

Case 1:
g=u(b)\in\Gamma^{\pm}(2)
, and
2\mid b
.

\begin{split}\Pi_{\psi}(g)f(\epsilon,w)&=\sum_{l\in L/L\cap X^{\ast}}\Pi_{\psi}(g)(f^{\prime})(\epsilon,w+l)\psi(\epsilon\tfrac{\langle l,w\rangle}{2}+\tfrac{\langle x_{l},x^{\ast}_{l}\rangle}{2})\\
&=\sum_{l\in L\cap X}\Pi_{\psi}(g)(f^{\prime})(\epsilon,w+l)\psi(\epsilon\tfrac{\langle l,w\rangle}{2})\\
&=\sum_{l\in L\cap X}\Pi_{\psi}(g)(f^{\prime})([1,(\epsilon x^{\ast},\epsilon\tfrac{\langle x+l,x^{\ast}\rangle}{2})][\epsilon,x+l])\psi(\epsilon\tfrac{\langle l,w\rangle}{2})\\
&=\sum_{l\in L\cap X}\Pi_{\psi}(g)(f^{\prime})(\epsilon,x+l)\psi(\epsilon\langle l,w\rangle)\psi(\epsilon\tfrac{\langle x,x^{\ast}\rangle}{2})\\
&=\sum_{l\in L\cap X}\psi(\tfrac{1}{2}\langle(x+l),\epsilon(x+l)b\rangle)f^{\prime}(\epsilon,x+l)\psi(\epsilon\langle l,w\rangle)\psi(\epsilon\tfrac{\langle x,x^{\ast}\rangle}{2})\\
&=\sum_{l\in L\cap X}\int_{X^{\ast}/X^{\ast}\cap L}\psi(\tfrac{1}{2}\langle(x+l),\epsilon(x+l)b\rangle)\psi(\epsilon\tfrac{\langle\dot{y}^{\ast},x+l\rangle}{2})f(\epsilon,x+l+\epsilon\dot{y}^{\ast})\psi(\epsilon\langle l,w\rangle)\psi(\epsilon\tfrac{\langle x,x^{\ast}\rangle}{2})d\dot{y}^{\ast}\\
&=\sum_{l\in L\cap X}\int_{X^{\ast}/X^{\ast}\cap L}\psi(\epsilon\tfrac{\langle\dot{y}^{\ast},x+l\rangle}{2})f(\epsilon,x+l+\epsilon\dot{y}^{\ast})\psi(\epsilon\langle l,x^{\ast}+xb\rangle)\psi(\epsilon\tfrac{\langle x,x^{\ast}+\epsilon xb\rangle}{2})d\dot{y}^{\ast}\\
&=f([\epsilon,w]g)=\nu(\epsilon,g)f([\epsilon,w]g).\end{split}

Case 2:
g=h(a)\in\Gamma^{\pm}(2)
,
a\in\{\pm 1\}
.

\begin{split}\Pi_{\psi}(g)f(\epsilon,w)&=\sum_{l\in L\cap X}\Pi_{\psi}(g)(f^{\prime})(\epsilon,x+l)\psi(\epsilon\langle l,w\rangle)\psi(\epsilon\tfrac{\langle x,x^{\ast}\rangle}{2})\\
&=\sum_{l\in L\cap X}|a|^{1/2}(a,\epsilon)_{F}f^{\prime}(\epsilon,(x+l)a)\psi(\epsilon\langle l,w\rangle)\psi(\epsilon\tfrac{\langle x,x^{\ast}\rangle}{2})\\
&=\sum_{l\in L\cap X}\int_{X^{\ast}/X^{\ast}\cap L}\psi(\epsilon\tfrac{\langle\dot{y}^{\ast},(x+l)a\rangle}{2})f(\epsilon,xa+la+\epsilon\dot{y}^{\ast})(a,\epsilon)_{F}\psi(\epsilon\langle l,w\rangle)\psi(\epsilon\tfrac{\langle x,x^{\ast}\rangle}{2})d\dot{y}^{\ast}\\
&=\sum_{l\in L\cap X}\int_{X^{\ast}/X^{\ast}\cap L}\psi(\epsilon\tfrac{\langle\dot{y}^{\ast},(x+l)a\rangle}{2})f(\epsilon,xa+la+\epsilon\dot{y}^{\ast})(a,\epsilon)_{F}\psi(\epsilon\langle la,wa\rangle)\psi(\epsilon\tfrac{\langle xa,x^{\ast}a\rangle}{2})d\dot{y}^{\ast}\\
&=(a,\epsilon)_{F}f(\epsilon,wa)=(a,\epsilon)_{F}f([\epsilon,w]g)=\nu(\epsilon,g)f([\epsilon,w]g).\end{split}

(6.15)

Case 3:
g=h_{-1}\in\Gamma^{\pm}(2)
.

\begin{split}\Pi_{\psi}(g)f(\epsilon,w)&=\sum_{l\in L\cap X}\Pi_{\psi}(g)(f^{\prime})(\epsilon,x+l)\psi(\epsilon\langle l,w\rangle)\psi(\epsilon\tfrac{\langle x,x^{\ast}\rangle}{2})\\
&=\sum_{l\in L\cap X}f^{\prime}(-\epsilon,x+l)\psi(\epsilon\langle l,w\rangle)\psi(\epsilon\tfrac{\langle x,x^{\ast}\rangle}{2})\\
&=\sum_{l\in L\cap X}\int_{X^{\ast}/X^{\ast}\cap L}\psi(-\epsilon\tfrac{\langle\dot{y}^{\ast},x+l\rangle}{2})f(-\epsilon,x+l-\epsilon\dot{y}^{\ast})\psi(\epsilon\langle l,w\rangle)\psi(\epsilon\tfrac{\langle x,x^{\ast}\rangle}{2})d\dot{y}^{\ast}\\
&=\sum_{l\in L\cap X}\int_{X^{\ast}/X^{\ast}\cap L}\psi(-\epsilon\tfrac{\langle\dot{y}^{\ast},x+l\rangle}{2})f(-\epsilon,x+l-\epsilon\dot{y}^{\ast})\psi(-\epsilon\langle l,-x^{\ast}\rangle)\psi(-\epsilon\tfrac{\langle x,-x^{\ast}\rangle}{2})d\dot{y}^{\ast}\\
&=f(-\epsilon,x-x^{\ast})\\
&=f([\epsilon,w]g).\end{split}

(6.16)

Case 4:
g=\omega\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

\begin{split}&\Pi_{\psi}(g)f([\epsilon,w])\\
&=\sum_{l\in L\cap X}\pi_{\psi}(g)(f^{\prime})(\epsilon,x+l)\psi(\epsilon\langle l,w\rangle)\psi(\epsilon\tfrac{\langle x,x^{\ast}\rangle}{2})\\
&=\sum_{l\in L\cap X}\int_{X^{\ast}}\nu(\epsilon,g)f^{\prime}(\epsilon,y^{\ast}\omega^{-1})\psi(\langle\epsilon(x+l),y^{\ast}\rangle)\psi(\epsilon\langle l,w\rangle)\psi(\epsilon\tfrac{\langle x,x^{\ast}\rangle}{2})dy^{\ast}\\
&=\sum_{l\in L\cap X}\int_{X^{\ast}}\nu(\epsilon,g)f^{\prime}(\epsilon,y^{\ast}\omega^{-1})\psi(\epsilon\langle x,y^{\ast}\rangle)\psi(\epsilon\langle l,x^{\ast}+y^{\ast}\rangle)\psi(\epsilon\tfrac{\langle x,x^{\ast}\rangle}{2})dy^{\ast}\\
&=\sum_{l\in L\cap X}\int_{X^{\ast}}\int_{X^{\ast}/X^{\ast}\cap L}\nu(\epsilon,g)f(\epsilon,y^{\ast}\omega^{-1}+\epsilon\dot{z}^{\ast})\psi(\epsilon\tfrac{\langle\dot{z}^{\ast},y^{\ast}\omega^{-1}\rangle}{2})\psi(\epsilon\langle x,y^{\ast}\rangle)\psi(\epsilon\langle l,x^{\ast}+y^{\ast}\rangle)\psi(\epsilon\tfrac{\langle x,x^{\ast}\rangle}{2})dy^{\ast}dz^{\ast}\\
&=\sum_{l^{\ast}\in L\cap X^{\ast}}\int_{X^{\ast}/X^{\ast}\cap L}\nu(\epsilon,g)f(\epsilon,(-x^{\ast}+l^{\ast})\omega^{-1}+\epsilon\dot{z}^{\ast})\psi(\epsilon\tfrac{\langle\dot{z}^{\ast},(-x^{\ast}+l^{\ast})\omega^{-1}\rangle}{2})\psi(\epsilon\langle x,-x^{\ast}+l^{\ast}\rangle)\psi(\epsilon\tfrac{\langle x,x^{\ast}\rangle}{2})dz^{\ast}\\
&=\sum_{l\in L\cap X}\int_{X^{\ast}/X^{\ast}\cap L}\nu(\epsilon,g)f(\epsilon,-x^{\ast}\omega^{-1}+l+\epsilon\dot{z}^{\ast})\psi(\epsilon\tfrac{\langle\dot{z}^{\ast},-x^{\ast}\omega^{-1}+l\rangle}{2})\psi(\epsilon\langle x,-x^{\ast}+l\omega\rangle)\psi(\epsilon\tfrac{\langle x,x^{\ast}\rangle}{2})dz^{\ast}\\
&=\sum_{l\in L\cap X}\int_{X^{\ast}/X^{\ast}\cap L}\nu(\epsilon,g)f(\epsilon,x^{\ast}\omega+l+\epsilon\dot{z}^{\ast})\psi(\epsilon\tfrac{\langle\dot{z}^{\ast},x^{\ast}\omega+l\rangle}{2})\psi(\epsilon\langle l,x\omega\rangle)\psi(-\epsilon\tfrac{\langle x,x^{\ast}\rangle}{2})dz^{\ast}\\
&=\sum_{l\in L\cap X}\int_{X^{\ast}/X^{\ast}\cap L}\nu(\epsilon,g)f(\epsilon,x^{\ast}\omega+l+\epsilon\dot{z}^{\ast})\psi(\epsilon\tfrac{\langle\dot{z}^{\ast},x^{\ast}\omega+l\rangle}{2})\psi(\epsilon\langle l,x\omega\rangle)\psi(\epsilon\tfrac{\langle x^{\ast}\omega,x\omega\rangle}{2})dz^{\ast}\\
&=\nu(\epsilon,g)f([\epsilon,wg])=\nu(\epsilon,g)f([\epsilon,w]g).\end{split}

(6.17)

Case 5:
g=u_{-}(c)=\omega^{-1}u(-c)\omega\in\Gamma^{\pm}(2)
with
2\mid c
. Then:

\begin{split}\Pi_{\psi}(g)f(\epsilon,w)&=\Pi_{\psi}(\omega^{-1}u(-c)\omega)f(\epsilon,w)\\
&=\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}(\omega^{-1},u(-c))^{-1}\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}(\omega^{-1}u(-c),\omega)^{-1}\Pi_{\psi}(\omega^{-1})\Pi_{\psi}(u(-c))\Pi_{\psi}(\omega)f(\epsilon,w)\\
&=\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}(\omega^{-1}u(-c),\omega)^{-1}\Pi_{\psi}(\omega^{-1})\Pi_{\psi}(u(-c))\Pi_{\psi}(\omega)f(\epsilon,w)\\
&=\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}(\omega^{-1}u(-c),\omega)^{-1}\nu(\epsilon,\omega^{-1})\nu(\epsilon,\omega)f([\epsilon,wg])\\
&=\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(\omega^{-1}u(-c),\omega)^{-1}\nu(\epsilon,\omega^{-1})\nu(\epsilon,\omega)f([\epsilon,wg])\\
&=e^{\tfrac{\pi i\operatorname{sgn}(c)}{4}}f([\epsilon,wg]).\end{split}

(6.18)

Write
f(1,w)=f_{1}(w)
and
f(-1,w)=f_{2}(w)
. Let us denote
\breve{\Gamma}(2)^{\pm}=\langle\omega\rangle\ltimes\Gamma(2)^{\pm}
. Recall the results from Section
7.5
. Let
g\in\breve{\Gamma}(2)
. Then:

6.7.1.

•

\Pi_{\psi}(g)f_{1}(w)=\pi_{\psi}(g)f_{1}(w)=\widetilde{\beta}(g)^{-1}f_{1}(wg)
.

•

\Pi_{\psi}(h_{-1})f_{1}(w)=\Pi_{\psi}(h_{-1})f(1,w)=f(-1,wh_{-1})=f_{2}(wh_{-1})
.

6.7.2.

•

\Pi_{\psi}(h_{-1})f_{2}(w)=\Pi_{\psi}(h_{-1})f(-1,w)=f(1,wh_{-1})=f_{1}(wh_{-1})
.

•

\displaystyle\Pi_{\psi}(g)f_{2}(w)

\displaystyle=\Pi_{\psi}(g)\Pi_{\psi}(h_{-1})f_{1}(wh_{-1})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}(g,h_{-1})\Pi_{\psi}(gh_{-1})f_{1}(wh_{-1})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}(g,h_{-1})\Pi_{\psi}(h_{-1}g^{s(-1)})f_{1}(wh_{-1})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}(g,h_{-1})\widetilde{C}(h_{-1},g^{s(-1)})^{-1}\Pi_{\psi}(h_{-1})\Pi_{\psi}(g^{s(-1)})f_{1}(wh_{-1})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}(g,h_{-1})\widetilde{\beta}(g^{s(-1)})^{-1}f_{2}(wg^{s(-1)}).

6.7.3.

If
\det g=1
, write
h_{\epsilon}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&\epsilon\end{pmatrix}
, then

\displaystyle\Pi_{\psi}(g)f([\epsilon,w])

\displaystyle=\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}(g,h_{\epsilon})\widetilde{\beta}(g^{s(\epsilon)})^{-1}f([\epsilon,wg^{s(\epsilon)}]).

6.7.4.

If
\det g=-1
, let us write
g=h_{-1}(h_{-1}g)
. Then:

\displaystyle\Pi_{\psi}(g)f([\epsilon,w])

\displaystyle=\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}(h_{-1},h_{-1}g)^{-1}\Pi_{\psi}(h_{-1})[\Pi_{\psi}(h_{-1}g)f]([\epsilon,w])

\displaystyle=[\Pi_{\psi}(h_{-1}g)f]([-\epsilon,wh_{-1}])

\displaystyle=\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}(h_{-1}g,h_{-\epsilon})\widetilde{\beta}(h_{-1}g^{s(-\epsilon)})^{-1}f([-\epsilon,wg^{s(-\epsilon)}])

\displaystyle=\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}(g,h_{-\epsilon})\widetilde{\beta}(h_{-1}g^{s(-\epsilon)})^{-1}f([-\epsilon,wg^{s(-\epsilon)}]).

6.7.5.

Let
g=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\breve{\Gamma}(2)^{\pm}
.

If
c=0
, then

\displaystyle\Pi_{\psi}(g)f([\epsilon,w])=((\det g)\epsilon,a)_{\mathbb{R}}f([(\det g)\epsilon,wg^{s((\det g)\epsilon)}]).

If
c\neq 0
and
\det g=1
, then

\displaystyle\Pi_{\psi}(g)f([\epsilon,w])=e^{\tfrac{\pi i(1-\epsilon)}{4}}(c,\epsilon)_{\mathbb{R}}\beta(d,\epsilon c)^{-1}_{\mathbb{R}}f([\epsilon,wg^{s(\epsilon)}]).

If
c\neq 0
and
\det g=-1
, then
h_{-1}g^{s(-\epsilon)}=\begin{pmatrix}a&-\epsilon b\\
-\epsilon c&d\end{pmatrix}
, and

\displaystyle\Pi_{\psi}(g)f([\epsilon,w])

\displaystyle=\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}(g,h_{-\epsilon})\widetilde{\beta}(h_{-1}g^{s(-\epsilon)})^{-1}f([-\epsilon,wg^{s(-\epsilon)}])

\displaystyle=e^{\tfrac{\pi i(1-\epsilon)}{4}}(-c,-\epsilon)_{\mathbb{R}}\beta(d,-\epsilon c)^{-1}f([-\epsilon,wg^{s(-\epsilon)}]).

We conclude:

\displaystyle\Pi_{\psi}(g)f([\epsilon,w])=\Upsilon(g)f([(\det g)\epsilon,wg^{s((\det g)\epsilon)}]),

(6.19)

for the constant

\Upsilon(g)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}((\det g)\epsilon,a)_{\mathbb{R}}&\textrm{ if }c=0,\\
e^{\tfrac{\pi i(1-\epsilon)}{4}}(c\det g,\epsilon\det g)_{\mathbb{R}}\beta(d,(\det g)\epsilon c)^{-1}&\textrm{ if }c\neq 0.\end{array}\right.

6.8.
Lattice

(cf.
[
De
]
,
[
DeSe
]
)
Let
w_{1}=ae_{1}+be_{1}^{\ast}
,
w_{2}=ce_{1}+de_{1}^{\ast}
be two elements of
W
. Then
\mathbb{Z}w_{1}+\mathbb{Z}w_{2}
forms a lattice in
W
iff
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
are
\mathbb{R}
-linear independence iff
\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
. Moreover, it is a
\psi
-self dual lattice iff
\langle w_{1},w_{2}\rangle=1
or
-1
iff
\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})
. Let
\mathcal{L}_{\psi}
denote the set of all
\psi
-self dual lattices in
W
. We can define a right
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})
-action on
\mathcal{L}_{\psi}
as follows:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathcal{L}_{\psi}\times\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})&\longrightarrow&\mathcal{L}_{\psi};\\
(L=\mathbb{Z}w_{1}\oplus\mathbb{Z}w_{2},g)&\longmapsto&Lg=\mathbb{Z}(w_{1}g)\oplus\mathbb{Z}(w_{2}g).\end{array}

Let
L_{0}=\mathbb{Z}e_{1}\oplus\mathbb{Z}e_{1}^{\ast}\in\mathcal{L}_{\psi}
. Then:

•

\mathcal{L}_{\psi}=\{L_{0}g\mid g\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})\}
.

•

L_{0}g=L_{0}
iff
g\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{Z})
.

•

There exists a bijection:
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{Z})\setminus\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}_{\psi};g\longmapsto L_{0}g
.

6.9.
Lagrangian Grassmanian

By Section
5.8
, we can identity
\mathbb{C}
with
W
by
a+ib\longrightarrow ae_{1}+be_{1}^{\ast}
, and define
\langle z,z^{\prime}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}=i\operatorname{Im}(z\overline{z}^{\prime})
. A Lagrangian plane of
(\mathbb{C},\langle,\rangle_{\mathbb{C}})
is a set
\mathbb{R}z
, for some
z\in\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
. Let
\Lambda
denote the set of all Lagrangians in
\mathbb{C}
. Then there exists a
left
\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
-action on
\Lambda
given as follows:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathbb{C}^{\ast}\times\Lambda&\longrightarrow&\Lambda;\\
(t,\mathbb{R}z)&\longmapsto&t(\mathbb{R}z)=\mathbb{R}t(z).\end{array}

Then
\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
acts transitively on
\Lambda
, and there exists a bijective map:

\mathbb{C}^{\ast}/\mathbb{R}^{\ast}\longrightarrow\Lambda;z\longmapsto\mathbb{R}z.

Note that
\mathbb{C}^{\ast}/\mathbb{R}^{\ast}\simeq\operatorname{U}(\mathbb{C})/\mu_{2}
.

6.10.
Modular functions

Let us consider the left action of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})
on
\mathcal{H}^{\pm}
by
gz=\tfrac{az+b}{cz+d}
, for
g\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})
,
z\in\mathcal{H}^{\pm}
. This action is transitive and the stabilizer of
i
is just the group
\operatorname{SO}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
. Then there exists a bijective map:

i:\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})/\operatorname{SO}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}^{\pm};[g]\longmapsto gi.

Note that
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})=P_{>0}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})\operatorname{SO}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
,
P_{>0}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})\cap\operatorname{SO}_{2}(\mathbb{R})=I
.
So there exists a bijective map:

\displaystyle\overline{i}:P^{\pm}_{>0}(\mathbb{R})\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}^{\pm};g=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
0&(\det g)a^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\longmapsto ab\det g+ia^{2}\det g.

(6.20)

For
g=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{SL}^{\pm}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, let us write
g=p_{g}k_{g}
, for
p_{g}\in P^{\pm}_{>0}(\mathbb{R})
and
k_{g}\in\operatorname{SO}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
. Then:

Lemma 6.10
.

p_{g}=\begin{pmatrix}\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{c^{2}+d^{2}}}&\det g\tfrac{bd+ac}{\sqrt{c^{2}+d^{2}}}\\
0&\det g\sqrt{c^{2}+d^{2}}\end{pmatrix}
,
k_{g}=\det g\begin{pmatrix}\tfrac{d}{\sqrt{c^{2}+d^{2}}}&-\tfrac{c}{\sqrt{c^{2}+d^{2}}}\\
\tfrac{c}{\sqrt{c^{2}+d^{2}}}&\tfrac{d}{\sqrt{c^{2}+d^{2}}}\end{pmatrix}
.

Proof.

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{c^{2}+d^{2}}}\tfrac{d\det g}{\sqrt{c^{2}+d^{2}}}+(\tfrac{bd+ac}{\sqrt{c^{2}+d^{2}}})\tfrac{c}{\sqrt{c^{2}+d^{2}}}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{d\det g}{\sqrt{c^{2}+d^{2}}}+\tfrac{cbd+ac^{2}}{c^{2}+d^{2}}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{dad-dbc+cbd+ac^{2}}{c^{2}+d^{2}}=a;

\displaystyle(\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{c^{2}+d^{2}}})\tfrac{-c\det g}{\sqrt{c^{2}+d^{2}}}+(\tfrac{bd+ac}{\sqrt{c^{2}+d^{2}}})\tfrac{d}{\sqrt{c^{2}+d^{2}}}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{-c\det g}{\sqrt{c^{2}+d^{2}}}-\tfrac{bd^{2}+acd}{c^{2}+d^{2}}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{-cad+bc^{2}+bd^{2}+acd}{c^{2}+d^{2}}=b.

∎

Recall the functions
\widetilde{s}(k_{g})
,
\overline{s}(k_{g})
from Lemmas
6.5
,
6.6
. Let us extend them to the group
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})
as follows:

\displaystyle\widetilde{s}(g)=\widetilde{s}(k_{g})\quad\textrm{ and }\quad\overline{s}(g)=\overline{s}(k_{g}).

(6.21)

Lemma 6.11
.

m_{X^{\ast}}(g)=m_{X^{\ast}}(p_{g})m_{X^{\ast}}(k_{g})
, for
g=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})
.

Proof.

1) If
c=0
,
\det g=1
, then
m_{X^{\ast}}(g)=e^{\tfrac{i\pi}{4}[1-\operatorname{sgn}(d)]}
,
m_{X^{\ast}}(p_{g})=1
,
m_{X^{\ast}}(k_{g})=e^{\tfrac{i\pi}{4}[1-\operatorname{sgn}(\tfrac{d}{\sqrt{c^{2}+d^{2}}})]}
.

2) If
c=0
,
\det g=-1
, then
m_{X^{\ast}}(g)=e^{\tfrac{i\pi}{4}[1+\operatorname{sgn}(d)]}
,
m_{X^{\ast}}(p_{g})=1
,
m_{X^{\ast}}(k_{g})=e^{\tfrac{i\pi}{4}[1+\operatorname{sgn}(\tfrac{d}{\sqrt{c^{2}+d^{2}}})]}
.

3) If
c\neq 0
,
\det g=1
, then
m_{X^{\ast}}(g)=e^{\tfrac{i\pi}{4}[-\operatorname{sgn}(c)]}
,
m_{X^{\ast}}(p_{g})=1
,
m_{X^{\ast}}(k_{g})=e^{\tfrac{i\pi}{4}[-\tfrac{c}{\sqrt{c^{2}+d^{2}}}]}
.

4) If
c\neq 0
,
\det g=-1
, then
m_{X^{\ast}}(g)=e^{\tfrac{i\pi}{4}[\operatorname{sgn}(c)]}
,
m_{X^{\ast}}(p_{g})=1
,
m_{X^{\ast}}(k_{g})=e^{\tfrac{i\pi}{4}[\tfrac{c}{\sqrt{c^{2}+d^{2}}}]}
.
∎

As a consequence, we have:

\overline{s}(g)=\overline{s}(p_{g})\overline{s}(k_{g}),\quad\quad\widetilde{s}(g)=\widetilde{s}(p_{g})\widetilde{s}(k_{g}),\quad\textrm{ and }\quad\widetilde{s}(g)=\overline{s}(g)m_{X^{\ast}}(g).

Let us modify the above
\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}
and
\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}
as follows:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\widetilde{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})=\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})\widetilde{s}(g_{1})\widetilde{s}(g_{2})\widetilde{s}(g_{1}g_{2})^{-1},

(6.22)

\displaystyle\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})=\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})\overline{s}(g_{1})\overline{s}(g_{2})\overline{s}(g_{1}g_{2})^{-1}.

(6.23)

Lemma 6.12
.

(1)

\widetilde{\widetilde{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})=\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})
, for
g_{i}\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})
.

(2)

\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(p,g)=1
, for
g\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})
,
p\in P^{\pm}_{>0}(\mathbb{R})
.

(3)

\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(g,k)=1
, for
g\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})
,
k\in\operatorname{SO}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
.

Proof.

1)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\widetilde{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})\widetilde{s}(g_{1})\widetilde{s}(g_{2})\widetilde{s}(g_{1}g_{2})^{-1}

\displaystyle=\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})m_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1}g_{2})m_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1})^{-1}m_{X^{\ast}}(g_{2})^{-1}\widetilde{s}(g_{1})\widetilde{s}(g_{2})\widetilde{s}(g_{1}g_{2})^{-1}

\displaystyle=\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})\overline{s}(g_{1})\overline{s}(g_{2})\overline{s}(g_{1}g_{2})^{-1}

\displaystyle=\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2}).

2)

\overline{s}(pg)=\overline{s}(pp_{g}k_{g})=\overline{s}(pp_{g})\overline{s}(k_{g})=\overline{s}(p)\overline{s}(p_{g})\overline{s}(k_{g})=\overline{s}(p)\overline{s}(g);

\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}(p,g)=1;

\displaystyle\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(p,g)

\displaystyle=\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}(p,g)\overline{s}(p)\overline{s}(g)\overline{s}(pg)^{-1}=1.

3) Let
k_{1},k_{2}\in\operatorname{SO}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
.

\displaystyle\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(p_{g},k_{g})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}(p_{g},k_{g})\widetilde{s}(p_{g})\widetilde{s}(k_{g})\widetilde{s}(g)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{s}(p_{g})\widetilde{s}(k_{g})\widetilde{s}(g)^{-1}=1;

\displaystyle\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(k_{1},k_{2})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}(k_{1},k_{2})\widetilde{s}(k_{1})\widetilde{s}(k_{2})\widetilde{s}(k_{1}k_{2})^{-1}=1;

\displaystyle\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(g,k)=\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(p_{g}k_{g},k)

\displaystyle=\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(p_{g},k_{g})^{-1}\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(p_{g},k_{g}k)\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(k_{g},k)=1.

∎

For
z\in\mathcal{H}^{\pm}
, let us write
p_{z}
for the corresponding element in
P_{>0}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})
via the map
\overline{i}
.

Definition 6.13
.

For
g\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R}),z\in\mathcal{H}^{\pm}
, we define:

\overline{j}(g,z)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Delta}}{{=}}\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(g,p_{z})
.

Lemma 6.14
.

\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})=\overline{j}(g_{1},g_{2}z)\overline{j}(g_{2},z)\overline{j}(g_{1}g_{2},z)^{-1}
, for
g_{1},g_{2}\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})
,
z\in\mathcal{H}^{\pm}
.

Proof.

\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1}g_{2},p_{z})=\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2}p_{z})\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{2},p_{z})
. Then:

\displaystyle\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})

\displaystyle=\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2}p_{z})\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{2},p_{z})\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1}g_{2},p_{z})^{-1}

\displaystyle=\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},p_{g_{2}p_{z}}k_{g_{2}p_{z}})\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{2},p_{z})\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1}g_{2},p_{z})^{-1}

\displaystyle=\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},p_{g_{2}p_{z}}k_{g_{2}p_{z}})\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(p_{g_{2}p_{z}},k_{g_{2}p_{z}})\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{2},p_{z})\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1}g_{2},p_{z})^{-1}

\displaystyle=\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},p_{g_{2}p_{z}})\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1}p_{g_{2}p_{z}},k_{g_{2}p_{z}})\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{2},p_{z})\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1}g_{2},p_{z})^{-1}

\displaystyle=\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},p_{g_{2}p_{z}})\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{2},p_{z})\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1}g_{2},p_{z})^{-1}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{j}(g_{1},g_{2}z)\widetilde{j}(g_{2},z)\widetilde{j}(g_{1}g_{2},z)^{-1}.

∎

6.11.
Holomorphic function

Form Lemma
6.10
, we can see that
p_{g}
is a
C^{\infty}
-function from
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})
to
P_{>0}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})
. Moreover,
\overline{i}
is a
C^{\infty}
-homeomorphism.
Let

\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{R})=\{\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{bmatrix}\in M_{2}(\mathbb{R})\mid a+d=0\}

denote the real Lie algebra of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
. It is known that there exist the left and right regular
\operatorname{SL}^{\pm}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
-actions on
C^{\infty}(\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R}),\mathbb{R})
. These actions can extend to the lie algebra in the following way:

[l_{Z}f](g)=\tfrac{d}{dt}[f(e^{-tZ}g)]|_{t=0},\quad\quad Z\in\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{R}),

[r_{Z}f](g)=\tfrac{d}{dt}[f(ge^{tZ})]|_{t=0},\quad\quad Z\in\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{R}).

If
f
is a real-
C^{\infty}
complex function, we can extend this action to the complex Lie algebra by linearity. Following
[
LiVe
, pp.181-182]
, let

h=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{bmatrix},e^{+}=\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{bmatrix},e^{-}=\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
1&0\end{bmatrix},J^{+}=\tfrac{1}{2}\begin{bmatrix}-i&1\\
1&i\end{bmatrix},J^{-}=\tfrac{1}{2}\begin{bmatrix}i&1\\
1&-i\end{bmatrix},J^{0}=\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{bmatrix}.

(6.24)

The following two results come from
[
LiVe
, p.182, Section 2.3.8,Lmm]
for
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
. We repeat the proofs for
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})
without too much change.

Lemma 6.15
.

For a real-
C^{\infty}
complex function
f
on
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})
,
f
is right
\operatorname{SO}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
-invariant iff
r_{J^{0}}f=0
.

Proof.

Let us write
f=f_{1}+if_{2}
.

(\Rightarrow)
For
k=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta&\sin\theta\\
-\sin\theta&\cos\theta\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{SO}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
,
f(gk)=f(g)
. Write

J^{0}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}=c^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}-i&0\\
0&i\end{pmatrix}c,\qquad c=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{bmatrix}1&i\\
i&1\end{bmatrix}.

Then

e^{t\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}}=c^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}e^{-it}&0\\
0&e^{it}\end{bmatrix}c=\begin{pmatrix}\cos t&\sin t\\
-\sin t&\cos t\end{pmatrix}.

Then
[f_{i}(ge^{t\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}})]=f_{i}(g)
. Hence
r_{J^{0}}f=0
.

(\Leftarrow)
Let us write
g_{t}=\begin{pmatrix}\cos t&\sin t\\
-\sin t&\cos t\end{pmatrix}
. If
r_{J^{0}}f=0
, then
r_{J^{0}}l_{g_{t}}f=l_{g_{t}}r_{J^{0}}f=0
. So
f_{i}(gg_{t})=f_{i}(g)
, for any
t
. So
f
is right
\operatorname{SO}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
-invariant.
∎

For any function
f
on
\mathcal{H}^{\pm}
, let us define

[I(f)](g)=f(gi).

Lemma 6.16
.

For a complex valued real-
C^{\infty}
-function
f
on
\mathcal{H}^{\pm}
,
f
is holomorphic iff
r_{J^{-}}[I(f)]=0
.

Proof.

Let us write:

J^{-}=\begin{bmatrix}0&\tfrac{1}{2}\\
\tfrac{1}{2}&0\end{bmatrix}+i\begin{bmatrix}\tfrac{1}{2}&0\\
0&-\tfrac{1}{2}\end{bmatrix},\quad\quad f(x+iy)=u(x,y)+iv(x,y).

Then:

\displaystyle e^{\begin{bmatrix}0&\tfrac{t}{2}\\
\tfrac{t}{2}&0\end{bmatrix}}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}+\begin{bmatrix}0&\tfrac{t}{2}\\
\tfrac{t}{2}&0\end{bmatrix}+o(t^{2}),

\displaystyle e^{\begin{bmatrix}t/2&0\\
0&-t/2\end{bmatrix}}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}+\begin{bmatrix}t/2&0\\
0&-t/2\end{bmatrix}+o(t^{2});

\displaystyle r_{J^{-}}[I(f)](\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{bmatrix})

\displaystyle=\tfrac{d}{dt}f(e^{\begin{bmatrix}0&\tfrac{t}{2}\\
\tfrac{t}{2}&0\end{bmatrix}}i)|_{t=0}+i\tfrac{d}{dt}f(e^{\begin{bmatrix}t/2&0\\
0&-t/2\end{bmatrix}}i)|_{t=0}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{d}{dt}f(\begin{bmatrix}1&\tfrac{t}{2}\\
\tfrac{t}{2}&1\end{bmatrix}i)|_{t=0}+i\tfrac{d}{dt}f(\begin{bmatrix}1+t/2&0\\
0&1-t/2\end{bmatrix}i)|_{t=0}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{d}{dt}[u(\tfrac{t}{1-t^{2}/4},1)+iv(\tfrac{t}{1-t^{2}/4},1)]|_{t=0}+i\tfrac{d}{dt}[u(0,\tfrac{1+t/2}{1-t/2})+iv(0,\tfrac{1+t/2}{1-t/2})]|_{t=0}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{\partial u}{\partial x}(0,1)+i\tfrac{\partial v}{\partial x}(0,1)+i\tfrac{\partial u}{\partial y}(0,1)-i\tfrac{\partial v}{\partial y}(0,1)

\displaystyle=(\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x}+i\tfrac{\partial}{\partial y})f(i)

\displaystyle=2\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\overline{z}}f(i);

\displaystyle r_{J^{-}}[I(f)](g)

\displaystyle=r_{J^{-}}[g^{-1}I(f)](\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{bmatrix})

\displaystyle=g^{-1}r_{J^{-}}[I(f)](\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{bmatrix})

\displaystyle=2\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\overline{z}}f(gi).

∎

6.12.
\widehat{\operatorname{SL}^{\pm}_{2}}(\mathbb{R})

Let
\widehat{\operatorname{SL}^{\pm}_{2}}(\mathbb{R})
denote the covering group over
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})
associated to the above
2
-cocycle
\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}
. Then there exists an exact sequence:

1\longrightarrow T\longrightarrow\widehat{\operatorname{SL}^{\pm}_{2}}(\mathbb{R})\longrightarrow\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})\to 1.

Let
g_{i}=\begin{pmatrix}a_{i}&b_{i}\\
c_{i}&d_{i}\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})
. Then:

\begin{split}\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(p_{g_{1}}k_{g_{1}},p_{g_{2}}k_{g_{2}})&=\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(p_{g_{1}}k_{g_{1}},p_{g_{2}}k_{g_{2}})\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(p_{g_{2}},k_{g_{2}})\\
&=\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(p_{g_{1}}k_{g_{1}},p_{g_{2}})\\
&=\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(p_{g_{1}},k_{g_{1}})\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(p_{g_{1}}k_{g_{1}},p_{g_{2}})\\
&=\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(p_{g_{1}},k_{g_{1}}p_{g_{2}})\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(k_{g_{1}},p_{g_{2}})\\
&=\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(k_{g_{1}},p_{g_{2}})\\
&=\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}(k_{g_{1}},p_{g_{2}})\overline{s}(k_{g_{1}})\overline{s}(p_{g_{2}})\overline{s}(k_{g_{1}}p_{g_{2}})^{-1}.\end{split}

(6.25)

By Lemma
6.10
, the maps
g\to k_{g}
,
g\to p_{g}
both are real
C^{\infty}
.

If
c\neq 0
,

\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})=\overline{s}(k_{g_{1}})\overline{s}(p_{g_{2}})\overline{s}(k_{g_{1}}p_{g_{2}})^{-1}.

In this case,
k_{g_{1}}p_{g_{2}}=\begin{pmatrix}\ast&\ast\\
c^{\prime}&\ast\end{pmatrix}
with
c^{\prime}\neq 0
. By Lemma
6.6
, we know that
\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})
is
C^{\infty}
on
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
.

If
d\neq 0
,

\displaystyle\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})

\displaystyle=\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(\omega,\omega^{-1}g_{1})^{-1}\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(\omega,\omega^{-1}g_{1}g_{2})\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(\omega^{-1}g_{1},g_{2}).

So it is also
C^{\infty}
on
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
. Hence
\widehat{\operatorname{SL}^{\pm}_{2}}(\mathbb{R})
is a smooth manifold, and
\widehat{\operatorname{SL}^{\pm}_{2}}(\mathbb{R})\longrightarrow\operatorname{SL}^{\pm}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
is a
C^{\infty}
-map.

6.13.
Extended Weil representation of
\widehat{\operatorname{SL}^{\pm}_{2}}(\mathbb{R})

The group
\widehat{\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{\pm}}(\mathbb{R})
is generated by

[u(b)=\begin{pmatrix}1&b\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},1],[h(a)=\begin{pmatrix}a&0\\
0&a^{-1}\end{pmatrix},1],[h_{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix},1],[\omega=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix},1],[1,t],

for
t\in T
,
b\in F
,
a\in F^{\times}
. By (
3.14
)(
6.31
), we have:

\displaystyle\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})\widetilde{s}(g_{1})\widetilde{s}(g_{2})\widetilde{s}(g_{1}g_{2})^{-1}.

1) If
g\in\in P^{\pm}_{>0}(\mathbb{R})
,
\widetilde{s}(g)=1
.

2) If
g=h(a)
with
a<0
,
\widetilde{s}(g)=m_{X^{\ast}}(g)\overline{s}(g)=i\overline{s}(g)=-1
.

3) If
g=\omega
,
\widetilde{s}(g)=-i
.

4) If
g=\omega^{-1}
,
\widetilde{s}(g)=i
.

5) If
g=\omega^{-1}u(b)\omega=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-b&1\end{pmatrix}
, then
\widetilde{s}(g)=m_{X^{\ast}}(g)\overline{s}(g)=e^{\tfrac{\pi i(\operatorname{sgn}b)}{4}}\sqrt{\tfrac{b}{\sqrt{1+b^{2}}}i+\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{1+b^{2}}}}
.

Go back to
(\ref{Cdou})
, and modify the action of
\Pi_{\psi}(g)
on
L^{2}(\mu_{2}\times\mathbb{R})
by
\widetilde{s}(g)
. Let us write

\displaystyle\overline{\overline{\Pi}}_{\psi}(g)=\Pi_{\psi}(g)\widetilde{s}(g).

(6.26)

Then:

\displaystyle\overline{\overline{\Pi}}_{\psi}(g_{1})\overline{\overline{\Pi}}_{\psi}(g_{2})

\displaystyle=\Pi_{\psi}(g_{1})\widetilde{s}(g_{1})\Pi_{\psi}(g_{2})\widetilde{s}(g_{2})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})\Pi_{\psi}(g_{1}g_{2})\widetilde{s}(g_{1})\widetilde{s}(g_{2})

\displaystyle=\overline{\overline{\Pi}}_{\psi}(g_{1}g_{2})\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})\widetilde{s}(g_{1})\widetilde{s}(g_{2})\widetilde{s}(g_{1}g_{2})^{-1}

\displaystyle=\overline{\overline{\Pi}}_{\psi}(g_{1}g_{2})\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2}).

By (
4.2
)(
4.4
)(
4.5
)(
4.6
), the representation
\overline{\overline{\Pi}}_{\psi}
of
\widehat{\operatorname{SL}^{\pm}_{2}}(\mathbb{R})
can be realized on
L^{2}(\mu_{2}\times\mathbb{R})
by the following formulas:

\overline{\overline{\Pi}}_{\psi}[u(b)]f([\epsilon,x])=e^{\pi i\epsilon x^{2}b}f([\epsilon,x]);

(6.27)

\overline{\overline{\Pi}}_{\psi}[h(a)]f([\epsilon,x])=\operatorname{sgn}(a)|a|^{1/2}(a,\epsilon)_{F}f([\epsilon,xa]);

(6.28)

\overline{\overline{\Pi}}_{\psi}[\omega]f([\epsilon,x])=-i\nu(\epsilon,\omega)\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{2\pi i\epsilon xy}f([\epsilon,-y])dy;

(6.29)

\overline{\overline{\Pi}}_{\psi}[h_{-1},t]f([\epsilon,x])=tf([-\epsilon,x]).

(6.30)

6.14.
Modular forms on
\widehat{\operatorname{SL}^{\pm}_{2}}(\mathbb{R})

Recall that a character of
\operatorname{SO}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
has the form:

\chi_{n}:\operatorname{SO}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times};\begin{pmatrix}\cos t&\sin t\\
-\sin t&\cos t\end{pmatrix}\longmapsto e^{-int}=[i\sin(-t)+\cos(-t)]^{n}.

Note that there exists a group embedding map:

\operatorname{SO}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\longrightarrow\widehat{\operatorname{SL}^{\pm}_{2}}(\mathbb{R});k\longmapsto[k,1].

Let
\Gamma
denote a discrete group of
\operatorname{SL}^{\pm}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, and let
\widehat{\Gamma}
be inverse image of it in
\widehat{\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{\pm}}(\mathbb{R})
. Let
\delta_{\Gamma}
denote a character of
\Gamma
. Let
\widehat{\delta_{\Gamma}}
denote its extending representation on
\widehat{\Gamma}
.
By
[
LiVe
, pp.184-188]
, let
\mathcal{M}(\widehat{\operatorname{SL}^{\pm}_{2}}(\mathbb{R}),J^{-},\chi_{n},\widehat{\delta_{\Gamma}})
denote the space of functions
f:\widehat{\operatorname{SL}^{\pm}_{2}}(\mathbb{R})\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
such that

(1)

f
is analytic on
\widehat{\operatorname{SL}^{\pm}_{2}}(\mathbb{R})
,

(2)

f(gk(t))=\chi_{n}(t)f(g)
,

(3)

r_{J^{-}}(f)=0
,

(4)

f(\gamma g)=\widehat{\delta_{\Gamma}}(\gamma)f(g)
,

for
g\in\widehat{\operatorname{SL}^{\pm}_{2}}(\mathbb{R})
, and
k(t)=\begin{pmatrix}\cos t&\sin t\\
-\sin t&\cos t\end{pmatrix}
,
\gamma\in\widehat{\Gamma}
.
Let us extend
\chi_{n}
to a function on
\widehat{\operatorname{SL}^{\pm}_{2}}(\mathbb{R})
from Lemma
6.10
. For
g=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})
, define

\chi_{n}([g,\epsilon])=\epsilon(ci+d)^{n}.

If write
g=p_{g}k_{g}
, then

\chi_{n}([p_{g},\epsilon])=\epsilon(\det g\sqrt{c^{2}+d^{2}})^{n},\quad\quad\chi_{n}([k_{g},1])=(\frac{\det g}{\sqrt{c^{2}+d^{2}}})^{n}(ci+d)^{n},

\chi_{n}([g,\epsilon])=\chi_{n}([p_{g},\epsilon][k_{g},1])=\chi_{n}([p_{g},\epsilon])\chi_{n}([k_{g},1]).

Lemma 6.17
.

For
[g,\epsilon]\in\widehat{\operatorname{SL}^{\pm}_{2}}(\mathbb{R})
,
k(t)=\begin{pmatrix}\cos t&\sin t\\
-\sin t&\cos t\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{SO}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, we have:

\chi_{n}([g,\epsilon][k(t),1])=e^{-int}\chi_{n}([g,\epsilon]).

Proof.

\displaystyle[g,\epsilon][k(t),1]

\displaystyle=[gk(t),\overline{\overline{C}}_{X^{\ast}}(g,k(t))\epsilon]

\displaystyle=[gk(t),\epsilon]

\displaystyle=[p_{g},\epsilon][k_{g},1][k(t),1].

\displaystyle\chi_{n}([g,\epsilon][k(t),1])

\displaystyle=\chi_{n}([gk(t),\epsilon])

\displaystyle=\chi_{n}([p_{g},\epsilon][k_{g}k(t),1])

\displaystyle=\chi_{n}([p_{g},\epsilon])\chi_{n}([k_{g},1])\chi_{n}([k(t),1])

\displaystyle=\chi_{n}([g,\epsilon])\chi_{n}([k(t),1]).

∎

Note that
\chi_{n}
is a
C^{\infty}
-function on
\widehat{\operatorname{SL}^{\pm}_{2}}(\mathbb{R})
. Similarly as
[
LiVe
, p.186, 2.3.14, Lemma]
, we also have:

Lemma 6.18
.

r_{J^{-}}(\chi_{n})=0
.

Proof.

We follow the proof of Lemma 2.3.14 in
[
LiVe
]
. Recall the notations
h,e^{+},e^{-},J^{0},J^{+},J^{-}
from (
6.24
). Let
I=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
. Then:

r_{e}(\chi_{n})(I)=\frac{d}{dt}(\chi_{n}([e^{\begin{bmatrix}0&t\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}},1]))|_{t=0}=\frac{d}{dt}(\chi_{n}(\begin{bmatrix}1&t\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}))|_{t=0}=0;

r_{h}(\chi_{n})(I)=\frac{d}{dt}(\chi_{n}([e^{\begin{bmatrix}t&0\\
0&-t\end{bmatrix}},1]))|_{t=0}=\frac{d}{dt}(e^{-nt})|_{t=0}=-n;

r_{J^{0}}(\chi_{n})(I)=\frac{d}{dt}\chi_{n}([\begin{pmatrix}\cos t&\sin t\\
-\sin t&\cos t\end{pmatrix},1])|_{t=0}=-in.

Note that
J^{-}=\frac{1}{2}(ih+2e^{+}-J^{0})
. Hence
r_{J^{-}}(\chi_{n})(I)=-in+in=0
. For the general
[g,\epsilon]\in\widehat{\operatorname{SL}^{\pm}_{2}}(\mathbb{R})
, we have

\chi_{n}([g,\epsilon])=\chi_{n}([p_{g},\epsilon])\chi_{n}([k_{g},1])=\epsilon(\det g\sqrt{c^{2}+d^{2}})^{n}\chi_{n}([k_{g},1])

∎

Let
f\in\mathcal{M}(\widehat{\operatorname{SL}^{\pm}_{2}}(\mathbb{R}),J^{-},\chi_{n},\widehat{\delta_{\Gamma}})
. We can define a function on
\widehat{\operatorname{SL}^{\pm}_{2}}(\mathbb{R})
twisted by
\chi_{n}
as follows:

f_{n}=f\chi_{n}^{-1}.

Then
f_{n}
is
\operatorname{SO}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
-invariant and it satisfies the above conditions (1)(2)(3)(4). Note that
\widehat{\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{\pm}}(\mathbb{R})=\widehat{P_{>0}^{\pm}}(\mathbb{R})\operatorname{SO}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
,
\widehat{P_{>0}^{\pm}}(\mathbb{R})\cap\operatorname{SO}_{2}(\mathbb{R})=I
.
So there exists a bijective map:

\overline{i}:\widehat{P^{\pm}_{>0}}(\mathbb{R})\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}^{\pm}\times T;[g,t]=[\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
0&(\det g)a^{-1}\end{pmatrix},t]\longmapsto(ab\det g+ia^{2}\det g,t).

For the above
f_{n}
, through
\overline{i}
, it defines a function on
\mathcal{H}^{\pm}\times T
. By Lemma
6.16
, it is holomorphic on
\mathcal{H}^{\pm}
.

6.15.
